M12. True (T) or false (F)? Give a counterexample for F.
     a) Every smooth quintic threefold has h^{2,1} = 101.
     b) A smooth hypersurface of degree 6 in P⁴ is Calabi-Yau.
     c) χ of a CY 3-fold is always even.
     d) Every CY 3-fold has a mirror CY 3-fold.
     e) The mirror quintic has χ = 200.
     f) Every smooth quintic contains exactly 2875 lines.
     g) N_d of the quintic is always an integer.
     h) The instanton numbers n_d are integers.
     i) QH*(Pⁿ) is isomorphic to H*(Pⁿ) ⊗ Q[q] as a ring.
     j) The small quantum product is associative.
     k) On a CY 3-fold, H∘H has only classical terms.
     l) The number of rational plane quartics through 11 points is 620.
     m) Bott's formula needs isolated fixed points.
     n) χ(M) equals the number of torus-fixed points (isolated case).
     o) ∑(5n)!/(n!)⁵zⁿ converges for |z| < 1/3125.
     p) The monodromy of the quintic mirror at z = 0 has finite order.
     q) T-duality of a circle sends radius R to 1/R.
     r) χ(F, G) = χ(G, F) on an elliptic curve.
     s) dim Hom(F, G) = χ(F, G) for all coherent sheaves F, G on E.
     t) Every smooth complete toric surface with r rays has K² = 12 − r.
     u) Every lattice polygon with exactly one interior point is
        reflexive.
     v) Batyrev mirrors satisfy h^{1,1}(Z_Δ) = h^{2,1}(Z_{Δ°}) in dim 3.
     Answers:
       a) T (Lefschetz + χ = −200)
       b) F: K_X = 𝒪(1)|_X (general type)
       c) T (2(h^{1,1} − h^{2,1}))
       d) F: a rigid CY (h^{2,1} = 0) has no Kähler mirror
       e) T
       f) F: the Fermat quintic has infinitely many lines
       g) F: N₂ = 4876875/8
       h) T (Gopakumar-Vafa integrality, proved by Ionel-Parker)
       i) F: H^{n+1} = q ≠ 0 in QH, while H^{n+1} = 0 classically
       j) T (WDVV)
       k) F: H∘H = (Y(q)/5)H², Y = 5 + 2875q + ...
       l) T      m) F: it works with fixed components (Atiyah-Bott)
       n) T      o) T       p) F: it is maximally unipotent, infinite
       q) T (in string units α′ = 1)
       r) F: χ(𝒪, 𝒪(p)) = 1, χ(𝒪(p), 𝒪) = −1
       s) F: χ(𝒪, 𝒪) = 0 but Hom(𝒪, 𝒪) = C
       t) T (Noether: K² + χ = 12)
       u) T (in dimension 2 only: one interior point ⇔ reflexive)
       v) T

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: keep one table on the wall — the Hodge diamond of a CY
  3-fold with h^{1,1} and h^{2,1} boxed, the quintic's numbers (1, 101,
  −200) and its mirror's (101, 1, 200) — and insist that every new
  example be placed in it by the same two steps: a Chern class
  expansion c(TP)/c(N) for χ, and a Lefschetz or monomial count for the
  other number. For enumerative problems always write the dimension
  count first (2n − 3 − d for lines, 3d − 1 points for plane curves,
  5d + 1 for M̄_{0,0}(P⁴, d)); most wrong answers come from counting the
  wrong number of conditions. Do Schubert calculus with Chern roots and
  the four G(2,4) numbers (σ₁⁴ = 2, σ₁₁² = σ₂² = σ₁²σ₁₁ = 1), and check
  every localization with ∫_{Pⁿ}Hⁿ = 1 before trusting a sign convention.
  For periods, turn every hypergeometric series into its coefficient
  ratio, then read off θ-operator, radius and exponents. For HMS on the
  torus draw the lines on a square and count intersections. Harder:
  derive the Aspinwall-Morrison 1/k³ from the obstruction bundle on
  M̄_{0,0}(P¹, k); compute n₂ = 609250 by Kontsevich's graph sum on
  M̄_{0,0}(P⁴, 2); prove WDVV from the splitting axiom and deduce N₅;
  compute Y(q) to q⁵ and extract n₅; work out the Frobenius basis ϖ₀,
  ..., ϖ₃ and the integral monodromy around the conifold (a symplectic
  reflection in the vanishing S³); verify Batyrev's formula for the
  sextic in P(1,1,1,1,2) with its resolved polytope; and state and check
  Polishchuk-Zaslow's identification of m₂ with theta-function products.


================================================================================
TOPIC 13.8 — DIFFERENTIAL GEOMETRY (ADVANCED): RIEMANNIAN GEOMETRY,
COMPARISON THEOREMS, RICCI FLOW, GEOMETRIC ANALYSIS, KÄHLER GEOMETRY,
SYMPLECTIC TOPOLOGY
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - LEVI-CIVITA CONNECTION: the unique torsion-free (∇_XY − ∇_YX = [X, Y])
    metric connection, given by the KOSZUL formula; in coordinates
    Γᵏᵢⱼ = ½gᵏˡ(∂ᵢgⱼₗ + ∂ⱼgᵢₗ − ∂ₗgᵢⱼ). For g = dr² + f(r)²dθ²: Γʳ_θθ =
    −ff′, Γ^θ_rθ = f′/f, Gaussian curvature K = −f″/f. For g =
    e^{2u}(dx² + dy²): K = −e^{−2u}Δu (Δ the flat Laplacian).
  - CURVATURE: R(X, Y)Z = ∇_X∇_YZ − ∇_Y∇_XZ − ∇_[X,Y]Z; symmetries
    R_{ijkl} = −R_{jikl} = −R_{ijlk} = R_{klij}, first Bianchi identity;
    n²(n² − 1)/12 independent components (1, 6, 20 for n = 2, 3, 4).
    SECTIONAL K(σ) = ⟨R(X, Y)Y, X⟩/|X ∧ Y|², RICCI Ric(v, v) = ∑ K(v, eᵢ)
    (|v| = 1, eᵢ ⊥ v), SCALAR R = tr Ric. CONSTANT CURVATURE κ: Ric =
    (n − 1)κg, R = n(n − 1)κ. EINSTEIN: Ric = λg. Scaling g ↦ cg: K and R
    divide by c, Ric (as a (0,2)-tensor) is unchanged. In dimension 2 R =
    2K; in dimension 3 Ric determines the whole curvature tensor.
  - MODEL SPACES Mⁿ_κ: Sⁿ(1/√κ), Rⁿ, Hⁿ(1/√−κ); snκ(t) = sin(√κ t)/√κ,
    t, sinh(√−κ t)/√−κ; g = dt² + snκ(t)²g_{S^{n−1}} in polar
    coordinates. Products: a mixed plane in M × N has K = 0; Ric splits.
  - GEODESICS AND EXP: ∇_γ′γ′ = 0; GAUSS LEMMA; normal coordinates with
    gᵢⱼ = δᵢⱼ − ⅓R_{ikjl}xᵏxˡ + O(|x|³). HOPF-RINOW: metric completeness ⇔
    geodesic completeness ⇔ closed bounded sets compact; then any two
    points are joined by a minimizing geodesic. INJECTIVITY RADIUS,
    CUT LOCUS; KLINGENBERG: compact, 0 < K ≤ Δ ⇒ inj ≥ min(π/√Δ, ½·length
    of the shortest closed geodesic).
  - JACOBI FIELDS J″ + R(J, γ′)γ′ = 0 = variation fields of geodesic
    variations; in Mⁿ_κ, J(0) = 0 ⇒ J(t) = snκ(t)E(t), E parallel ⊥ γ′.
    CONJUGATE POINTS (J(0) = 0 = J(t₀), J ≢ 0) = critical values of exp.
    SECOND VARIATION / INDEX FORM I(V, V) = ∫(|V′|² − ⟨R(V, γ′)γ′, V⟩)dt;
    a geodesic past its first conjugate point is not minimizing (Jacobi).
  - RAUCH COMPARISON: K_M ≤ K_N along γ ⇒ Jacobi fields in M (J(0) = 0,
    |J′(0)| equal) grow at least as fast as in N, until the first
    conjugate point of N. TOPONOGOV: K ≥ κ ⇒ geodesic triangles (hinges)
    are FATTER than in M²_κ (third side ≤ model side); K ≤ 0 on a simply
    connected complete M (CAT(0)) ⇒ triangles are THINNER.
  - BONNET-MYERS: complete, Ric ≥ (n − 1)k > 0 ⇒ diam ≤ π/√k, M compact,
    π₁ finite. CHENG: equality diam = π/√k ⇒ M isometric to Sⁿ(1/√k).
    SYNGE: compact, K > 0: n even & orientable ⇒ simply connected; n even
    ⇒ π₁ = 1 or Z/2; n odd ⇒ M orientable. Uniform positivity is needed
    (paraboloid: K > 0, noncompact).
  - CARTAN-HADAMARD: complete, K ≤ 0 ⇒ exp_p : TₚM → M is a covering map;
    universal cover ≅ Rⁿ, π₁ torsion-free, πᵢ = 0 (i ≥ 2). PREISSMAN:
    compact, K < 0 ⇒ every abelian subgroup of π₁ is cyclic (so T² × Σ₂
    or Σ₂ × Σ₂ have no metric with K < 0). GAUSS-BONNET ∫K dA = 2πχ(M).
  - BISHOP-GROMOV: Ric ≥ (n − 1)κ ⇒ r ↦ vol B(p, r)/V_κ(r) is
    non-increasing, V_κ(r) = |S^{n−1}|∫₀ʳ snκ(t)^{n−1}dt; so vol B ≤ V_κ
    and vol M ≤ vol Sⁿ(1) if Ric ≥ n − 1 (equality ⇔ isometric).
    LAPLACIAN COMPARISON Δr ≤ (n − 1)snκ′/snκ. Ric ≥ 0 ⇒ π₁ of a compact
    M has polynomial growth (Milnor); CHEEGER-GROMOLL SPLITTING: Ric ≥ 0
    and a line ⇒ M = N × R isometrically.
  - SPHERE THEOREMS: complete simply connected, ¼ < K ≤ 1 ⇒ M
    homeomorphic to Sⁿ (Rauch-Berger-Klingenberg), DIFFEOMORPHIC (Brendle-
    Schoen 2009, via Ricci flow). Sharp: CPⁿ has ¼ ≤ K ≤ 1 (after
    scaling). GROVE-SHIOHAMA: K ≥ 1, diam > π/2 ⇒ homeomorphic to Sⁿ.
  - GEOMETRIC ANALYSIS: Δ = div∇ (spectrum −λ, 0 = λ₀ < λ₁ ≤ ...).
    BOCHNER: ½Δ|∇f|² = |∇²f|² + ⟨∇f, ∇Δf⟩ + Ric(∇f, ∇f); Weitzenböck on
    1-forms Δ_H = ∇*∇ + Ric. Consequences: compact, Ric > 0 ⇒ b₁ = 0;
    Ric ≥ 0 ⇒ harmonic 1-forms parallel, b₁ ≤ n (= n only for flat Tⁿ).
    LICHNEROWICZ: Ric ≥ (n − 1)k > 0 ⇒ λ₁ ≥ nk; OBATA: equality ⇔ round
    Sⁿ(1/√k). ZHONG-YANG: Ric ≥ 0, diam d ⇒ λ₁ ≥ π²/d². CHEEGER λ₁ ≥
    h²/4. Spectrum of Sⁿ(1): k(k + n − 1) with multiplicity C(n + k, n) −
    C(n + k − 2, n). WEYL: N(λ) ~ ω_n vol(M) λ^{n/2}/(2π)ⁿ. HODGE: each
    de Rham class has a unique harmonic representative.
  - RICCI FLOW ∂g/∂t = −2Ric (Hamilton 1982; short-time existence via the
    DeTurck trick). Evolutions: ∂R/∂t = ΔR + 2|Ric|², ∂(dμ)/∂t = −R dμ,
    d vol/dt = −∫R dμ. MAXIMUM PRINCIPLE: R_min(t) ≥ R_min(0)/(1 −
    2R_min(0)t/n), so R_min(0) > 0 ⇒ singular time T ≤ n/(2R_min(0)).
    EINSTEIN Ric = λg₀ ⇒ g(t) = (1 − 2λt)g₀: Sⁿ(r₀): r² = r₀² −
    2(n − 1)t, T = r₀²/(2(n − 1)); Hⁿ expands; flat metrics are static.
    NORMALIZED flow ∂g/∂t = −2Ric + (2/n)r̄g preserves volume. Surfaces:
    ∂g/∂t = −Rg, ∂R/∂t = ΔR + R²; the normalized flow converges to
    constant curvature (Hamilton, Chow): uniformization.
  - SOLITONS: Ric + ∇²f = λg (shrinking λ > 0, steady λ = 0, expanding
    λ < 0): self-similar solutions. GAUSSIAN (Rⁿ, f = |x|²/4, λ = ½);
    round cylinder S^{n−1} × R (r² = 2(n − 2), f = z²/4, λ = ½); CIGAR
    (R², (dx² + dy²)/(1 + x² + y²)), f = −log(1 + |x|²), K = 2/(1 + |x|²),
    R + |∇f|² = 4; Bryant soliton (steady, R³).
  - HAMILTON-PERELMAN: compact 3-manifold with Ric > 0 ⇒ spherical space
    form (Hamilton 1982); Böhm-Wilking: curvature operator > 0 ⇒ space
    form. PERELMAN: F(g, f) = ∫(R + |∇f|²)e^{−f}dμ is nondecreasing
    (dF/dt = 2∫|Ric + ∇²f|²e^{−f}dμ under the coupled flow); W-entropy,
    no local collapsing, canonical neighbourhoods (necks S² × R, caps),
    RICCI FLOW WITH SURGERY ⇒ THURSTON GEOMETRIZATION (8 geometries S³,
    E³, H³, S² × R, H² × R, Nil, Sol, SL̃₂R) and the POINCARÉ CONJECTURE.
  - KÄHLER MANIFOLDS: complex (M, J) with Hermitian g whose form ω(X, Y)
    = g(JX, Y) is closed; equivalently ∇J = 0, holonomy ⊆ U(n). Locally
    ω = i∂∂̄φ (φ a Kähler POTENTIAL); ω = i gⱼₖ̄ dzʲ ∧ dz̄ᵏ, and i dz ∧ dz̄
    = 2dx ∧ dy. FUBINI-STUDY ω_FS = i∂∂̄ log|Z|² on CPⁿ (∫_{CP¹}ω_FS = 2π,
    vol CPⁿ = (2π)ⁿ/n!). RICCI FORM ρ = −i∂∂̄ log det(gⱼₖ̄), closed with
    [ρ] = 2πc₁(M); ρ(ω_FS) = (n + 1)ω_FS. Complex submanifolds of Kähler
    manifolds are Kähler: all projective manifolds are Kähler.
  - HODGE THEORY on compact Kähler M: Δ_d = 2Δ_∂̄, Hᵏ(M; C) =
    ⊕_{p+q=k}H^{p,q}, h^{p,q} = h^{q,p} = h^{n−p,n−q}; odd Betti numbers
    even, b_{2k} ≥ 1 ([ω]ᵏ ≠ 0); HARD LEFSCHETZ Lᵏ : H^{n−k} ≅ H^{n+k}.
    Examples: S¹ × S³ (Hopf surface, b₁ = 1) is complex non-Kähler; K3:
    h^{2,0} = 1, h^{1,1} = 20; quintic threefold h^{1,1} = 1, h^{2,1} =
    101, χ = −200. Surfaces: σ = ∑(−1)^q h^{p,q} = 2 + 2h^{2,0} − h^{1,1}.
  - CALABI-YAU / KÄHLER-EINSTEIN: YAU: compact Kähler with c₁ = 0 (in
    H²(M; R)) ⇒ a unique Ricci-flat Kähler metric in each Kähler class;
    AUBIN-YAU: c₁ < 0 ⇒ a unique KE metric with ρ = −ω. Fano c₁ > 0:
    obstructions MATSUSHIMA (Aut⁰ reductive) and FUTAKI; Bl₁CP², Bl₂CP²
    have no KE metric, CP², CP¹ × CP¹, Bl_kCP² (3 ≤ k ≤ 8) do (Tian);
    CHEN-DONALDSON-SUN: KE ⇔ K-polystable. Degree-d hypersurface in
    CP^{n+1}: c₁ = (n + 2 − d)h.
  - SYMPLECTIC MANIFOLDS: ω closed, non-degenerate; dim 2n; ωⁿ ≠ 0 is a
    volume form, so on closed M [ω]ᵏ ≠ 0 in H^{2k} (S⁴, S⁶, S¹ × S³ are
    not symplectic). DARBOUX: locally ω = ∑dqᵢ ∧ dpᵢ; MOSER: a path ωₜ of
    symplectic forms with [ωₜ] constant on closed M is an isotopy (two
    area forms of equal total area on a closed surface are
    symplectomorphic). Examples: T*N (ω = −dλ), Kähler manifolds,
    coadjoint orbits; Kodaira-Thurston manifold (b₁ = 3) is symplectic
    but not Kähler. Compatible J exist and form a contractible space.
  - HAMILTONIAN DYNAMICS: ι_{X_H}ω = dH (convention ω = dq ∧ dp: q̇ =
    ∂H/∂p, ṗ = −∂H/∂q); {F, G} = ω(X_F, X_G) = ∑(F_qG_p − F_pG_q), Ḟ =
    {F, H}. MOMENT MAP μ : M → 𝔤* with d⟨μ, ξ⟩ = ι_{ξ_M}ω, equivariant.
    ATIYAH-GUILLEMIN-STERNBERG: for a torus, μ(M) is the convex hull of
    μ(fixed points); DELZANT: toric manifolds ↔ Delzant polytopes (CP²:
    triangle, S² × S²: rectangle). DUISTERMAAT-HECKMAN: μ_*(Liouville)
    is piecewise polynomial (S²: uniform, Archimedes).
  - SYMPLECTIC TOPOLOGY (rigidity): LAGRANGIANS L (dim n, ω|L = 0):
    graphs of closed 1-forms in T*N, graphs of symplectomorphisms,
    Clifford torus; WEINSTEIN neighbourhood T*L. GROMOV NONSQUEEZING:
    B^{2n}(r) ↪ B²(R) × R^{2n−2} symplectically ⇒ r ≤ R (volume-preserving
    maps can squeeze); via J-holomorphic curves; symplectic capacities,
    Gromov width. ARNOLD CONJECTURE (Floer homology): a non-degenerate
    Hamiltonian diffeomorphism of closed M has ≥ ∑bᵢ(M) fixed points
    (≥ 2^{2n} on T^{2n}, ≥ n + 1 on CPⁿ). Gromov: no closed exact
    Lagrangian in Cⁿ.

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. (a) For the metric g = dr² + f(r)²dθ² (f > 0) compute the Christoffel
    symbols and prove that the Gaussian curvature is K = −f″/f. (b) Find
    K for f = sin r, f = sinh r, and for the torus of revolution a²dφ² +
    (b + a cos φ)²dθ² (0 < a < b); check Gauss-Bonnet for the torus.
    (c) On the upper half-plane g = (dx² + dy²)/y² find all Γᵏᵢⱼ, K, and
    the distances d(i, 2i) and d(i, 1 + i).
    Answer: (a) Γʳ_θθ = −ff′, Γ^θ_rθ = Γ^θ_θr = f′/f, others 0. (b) +1,
    −1, K = cos φ/(a(b + a cos φ)), ∫K dA = 0 = 2πχ(T²). (c) Γˣ_xy =
    −1/y, Γʸ_xx = 1/y, Γʸ_yy = −1/y, K = −1; d(i, 2i) = ln 2, d(i, 1 + i)
    = arccosh(3/2) = ln((3 + √5)/2) ≈ 0.9624.
    Teacher explains: (a) g_rr = 1, g_θθ = f², g_rθ = 0, and only ∂_r g_θθ
    = 2ff′ is non-zero. From Γᵏᵢⱼ = ½gᵏˡ(∂ᵢgⱼₗ + ∂ⱼgᵢₗ − ∂ₗgᵢⱼ):
    Γʳ_θθ = ½·1·(−∂_r f²) = −ff′ and Γ^θ_rθ = ½f⁻²∂_r f² = f′/f. So
       ∇_r∂_r = 0, ∇_r∂_θ = ∇_θ∂_r = (f′/f)∂_θ, ∇_θ∂_θ = −ff′∂_r.
    Now R(∂_r, ∂_θ)∂_θ = ∇_r(∇_θ∂_θ) − ∇_θ(∇_r∂_θ) (the bracket is 0):
       ∇_r(−ff′∂_r) = −(f′² + ff″)∂_r,
       ∇_θ((f′/f)∂_θ) = (f′/f)(−ff′)∂_r = −f′²∂_r,
    so R(∂_r, ∂_θ)∂_θ = −ff″∂_r and K = ⟨R(∂_r, ∂_θ)∂_θ, ∂_r⟩/(|∂_r|²|∂_θ|²)
    = −ff″/f² = −f″/f. (b) sin″ = −sin gives K = 1 (round sphere in polar
    coordinates), sinh″ = sinh gives K = −1 (hyperbolic plane). For the
    torus put r = aφ, f = b + a cos(r/a): f″ = −(1/a)cos φ, so K =
    cos φ/(a(b + a cos φ)): positive on the outer half, negative on the
    inner half. dA = a(b + a cos φ)dφ dθ, so ∫K dA = ∫₀^{2π}∫₀^{2π} cos φ
    dφ dθ = 0 = 2πχ(T²). (c) E = G = y⁻². Γˣ_xy = E_y/(2E) = (−2y⁻³)/(2y⁻²)
    = −1/y, Γʸ_xx = −E_y/(2G) = 1/y, Γʸ_yy = G_y/(2G) = −1/y; the rest
    vanish. Conformal formula with u = −ln y: Δu = 1/y², K = −y²·y⁻² = −1.
    Vertical lines are geodesics: d(i, 2i) = ∫₁² dy/y = ln 2. In general
    cosh d(z, w) = 1 + |z − w|²/(2 Im z Im w); for i, 1 + i this is 1 +
    1/2 = 3/2, d = ln(3/2 + √(9/4 − 1)) = ln((3 + √5)/2).
    Common mistakes: forgetting the factor ½ in Γ; writing K = −f″ (the
    formula K = −f″/f needs |∂_r| = 1, i.e. r must be arclength); taking
    the horizontal segment from i to 1 + i as the geodesic (its length is
    1 > 0.9624; the geodesic is an arc of the circle |z − ½|² = 5/4).
    Now you try: find K for dr² + cosh²r dθ² and for the conformal metric
    4(dx² + dy²)/(1 + x² + y²)². (−1; +1)

D2. (a) Show that a metric of constant sectional curvature κ has Ric =
    (n − 1)κg and R = n(n − 1)κ, and that for a Riemannian product M × N
    Ric = Ric_M ⊕ Ric_N and every mixed plane has K = 0. (b) Compute Ric
    and R for S²(1) × S²(1), S²(1) × S²(2), S²(1) × H²(1), S³(1) × S¹.
    Which are Einstein? (c) When is Sᵖ(a) × S^q(b) (p, q ≥ 2) Einstein?
    Give the ratio b/a for S² × S³.
    Answer: (a) proof below. (b) Ric = g, R = 4 (Einstein); eigenvalues
    1, 1, ¼, ¼, R = 5/2; 1, 1, −1, −1, R = 0; 2, 2, 2, 0, R = 6; only
    the first is Einstein. (c) (p − 1)/a² = (q − 1)/b²; b/a = √2.
    Teacher explains: (a) Constant curvature means ⟨R(X, Y)Y, X⟩ = κ(|X|²
    |Y|² − ⟨X, Y⟩²) for all X, Y; by the symmetries this forces R(X, Y)Z =
    κ(⟨Y, Z⟩X − ⟨X, Z⟩Y). Then Ric(v, v) = ∑_{i=1}^{n−1} K(v, eᵢ) = (n −
    1)κ for unit v, so Ric = (n − 1)κg, and R = tr Ric = n(n − 1)κ. For a
    product the Levi-Civita connection is the product connection: for X
    tangent to M and U tangent to N, ∇_XU = 0 when both come from the
    factors, so R(X, U) = 0 and K(X, U) = 0; the Ricci trace over an
    orthonormal basis adapted to TM ⊕ TN only picks up the curvature of
    the factor. (b) S²(r) has Ric = r⁻²g. S²(1) × S²(1): Ric = 1·g ⊕ 1·g
    = g, R = 2 + 2 = 4: EINSTEIN with λ = 1 (sectional curvatures range
    over [0, 1], so Einstein does not mean constant curvature). S²(1) ×
    S²(2): Ric eigenvalues 1, 1, ¼, ¼; R = 2 + ½ = 5/2. S²(1) × H²(1):
    eigenvalues 1, 1, −1, −1, R = 0 (scalar flat but not Ricci flat).
    S³(1) × S¹: Ric = 2g_{S³} ⊕ 0, R = 6. (c) Sᵖ(a) has Ric = ((p −
    1)/a²)g, so the product is Einstein iff (p − 1)/a² = (q − 1)/b². For
    S² × S³: 1/a² = 2/b², b/a = √2, and then λ = 1/a².
    Common mistakes: thinking Ric of a product has off-diagonal terms;
    thinking the (0, 2)-tensor Ric scales with g (it is scale-invariant;
    only the Einstein CONSTANT scales as 1/c); believing "Einstein ⇒
    constant curvature" (true only for n ≤ 3).
    Now you try: compute R for S²(1) × S²(1) × S²(1) and decide whether
    CPⁿ with 1 ≤ K ≤ 4 is Einstein. (R = 6; yes, Ric = 2(n + 1)g)

D3. (a) Solve the Jacobi equation J″ + R(J, γ′)γ′ = 0 in a space of
    constant curvature κ for J(0) = 0, and find the first conjugate
    point. (b) Along a unit geodesic γ of length L in S²(1), evaluate the
    index form on V(t) = sin(πt/L)E(t) (E parallel, unit, ⊥ γ′); deduce
    that great-circle arcs longer than π do not minimize; compute I for
    L = 2π. (c) (Rauch) If K ≤ ¼ (resp. K ≤ −1) and J(0) = 0, |J′(0)| =
    1, J ⊥ γ′, give a lower bound for |J(π)| (resp. |J(1)|).
    Answer: (a) J = snκ(t)E(t); first conjugate point at t = π/√κ if κ >
    0, none if κ ≤ 0. (b) I = (L/2)(π²/L² − 1) < 0 for L > π; I = −3π/4
    for L = 2π. (c) |J(π)| ≥ 2 sin(π/2) = 2; |J(1)| ≥ sinh 1 ≈ 1.1752.
    Teacher explains: (a) In constant curvature R(J, γ′)γ′ = κ(J −
    ⟨J, γ′⟩γ′) = κJ for J ⊥ γ′. Writing J = h(t)E(t) with E parallel,
    J″ = h″E, and the equation is h″ + κh = 0, h(0) = 0, h′(0) = 1: h =
    snκ(t) = sin(√κ t)/√κ, t, sinh(√−κ t)/√−κ. The first positive zero
    exists only for κ > 0 and is π/√κ (π on the unit sphere: all great
    circles from p meet again at −p). (b) With V′ = (π/L)cos(πt/L)E and
    ⟨R(V, γ′)γ′, V⟩ = |V|² (K = 1):
       I(V, V) = ∫₀ᴸ ((π/L)²cos²(πt/L) − sin²(πt/L)) dt
               = (L/2)(π²/L²) − L/2 = (L/2)(π²/L² − 1).
    V vanishes at both ends, so V is the variation field of a proper
    variation, and the second variation of length is I(V, V) < 0 when L >
    π: nearby curves with the same endpoints are shorter, so γ is not
    minimizing. For L = 2π: I = π(¼ − 1) = −3π/4. (c) Rauch compares with
    the model of curvature κ = ¼ (sn = 2 sin(t/2), first conjugate point
    2π): |J(t)| ≥ 2 sin(t/2) for 0 ≤ t ≤ 2π, so |J(π)| ≥ 2. For K ≤ −1
    compare with H²: |J(t)| ≥ sinh t for all t; |J(1)| ≥ sinh 1 =
    (e − e⁻¹)/2 ≈ 1.1752. In particular K ≤ ¼ gives no conjugate points
    before 2π, K ≤ 0 none at all.
    Common mistakes: reversing Rauch (MORE curvature means SLOWER growth);
    applying the comparison beyond the model's first conjugate point;
    forgetting that V must vanish at the endpoints for I to be the second
    variation of a fixed-endpoint variation.
    Now you try: first conjugate point along a geodesic of S³(2), and
    |J(t)| for J(0) = 0, |J′(0)| = 1 there. (2π; 2|sin(t/2)|)

D4. (a) Prove BONNET-MYERS: a complete Mⁿ with Ric ≥ (n − 1)k > 0 has
    diam ≤ π/√k, is compact and has finite π₁. (b) Compare the bound with
    the true diameter for S³(1), S²(1) × S²(1), S²(1) × S³(1). (c) Use
    SYNGE's theorem to decide which of RP², RP⁴, RP² × RP², RP³ × RP³,
    the lens space L(5, 1), S¹ × S² carry a metric with K > 0.
    Answer: (a) proof below. (b) π vs π; π√3 ≈ 5.441 vs π√2 ≈ 4.443;
    2π ≈ 6.283 vs π√2. (c) yes: RP², RP⁴, L(5, 1); no: RP² × RP²,
    RP³ × RP³, S¹ × S².
    Teacher explains: (a) Let p, q have d(p, q) = L and γ a unit-speed
    minimizing geodesic (Hopf-Rinow). Take a parallel orthonormal frame
    E₁, ..., E_{n−1} ⊥ γ′ and Vᵢ = sin(πt/L)Eᵢ. Summing the index forms,
       ∑I(Vᵢ, Vᵢ) = ∫₀ᴸ ((n − 1)(π/L)²cos²(πt/L) − sin²(πt/L)Ric(γ′, γ′))dt
                  ≤ (n − 1)(L/2)(π²/L² − k).
    Since γ minimizes, every I(Vᵢ, Vᵢ) ≥ 0, so π²/L² ≥ k, L ≤ π/√k.
    Bounded and complete ⇒ compact. The universal cover M̃ carries the
    pulled-back metric with the same Ricci bound, so it is compact too,
    and the covering has finitely many sheets: |π₁(M)| < ∞. (b) S³(1):
    Ric = 2g = (3 − 1)·1·g, k = 1, bound π = diameter (Cheng's rigidity).
    S²(1) × S²(1): Ric = g = (4 − 1)(⅓)g, bound π/√(1/3) = π√3; the true
    diameter is √(π² + π²) = π√2 (distances add in quadrature). S²(1) ×
    S³(1): Ric ≥ g = (5 − 1)(¼)g, bound 2π; true diam π√2. (c) SYNGE: in
    even dimensions, K > 0 compact ⇒ π₁ = 1 (orientable) or Z/2. RP²
    and RP⁴ (round quotients) have π₁ = Z/2: fine. RP² × RP² has π₁ =
    Z/2 × Z/2 of order 4: impossible. RP³ × RP³ is orientable of even
    dimension 6 with π₁ ≠ 1: impossible. L(5, 1) = S³/(Z/5) is a round
    quotient, K = 1 (odd dimension: Synge only forces orientability).
    S¹ × S² has infinite π₁: excluded by Myers (even Ric > 0 is
    impossible).
    Common mistakes: using Myers with Ric > 0 only (the paraboloid z = x²
    + y² has K > 0 and is noncompact); forgetting that "Ric ≥ (n − 1)k"
    has the factor n − 1; confusing Synge's even and odd cases.
    Now you try: a complete 4-manifold with Ric ≥ 6g; bound its diameter.
    (Ric ≥ 3·2·g, k = 2, diam ≤ π/√2)

D5. (a) State CARTAN-HADAMARD and explain why a compact M with K ≤ 0 has
    universal cover Rⁿ and torsion-free π₁. (b) Which of T³, S² × S¹,
    RP³, Σ₂ × S¹, Σ₂ × Σ₂, T² × Σ₂ admit a metric with K ≤ 0? with K < 0?
    (Σ₂ = closed surface of genus 2.) (c) Using Gauss-Bonnet find the
    area of a hyperbolic (K = −1) surface of genus g and of a hyperbolic
    triangle with angles π/2, π/4, π/5; compute the hypotenuse of a
    right triangle with legs 3, 4 in H² and with legs 1, 1 on S²(1).
    Answer: (a) exp_p is a covering map. (b) K ≤ 0: T³, Σ₂ × S¹, Σ₂ × Σ₂,
    T² × Σ₂; K < 0: none of the listed. (c) 4π(g − 1); π/20; arccosh(cosh
    3 cosh 4) ≈ 6.310 > 5; arccos(cos²1) ≈ 1.275 < √2 ≈ 1.414.
    Teacher explains: (a) K ≤ 0 ⇒ Jacobi fields with J(0) = 0 satisfy
    (|J|²)″ = 2|J′|² − 2⟨R(J, γ′)γ′, J⟩ ≥ 0, so |J| never returns to 0:
    no conjugate points, exp_p is a local diffeomorphism everywhere.
    With the pulled-back metric exp_p*g, TₚM is complete (radial lines
    are geodesics), and a local isometry from a complete manifold is a
    covering map. So M̃ ≅ Rⁿ, all πᵢ (i ≥ 2) vanish. If g ∈ π₁ had finite
    order, the finite group ⟨g⟩ would act isometrically on the CAT(0)
    space M̃ and fix the CENTRE OF MASS of an orbit (strict convexity of
    d²) — impossible for a nontrivial deck transformation, which acts
    freely. (b) T³ is flat. S² × S¹ has universal cover S² × R, not
    contractible: no. RP³ has finite π₁ (torsion): no. Σ₂ × S¹, Σ₂ × Σ₂,
    T² × Σ₂ are products of K ≤ 0 metrics (hyperbolic × flat): K ≤ 0.
    K < 0 is impossible for T³, Σ₂ × S¹, Σ₂ × Σ₂, T² × Σ₂ by PREISSMAN:
    each π₁ contains Z² (e.g. Z ⊕ Z from two commuting factors), which is
    not cyclic. (c) Gauss-Bonnet: −Area = 2π(2 − 2g), Area = 4π(g − 1)
    (4π for g = 2). For a geodesic triangle ∫K dA + ∑(π − αᵢ) = 2π, so
    Area = π − ∑αᵢ = π − (10 + 5 + 4)π/20 = π/20. Hyperbolic Pythagoras:
    cosh c = cosh a cosh b = cosh 3 cosh 4, c ≈ 6.310 ≥ 5, as Toponogov
    (K ≤ 0 thin triangles: third side ≥ Euclidean) predicts. Spherical:
    cos c = cos a cos b = cos²1, c ≈ 1.275 ≤ √2, as TOPONOGOV for K ≥ 0
    predicts.
    Common mistakes: quoting Preissman for K ≤ 0 (T² is flat with π₁ =
    Z²); forgetting that products have mixed planes with K = 0 (so a
    product never has K < 0); using the Euclidean angle sum.
    Now you try: the area of a closed hyperbolic surface with χ = −6, and
    whether S³ × Σ₂ admits K ≤ 0. (12π; no: its universal cover S³ × H²
    is not contractible)

D6. (a) State the BISHOP-GROMOV volume comparison theorem and give the
    idea of its proof. (b) Compute V_κ(r) for n = 2 and n = 3, κ = ±1,
    and vol S³(1). (c) Let M³ be compact with Ric ≥ 2g. Show vol(M) ≤
    2π² and |π₁(M)|·vol(M) ≤ 2π²; deduce that vol(M) > π² forces M to be
    simply connected. What are the volumes of RP³ and L(p, q) (round)?
    Answer: (a) see below. (b) n = 2: 2π(1 − cos r), 2π(cosh r − 1);
    n = 3: π(2r − sin 2r), π(sinh 2r − 2r); vol S³(1) = 2π². (c) proof
    below; vol RP³ = π², vol L(p, q) = 2π²/p.
    Teacher explains: (a) If Ric ≥ (n − 1)κ then vol B(p, r)/V_κ(r) is
    non-increasing in r (and → 1 as r → 0). PROOF IDEA: in polar
    coordinates dvol = 𝒜(t, θ)dt dθ inside the cut locus. The mean
    curvature m = ∂_t log 𝒜 = Δr satisfies the Riccati inequality m′ +
    m²/(n − 1) ≤ −Ric(∂_r, ∂_r) ≤ −(n − 1)κ (from Bochner applied to r,
    with |∇²r|² ≥ (Δr)²/(n − 1)); comparing with the model, where
    equality holds, gives m ≤ (n − 1)snκ′/snκ (LAPLACIAN COMPARISON), so
    𝒜(t, θ)/snκ(t)^{n−1} decreases in t. Integrating a ratio of
    decreasing densities gives the monotone volume ratio. (b) V_κ(r) =
    |S^{n−1}|∫₀ʳ snκ(t)^{n−1}dt. n = 2: 2π∫₀ʳ sin t dt = 2π(1 − cos r) and
    2π(cosh r − 1). n = 3: 4π∫₀ʳ sin²t dt = 4π(r/2 − sin 2r/4) = π(2r −
    sin 2r), and 4π∫sinh² = π(sinh 2r − 2r). At r = π: vol S³(1) = 2π².
    (c) Ric ≥ 2g = (3 − 1)·1·g, so κ = 1; by Myers diam ≤ π, M = B(p, π)
    and vol M ≤ V₁(π) = 2π². The universal cover M̃ satisfies the same
    bound and is compact (Myers), with vol M̃ = |π₁|·vol M ≤ 2π². If
    vol M > π² then |π₁| < 2, so π₁ = 1. Round RP³ = S³/±1 has volume
    π²; a round lens space S³/(Z/p) has 2π²/p — so the inequality is
    sharp. (With Hamilton's theorem such M is then diffeomorphic to S³.)
    Common mistakes: using V_κ for the wrong dimension; forgetting the
    factor |S^{n−1}| (2π for n = 2, 4π for n = 3); applying Bishop-Gromov
    with a SECTIONAL lower bound only (Ricci suffices, and is what is
    used).
    Now you try: area of a disc of radius ln 2 in H², and the Bishop-
    Gromov upper bound for vol B(p, 2r)/vol B(p, r) if Ric ≥ 0, n = 3.
    (2π(5/4 − 1) = π/2; 2³ = 8)

D7. (a) From Bochner's formula ½Δ|∇f|² = |∇²f|² + ⟨∇f, ∇Δf⟩ + Ric(∇f,
    ∇f) prove LICHNEROWICZ: if Mⁿ is compact with Ric ≥ (n − 1)k > 0,
    then λ₁ ≥ nk. Also show Ric > 0 ⇒ b₁(M) = 0. (b) Find all eigenvalues
    and multiplicities of −Δ on S²(1) and on RP²(round). (c) Compute λ₁
    of S²(1) × S²(1) and compare with the Lichnerowicz bound; compute λ₁
    of the flat torus R²/(2πZ)² and of a circle of length L and compare
    the latter with the Zhong-Yang bound π²/d².
    Answer: (a) proof below. (b) S²: k(k + 1) with multiplicity 2k + 1
    (0, 2, 6, 12, ...; 1, 3, 5, 7, ...); RP²: only even k: 0, 6, 20,
    42, ... with multiplicities 1, 5, 9, 13, ... (c) λ₁ = 2 > 4/3; torus
    λ₁ = 1 (multiplicity 4); circle λ₁ = 4π²/L² = π²/d² (d = L/2):
    equality.
    Teacher explains: (a) Let −Δf = λf, λ > 0. Integrate Bochner over M:
    ∫½Δ|∇f|² = 0, and ∫⟨∇f, ∇Δf⟩ = −λ∫|∇f|² = −λ²∫f². By Cauchy-Schwarz
    on the eigenvalues of ∇²f, |∇²f|² ≥ (Δf)²/n = λ²f²/n. Hence
       0 ≥ (λ²/n)∫f² − λ²∫f² + (n − 1)kλ∫f²,
    i.e. λ(n − 1)/n ≥ (n − 1)k, λ ≥ nk. (OBATA: equality forces ∇²f =
    −kfg, which characterizes the round sphere.) For b₁: by Hodge, a
    class in H¹ has a harmonic 1-form α; Weitzenböck gives 0 = ∫⟨Δ_Hα,
    α⟩ = ∫|∇α|² + ∫Ric(α♯, α♯), so Ric > 0 forces α = 0. (b) On R³ write
    Δ = ∂²_r + (2/r)∂_r + r⁻²Δ_S. If u = rᵏY is a harmonic homogeneous
    polynomial, 0 = k(k − 1) + 2k + Δ_SY/Y, so −Δ_SY = k(k + 1)Y. The
    space of harmonic homogeneous polynomials of degree k in 3 variables
    has dimension C(k + 2, 2) − C(k, 2) = 2k + 1, and these restrictions
    span L²(S²). Degree 1: x, y, z with λ₁ = 2 = n for n = 2 (equality in
    Lichnerowicz, as Obata says). RP² = S²/±1: keep the EVEN functions,
    Y(−x) = (−1)ᵏY(x), so k even: 0, 6, 20, 42 with 1, 5, 9, 13. (c)
    Eigenfunctions of a product are products of eigenfunctions and
    eigenvalues add: λ₁(S² × S²) = 2 (from 0 + 2 or 2 + 0, multiplicity
    6). Ric = g = (4 − 1)(⅓)g gives the bound nk = 4/3 < 2 (not sharp:
    not a round sphere). Torus: e^{i(mx+ny)} has eigenvalue m² + n²;
    λ₁ = 1 from (±1, 0), (0, ±1). Circle of length L: e^{2πikx/L} has
    λ = 4π²k²/L², λ₁ = 4π²/L², diameter L/2, and π²/(L/2)² = 4π²/L²:
    Zhong-Yang is sharp.
    Common mistakes: sign of Δ (with Δ = div∇ the eigenvalues are −λ);
    forgetting the factor 1/n in |∇²f|² ≥ (Δf)²/n; counting 2k + 1 also
    for RP² with odd k.
    Now you try: the first three distinct eigenvalues of S³(1) with
    multiplicities, and λ₁ of S²(1/2). (0, 3, 8 with 1, 4, 9; 8)

D8. (a) Show that if Ric(g₀) = λg₀ then g(t) = (1 − 2λt)g₀ solves the
    Ricci flow ∂g/∂t = −2Ric; find r(t) and the singular time for
    Sⁿ(r₀), and r(t) for Hⁿ(r₀). (b) Run the Ricci flow on S²(1) ×
    S²(2), S³(1) × S²(1) and S²(1) × H²(1): which factor collapses first
    and when? (c) Using ∂R/∂t = ΔR + 2|Ric|² and |Ric|² ≥ R²/n, prove
    R_min(t) ≥ R_min(0)/(1 − 2R_min(0)t/n); check it (and d vol/dt =
    −∫R dμ) on the round S³(1) and S²(1).
    Answer: (a) r² = r₀² − 2(n − 1)t, T = r₀²/(2(n − 1)); Hⁿ: r² = r₀² +
    2(n − 1)t. (b) S²(1) collapses at t = ½ (other radius² then 3); S³(1)
    at t = ¼ (S² radius² then ½); S² at t = ½ while H² has radius² 2.
    (c) S³(1): R(t) = 6/(1 − 4t), equality, T = ¼; S²(1): vol = 4π(1 − 2t),
    d vol/dt = −8π = −∫2 dμ.
    Teacher explains: (a) Ric is scale invariant: Ric(cg₀) = Ric(g₀) =
    λg₀. So for g = c(t)g₀ the flow reads c′g₀ = −2λg₀, c = 1 − 2λt. On
    Sⁿ(r₀), g₀ = r₀²g_{S}, Ric = (n − 1)g_{S} = ((n − 1)/r₀²)g₀; hence
    r(t)² = r₀² − 2(n − 1)t, vanishing at T = r₀²/(2(n − 1)): the sphere
    shrinks to a round point. For Hⁿ(r₀), Ric = −((n − 1)/r₀²)g₀ and r²
    = r₀² + 2(n − 1)t: expansion forever. (b) The flow of a product is
    the product of the flows. S²(r): r² = r² − 2t, S³(r): r² − 4t. S²(1)
    × S²(2): first factor dies at t = ½ when the second has r² = 4 − 1 =
    3 (the solution crushes to S²(√3)-like). S³(1) × S²(1): S³ dies at ¼,
    S² has 1 − ½ = ½. S²(1) × H²(1): S² dies at ½ and H² has r² = 1 + 1
    = 2 (a model of a "neck" type singularity S² × R when the flat
    factor is R). (c) At a minimum point of R, ΔR ≥ 0, and 2|Ric|² ≥
    2R²/n (trace inequality). By the maximum principle R_min(t) is a
    supersolution of the ODE y′ = 2y²/n, whose solution is y =
    y₀/(1 − 2y₀t/n); if y₀ > 0 it blows up at n/(2y₀), so T ≤ n/(2R_min(0)).
    S³(1): R = 6/r² = 6/(1 − 4t), and 6/(1 − 2·6t/3) = 6/(1 − 4t): EQUALITY
    (Einstein metrics have |Ric|² = R²/n), n/(2R₀) = 3/12 = ¼ = T. S²(1):
    area 4πr² = 4π(1 − 2t), derivative −8π, and ∫R dμ = 2·4π = 8π (Gauss-
    Bonnet: ∫R dμ = 4πχ is constant in dimension 2).
    Common mistakes: writing r(t) = r₀ − 2(n − 1)t (it is r² that is
    linear); forgetting the factor 2 in ∂g/∂t = −2Ric; thinking the
    maximum principle bounds R_max (it controls the MINIMUM from below).
    Now you try: singular time for S⁴(2) and the scalar curvature of
    H³(1) at time t. (4/6 = 2/3; −6/(1 + 4t))

D9. (a) Show that the Gaussian soliton (Rⁿ, δ, f = |x|²/4) and the round
    cylinder S^{n−1}(r) × R with f = z²/4 satisfy Ric + ∇²f = ½g, and
    find r. (b) For Hamilton's CIGAR g = (dx² + dy²)/(1 + x² + y²) find
    K, the total curvature, the circumference at infinity, and verify
    that R + |∇f|² is constant for f = −log(1 + x² + y²). (c) Name the
    Thurston geometry of: S³/I* (Poincaré sphere), T³, S² × S¹, Σ₂ × S¹,
    the Heisenberg nilmanifold, the T²-bundle over S¹ with monodromy
    (2 1; 1 1), the unit tangent bundle of Σ₂; which of them become
    extinct under Ricci flow?
    Answer: (a) r² = 2(n − 2). (b) K = 2/(1 + |x|²), ∫K dA = 2π,
    circumference → 2π, R + |∇f|² = 4. (c) S³, E³, S² × R, H² × R, Nil,
    Sol, SL̃₂R; extinction in finite time: S³/I* and S² × S¹ (the
    latter after surgery).
    Teacher explains: (a) Flat Rⁿ: Ric = 0 and ∇²(|x|²/4) = ½δ, so λ = ½
    (shrinking). The associated flow is g(t) = (1 − t)φₜ*δ = δ up to
    diffeomorphism: flat space viewed as shrinking about the origin. On
    S^{n−1}(r) × R: Ric = ((n − 2)/r²)g_{sphere} ⊕ 0 and ∇²(z²/4) = ½dz²,
    so we need (n − 2)/r² = ½: r² = 2(n − 2) (n = 3: r² = 2). This is the
    NECK: under the flow the sphere radius² 2(n − 2)(1 − t) → 0 at t = 1.
    (b) Conformal factor e^{2u} with u = −½log(1 + ρ²), ρ² = x² + y².
    Δlog(1 + ρ²) = 4/(1 + ρ²)², so K = −e^{−2u}Δu = (1 + ρ²)·2/(1 + ρ²)² =
    2/(1 + ρ²): positive, maximal (R = 4) at the tip. dA = dx dy/(1 +
    ρ²): ∫K dA = ∫₀^∞ 2·2πρ dρ/(1 + ρ²)² = 2π. As ρ → ∞ the metric ≈
    (dρ² + ρ²dθ²)/ρ² = (d log ρ)² + dθ²: a cylinder of circumference 2π,
    consistent with total curvature 2π (Gauss-Bonnet with a cylindrical
    end). One checks Ric + ∇²f = 0 (a STEADY soliton) and |∇f|² =
    (1 + ρ²)·4ρ²/(1 + ρ²)² = 4ρ²/(1 + ρ²), so R + |∇f|² = (4 + 4ρ²)/(1 +
    ρ²) = 4. (c) Finite π₁ ⇒ S³ geometry (Perelman's elliptization; I*
    has order 120). T³: flat. S² × S¹: S² × R. Σ₂ × S¹: H² × R.
    Heisenberg quotient: Nil. The matrix (2 1; 1 1) has trace 3 > 2
    (Anosov), giving Sol. Unit tangent bundle of Σ₂ (Euler number 2 − 2g
    = −2 ≠ 0 over a hyperbolic base): SL̃₂R. Spherical space forms shrink
    to round points (Hamilton for Ric > 0); S² × S¹ develops neck
    pinches, and after surgery everything becomes extinct in finite time
    (Perelman, Colding-Minicozzi: true whenever π₁ is a free product of
    finite groups and copies of Z).
    Common mistakes: calling the cylinder soliton "steady" (it shrinks);
    forgetting the conformal factor in |∇f|²; confusing Nil (nilpotent,
    trace ±2 monodromy) with Sol (hyperbolic monodromy).
    Now you try: the cylinder shrinker radius for S³ × R and the value of
    λ for f = −|x|²/4 on Rⁿ. (r² = 4; λ = −½, expanding)

D10. (a) Show that ω = i∂∂̄φ is a closed real (1, 1)-form and compute the
    Fubini-Study form ω_FS = i∂∂̄ log(1 + |z|²) on CP¹: find its total
    area, the Gaussian curvature of the associated metric and its Ricci
    form. (b) Do the same for the Poincaré disc ω = i dz ∧ dz̄/(1 − |z|²)².
    (c) For a smooth hypersurface X_d ⊂ CP^{n+1} of degree d, find c₁(X)
    and decide the sign of the Kähler-Einstein constant (if any) for:
    a conic, a plane cubic, a quartic surface, a quintic surface, a cubic
    surface, a quintic threefold.
    Answer: (a) Area 2π, K = 2, ρ = 2ω_FS. (b) K = −2, ρ = −2ω. (c)
    c₁ = (n + 2 − d)h: conic ≅ CP¹ (positive), plane cubic (Ricci-flat),
    quartic K3 (Ricci-flat, Yau), quintic surface (negative, Aubin-Yau),
    cubic surface (positive; Bl₆CP² has a KE metric), quintic threefold
    (Ricci-flat).
    Teacher explains: (a) d = ∂ + ∂̄, ∂² = ∂̄² = 0 and ∂∂̄ = −∂̄∂, so
    d(i∂∂̄φ) = i(∂∂∂̄φ + ∂̄∂∂̄φ) = 0; conjugating, (i∂∂̄φ)‾ = −i∂̄∂φ =
    i∂∂̄φ: real. With φ = log(1 + |z|²), ∂̄φ = z dz̄/(1 + |z|²), and
    ∂(z/(1 + |z|²)) = dz/(1 + |z|²)², so ω = i dz ∧ dz̄/(1 + |z|²)² =
    2dx ∧ dy/(1 + ρ²)². Area = ∫₀^∞ 2·2πρ dρ/(1 + ρ²)² = 2π (the point at
    infinity has measure zero). The metric 2(dx² + dy²)/(1 + ρ²)² is ½ of
    the round metric 4(dx² + dy²)/(1 + ρ²)² of S²(1), i.e. S²(1/√2): K =
    2 and area 4π·½ = 2π ✓. RICCI FORM: g_{11̄} = (1 + |z|²)⁻², ρ =
    −i∂∂̄ log g_{11̄} = 2i∂∂̄ log(1 + |z|²) = 2ω. Check: ∫ρ = 4π =
    2π⟨c₁(CP¹), [CP¹]⟩ = 2π·χ(S²). (b) g_{11̄} = (1 − |z|²)⁻², and
    ∂∂̄ log(1 − |z|²) = −dz ∧ dz̄/(1 − |z|²)², so ρ = 2i∂∂̄ log(1 − |z|²)
    = −2ω: KE with λ = −2, Gaussian curvature −2 (ρ = K ω for surfaces).
    (c) ADJUNCTION: K_X = (K_{CP^{n+1}} + X)|_X = (d − n − 2)h, so c₁ =
    (n + 2 − d)h. Conic (n = 1, d = 2): c₁ = h, Fano. Plane cubic: 0,
    elliptic curve, flat. Quartic surface (n = 2, d = 4): c₁ = 0, K3,
    Ricci-flat by YAU. Quintic surface: c₁ = −h < 0, KE with negative
    constant (AUBIN-YAU). Cubic surface: c₁ = h > 0; it is Bl₆CP², which
    admits KE (Tian). Quintic threefold: c₁ = 0, Calabi-Yau.
    Common mistakes: dropping the i (then the form is imaginary);
    confusing ω_FS normalizations (∫_{CP¹} = π with the ½ in front);
    assuming every Fano manifold is KE (Bl₁CP² is not: Matsushima).
    Now you try: c₁ of a degree-6 hypersurface in CP⁴ and the Ricci form
    of ω_FS on CPⁿ. (−h; (n + 1)ω_FS)

D11. (a) Using the Hodge decomposition, prove that the odd Betti numbers
    of a compact Kähler manifold are even and that b_{2k} ≥ 1 for 0 ≤ k
    ≤ n. Deduce that S¹ × S³ and the Kodaira-Thurston manifold KT = (Nil³
    /Γ) × S¹ (b₁ = 3) are not Kähler. (b) For the smooth surface X_d ⊂
    CP³ of degree d use χ = d³ − 4d² + 6d and h^{2,0} = C(d − 1, 3) to
    give the Hodge diamond for d = 4, 5 and the signature. (c) For the
    quintic threefold (h^{1,1} = 1, h^{2,1} = 101) compute all Betti
    numbers and χ; state what Yau's theorem gives here.
    Answer: (a) proof below. (b) d = 4: h^{2,0} = 1, h^{1,1} = 20, σ =
    −16; d = 5: h^{2,0} = 4, h^{1,1} = 45, σ = −35. (c) b = 1, 0, 1, 204,
    1, 0, 1; χ = −200; a unique Ricci-flat Kähler metric in each Kähler
    class (a 1-parameter family: the class t[h], t > 0).
    Teacher explains: (a) b_{2k+1} = ∑_{p+q=2k+1}h^{p,q}; pairing h^{p,q}
    with h^{q,p} (p ≠ q since p + q is odd) gives an even sum. ω^k is
    closed and ∫ωⁿ = n!vol > 0, so [ω]ᵏ ≠ 0 and b_{2k} ≥ 1. S¹ × S³ is a
    complex manifold (Hopf surface (C² ∖ 0)/(z ~ 2z)) with b₁ = 1, b₂ = 0:
    not Kähler on two counts. KT has b₁ = 3 odd, so it is not Kähler,
    although it is complex and symplectic (Thurston 1976). (b) h^{1,0} = 0
    (Lefschetz hyperplane: b₁(X_d) = b₁(CP³) = 0). b₂ = χ − 2. d = 4: χ =
    64 − 64 + 24 = 24, b₂ = 22, h^{2,0} = C(3, 3) = 1, h^{1,1} = 22 − 2 =
    20; σ = 2 + 2h^{2,0} − h^{1,1} = 2 + 2 − 20 = −16 (the K3 value, cf.
    Rokhlin). d = 5: χ = 125 − 100 + 30 = 55, b₂ = 53, h^{2,0} = C(4, 3)
    = 4, h^{1,1} = 53 − 8 = 45, σ = 2 + 8 − 45 = −35. Check with
    Hirzebruch: σ = (c₁² − 2c₂)/3 = (5 − 110)/3 = −35 (c₁² = d(d − 4)² =
    5). The diamonds (rows h^{0,0}; h^{1,0} h^{0,1}; h^{2,0} h^{1,1}
    h^{0,2}; ...) are 1; 0 0; 1 20 1; 0 0; 1 and 1; 0 0; 4 45 4; 0 0; 1.
    (c) b₀ = b₆ = 1, b₁ = b₅ = 0, b₂ = b₄ = h^{1,1} = 1, b₃ = h^{3,0} +
    h^{2,1} + h^{1,2} + h^{0,3} = 1 + 101 + 101 + 1 = 204. χ = 4 − 204 =
    −200 = 2(h^{1,1} − h^{2,1}). Since c₁ = 0 (D10), YAU'S THEOREM gives
    for each Kähler class a unique Ricci-flat Kähler metric with
    holonomy SU(3).
    Common mistakes: forgetting h^{3,0} = h^{0,3} = 1 in b₃; using σ =
    b₂⁺ − b₂⁻ without knowing b₂⁺ = 1 + 2h^{2,0}; thinking non-Kähler
    means non-symplectic (KT is symplectic).
    Now you try: Hodge numbers h^{1,0}, h^{2,0}, h^{1,1} of a complex
    2-torus and its signature. (2, 1, 4; σ = 2 + 2 − 4 = 0)

D12. (a) Prove that S^{2n} (n ≥ 2) carries no symplectic form, and
    sketch MOSER'S trick: a path ωₜ of symplectic forms with [ωₜ]
    constant on closed M is induced by an isotopy. (b) For S²(1) with
    area form ω = dθ ∧ dz (cylindrical coordinates) find the moment map
    of rotation, the area of the band {a ≤ z ≤ b}, and the period of the
    flow of H = ½(q² + p²) on (R², dq ∧ dp); describe the moment image of
    the T²-action on CP² by [z₀ : z₁ : z₂] ↦ [z₀ : e^{iθ₁}z₁ : e^{iθ₂}z₂].
    (c) State GROMOV'S NONSQUEEZING THEOREM, show that B⁴(2) ⊂ R⁴ can be
    mapped into Z(1) = B²(1) × R² by a linear volume-preserving map, and
    state the Arnold lower bounds for non-degenerate Hamiltonian
    diffeomorphisms of S², T², CP², S² × S².
    Answer: (a) proof below. (b) μ = z (up to sign), band area 2π(b −
    a), period 2π, the triangle with vertices (0, 0), (1, 0), (0, 1).
    (c) r ≤ R needed for symplectic embeddings; L = diag(½, ½, 2, 2);
    2, 4, 3, 4.
    Teacher explains: (a) If ω were symplectic on closed S^{2n}, [ω] ∈
    H²(S^{2n}) = 0 (n ≥ 2), so ω = dα and ωⁿ = d(α ∧ ωⁿ⁻¹); Stokes gives
    ∫ωⁿ = 0, contradicting that ωⁿ is a volume form. MOSER: write
    dωₜ/dt = dσₜ (constant class). Seek the flow φₜ of Xₜ with φₜ*ωₜ =
    ω₀; differentiating and using Cartan's formula with dωₜ = 0 gives
    φₜ*(L_{Xₜ}ωₜ + dωₜ/dt) = φₜ*d(ι_{Xₜ}ωₜ + σₜ) = 0, solved by ι_{Xₜ}ωₜ
    = −σₜ, which has a unique solution Xₜ because ωₜ is non-degenerate;
    integrate Xₜ (M compact). (b) The rotation field
    is X = ∂_θ and ι_{∂θ}(dθ ∧ dz) = dz, so μ = z (image [−1, 1]). The
    pushforward of area under z is 2π dz: area{a ≤ z ≤ b} = 2π(b − a)
    (ARCHIMEDES; Duistermaat-Heckman: linear, here constant). H = ½(q² +
    p²): q̇ = p, ṗ = −q, circular motion of period 2π; H is the moment map
    of this S¹-action. On CP² the moment map is (|z₁|², |z₂|²)/|Z|² (up to
    sign and the normalization ∫_{CP¹}ω_FS = 2π): the image is the convex
    hull of the images (0, 0), (1, 0), (0, 1) of the three fixed points
    (ATIYAH-GUILLEMIN-STERNBERG); its area ½ gives vol CP² = (2π)²·½ =
    2π² = (2π)²/2!. (c) NONSQUEEZING: a symplectic embedding B^{2n}(r) ↪
    B²(R) × R^{2n−2} exists only if r ≤ R. The linear map L(x₁, y₁, x₂,
    y₂) = (x₁/2, y₁/2, 2x₂, 2y₂) has determinant 1 and sends B⁴(2) into
    {x₁² + y₁² ≤ 1} = Z(1), but it multiplies dx₁ ∧ dy₁ by ¼: not
    symplectic. ARNOLD (Floer homology): #Fix ≥ ∑bᵢ: S²: 1 + 1 = 2; T²:
    1 + 2 + 1 = 4; CP²: 3; S² × S²: 1 + 2 + 1 = 4.
    Common mistakes: applying the H²-argument to S² (H²(S²) ≠ 0: S² is
    symplectic); using the volume to rule out squeezing (volume allows
    it); counting fixed points of a DEGENERATE map with ∑bᵢ (the bound is
    then cup-length + 1, e.g. 3 on T²).
    Now you try: the area of the polar cap z ≥ ½ on S²(1), and the
    Arnold bound for a genus-3 surface. (π; 2 + 6 = 8)

PART B — MATHEMATICAL ONLY
--------------------------
M1. Christoffel symbols and Gaussian curvature of surfaces (ρ² = x² +
    y²; use K = −f″/f for dr² + f²dθ², K = −e^{−2u}Δu for e^{2u}|dx|²).
    a) dr² + r²dθ²                  b) dr² + sin²r dθ²
    c) dr² + 4sin²(r/2)dθ²          d) dr² + sinh²r dθ²
    e) dr² + cosh²r dθ²             f) dr² + e^{2r}dθ²
    g) dr² + (1 + r²)dθ²            h) dr² + r⁴dθ² (r > 0)
    i) (dx² + dy²)/y²               j) 4(dx² + dy²)/(1 + ρ²)²
    k) 4(dx² + dy²)/(1 − ρ²)²       l) e^{2x}(dx² + dy²)
    m) (dx² + dy²)/(1 + ρ²)         n) the graph z = x² + y² (as a
       function of ρ)
    o) the graph z = xy             p) the torus a²dφ² + (b + a cos φ)²dθ²
       at φ = 0 and at φ = π
    q) Γʳ_θθ and Γ^θ_rθ for dr² + sinh²r dθ²
    r) Γˣ_xy, Γʸ_xx, Γʸ_yy for (dx² + dy²)/y²
    Answers:
      a) 0        b) 1        c) ¼        d) −1       e) −1       f) −1
      g) −1/(1 + r²)²         h) −2/r²    i) −1       j) 1        k) −1
      l) 0        m) 2/(1 + ρ²)           n) 4/(1 + 4ρ²)²
      o) −1/(1 + x² + y²)²    p) 1/(a(b + a)) and −1/(a(b − a))
      q) −sinh r cosh r, coth r            r) −1/y, 1/y, −1/y

M2. Sectional curvature range, Ricci tensor and scalar curvature.
    a) S⁴(1)                  b) S³(2)                  c) H³(1)
    d) Hⁿ(½)                  e) S²(1) × R              f) S²(1) × S²(1) × R
    g) S²(1) × S²(3)          h) S³(1) × H³(1)          i) S²(1) × S³(√2)
    j) CP² (Fubini-Study, 1 ≤ K ≤ 4)                    k) flat T³
