M12. True (T) or false (F)? Give a counterexample for F.
     a) W^{1,2}(R²) ⊂ L^∞(R²).
     b) For bounded Ω, H₀¹(Ω) ⊂ L^{2*}(Ω) is compact (n ≥ 3).
     c) Every coercive functional on H₀¹ has a minimizer.
     d) −Δu = u⁵ has a positive solution in the unit ball of R³.
     e) −Δu = u⁵ has a positive solution on R³.
     f) Weak solutions of div(A∇u) = 0, A measurable and uniformly
        elliptic, are C¹.
     g) Δu ∈ L^∞ implies u ∈ C^{1,1}.
     h) De Giorgi's Hölder regularity holds for elliptic systems.
     i) Every entire minimal graph over R⁸ is a hyperplane.
     j) Any two coaxial unit circles bound a catenoid.
     k) Under MCF the sphere Sⁿ(r₀) ⊂ R^{n+1} vanishes at T = r₀²/(2n).
     l) Under curve shortening every closed immersed curve becomes round
        before it disappears.
     m) Burgers' equation with bounded smooth data has a global smooth
        solution.
     n) Weak solutions of scalar conservation laws are unique.
     o) Every positive solution of uₜ = Δu + u² on R³ blows up.
     p) Every positive solution of uₜ = uₓₓ + u² on R blows up.
     q) 2D Navier-Stokes on T² has global smooth solutions.
     r) Every 3D Leray-Hopf solution is known to satisfy the energy
        equality.
     s) L² is a scaling-critical space for 3D Navier-Stokes.
     t) For smooth 2D Euler, ‖ω(t)‖_∞ is constant in time.
     u) Every weak solution of 3D Euler conserves energy.
     v) The Hagen-Poiseuille flux is proportional to R².
     Answers:
       a) F: log log(1/|x|) (cut off)    b) F: bubbles u_λ, λ → ∞
       c) F: Bolza ∫((u′² − 1)² + u²) (not weakly lsc)
       d) F: Pohozaev                    e) T: Aubin-Talenti bubbles
       f) F: a = 1 on (0, ½), 3 on (½, 1): u′ jumps
       g) F: x₁x₂ log|x|                 h) F: De Giorgi 1968
       i) F: Bombieri-De Giorgi-Giusti   j) F: distance > 1.3255
       k) T
       l) F: a curve with a small inner loop forms a singularity when the
          loop contracts; Grayson needs embeddedness
       m) F: u₀ = −tanh x breaks at t = 1
       n) F: Riemann (0, 1): jump with s = ½ and the rarefaction
       o) F: p = 2 > 5/3, small data are global
       p) T (Fujita, p = 2 < 3)           q) T (Ladyzhenskaya)
       r) F: only the inequality is proved (equality under extra
          integrability, e.g. u ∈ L⁴ₜL⁴ₓ)
       s) F: ‖u_λ‖₂ = λ^{−1/2}‖u‖₂; L³ and Ḣ^{1/2} are critical
       t) T (ω is transported by a volume-preserving flow)
       u) F: Isett's C^{1/3−ε} dissipative solutions (Onsager)
       v) F: Q = πGR⁴/(8μ)

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: start every problem with SCALING — replace u by λ^βu(λx,
  λ²t) and see which terms balance: this single computation produces p*,
  the critical exponent (n + 2)/(n − 2), s_c for NLS and heat, the
  Fujita exponent's heuristic, and the critical spaces L³, Ḣ^{1/2} of
  Navier-Stokes. Then keep a small zoo of explicit solutions on the board
  and verify each by hand: the bubble 3^{1/4}(1 + r²)^{−1/2}, Liouville's
  log(8/(1 + r²)²), x/|x|, the catenoid and Scherk's surface, the
  shrinking sphere r² = r₀² − 2nt, the grim reaper, Burgers' shock and
  fan, the KdV soliton, Poiseuille, Lamb-Oseen and Taylor-Green. For
  existence questions ask the three direct-method questions (coercive?
  weakly lsc? compact?) and look for the Pohozaev or Gauss-Bonnet
  obstruction before trying to construct anything. For fluids, always
  check div u = 0 first and remember that div u = 0 kills both the
  nonlinearity and the pressure in energy estimates. Harder: prove the
  mountain pass theorem via the deformation lemma and check Palais-
  Smale for subcritical powers; prove De Giorgi's theorem (Caccioppoli,
  L^∞ bound by iteration, oscillation decay); prove Huisken's
  monotonicity formula and use it to classify compact self-shrinkers
  with H > 0; prove Kružkov uniqueness by doubling variables; prove
  Leray's existence theorem by Galerkin approximation and Aubin-Lions
  compactness, and the Ladyzhenskaya-Prodi-Serrin weak-strong
  uniqueness; and study the proof of the Caffarelli-Kohn-Nirenberg
  partial regularity theorem through the ε-regularity lemma for
  suitable weak solutions.


================================================================================
TOPIC 13.12 — DYNAMICAL SYSTEMS: HYPERBOLIC DYNAMICS, COMPLEX DYNAMICS,
KAM THEORY
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - BASIC LANGUAGE: a map f : X → X or a flow φᵗ; orbits, periodic points
    (least period), ω-limit sets, invariant sets. TOPOLOGICAL CONJUGACY
    h∘f = g∘h (h a homeomorphism) preserves periods, entropy, rotation
    numbers; smooth conjugacy also preserves multipliers (eigenvalues of
    Dfⁿ at periodic points). A conjugacy is SEMI- if h is only onto.
  - HYPERBOLIC FIXED POINT: of a map, no eigenvalue of Df(p) on |λ| = 1;
    of a flow, no eigenvalue of DX(p) with Re λ = 0. Sink, source, saddle;
    dim Eˢ, dim Eᵘ. HARTMAN-GROBMAN: near a hyperbolic fixed point f is
    TOPOLOGICALLY (C⁰, not C¹ in general) conjugate to its linear part.
    Hyperbolic linear maps/flows are C⁰-classified by dim Eˢ (and, for
    maps, orientation on Eˢ and Eᵘ). STERNBERG: C^∞ linearization when
    there are no resonances λᵢ = ∏λⱼ^{mⱼ}.
  - STABLE MANIFOLD THEOREM: Wˢ_loc(p) = {x : fⁿ(x) → p, staying near p}
    is an embedded disc tangent to Eˢ, as smooth as f; similarly Wᵘ_loc.
    Global Wˢ(p) = ⋃f⁻ⁿ(Wˢ_loc). Computed as a graph by invariance
    (power series matching). Transverse HOMOCLINIC point (Wˢ ⋔ Wᵘ ≠ {p})
    ⇒ SMALE-BIRKHOFF: a horseshoe, infinitely many periodic orbits.
  - HYPERBOLIC SET Λ (compact, invariant): TΛM = Eˢ ⊕ Eᵘ, invariant,
    ‖Dfⁿ|Eˢ‖ ≤ Cμⁿ, ‖Df⁻ⁿ|Eᵘ‖ ≤ Cμⁿ (μ < 1). ANOSOV diffeomorphism: all
    of M is hyperbolic. Example: toral automorphism A ∈ GL(n, ℤ) with no
    eigenvalue of modulus 1; CAT MAP A = [[2,1],[1,1]], λ = (3 ± √5)/2,
    irrational eigen-directions (dense leaves). #Fix(Aⁿ) = |det(Aⁿ − I)|
    (= tr Aⁿ − 2 for the cat map: 1, 5, 16, 45, 121). Geodesic flows of
    negatively curved manifolds are ANOSOV FLOWS.
  - SHADOWING (Anosov-Bowen): near a hyperbolic set every δ-pseudo-orbit
    is ε-shadowed by a unique true orbit. ANOSOV CLOSING LEMMA: an orbit
    returning close to itself is close to a periodic orbit. Consequences:
    periodic points dense in the non-wandering set Ω (Axiom A); STRUCTURAL
    STABILITY of Anosov diffeomorphisms (C¹-close ⇒ C⁰-conjugate).
    Axiom A + strong transversality ⇔ C¹ structural stability (Robbin,
    Robinson, Mañé). SMALE SPECTRAL DECOMPOSITION: Ω = Ω₁ ⊔ ... ⊔ Ωₖ
    basic sets. MARKOV PARTITIONS code Λ by a subshift of finite type.
  - SYMBOLIC DYNAMICS: full shift σ on {0,...,N−1}^ℤ; SUBSHIFT OF FINITE
    TYPE (SFT) σ_A with 0-1 matrix A: #Fix(σⁿ) = tr Aⁿ, h_top = log ρ(A)
    (Perron eigenvalue), ARTIN-MAZUR ZETA ζ(t) = exp ∑ Nₙtⁿ/n = 1/det(I −
    tA). Prime orbits of length n: (1/n)∑_{d|n} μ(n/d)N_d (Möbius).
    SMALE HORSESHOE: the invariant Cantor set of a horseshoe map is
    conjugate to the full 2-shift (2ⁿ points of period n, entropy log 2).
  - ENTROPY AND EXPONENTS: topological entropy h(fⁿ) = n·h(f); h = 0 for
    isometries and circle homeomorphisms; h = log d for z ↦ zᵈ on S¹
    and for rational maps of degree d on Ĉ (Lyubich); h(A) = ∑_{|λ|>1}
    log|λ| for toral automorphisms. VARIATIONAL PRINCIPLE h_top = sup h_μ.
    LYAPUNOV EXPONENT χ(x) = lim (1/n)log|(fⁿ)′(x)| (Oseledets in higher
    dimension); RUELLE h_μ ≤ ∑χ⁺; PESIN equality for smooth measures; SRB
    measures for Axiom A attractors.
  - ONE-DIMENSIONAL MAPS: doubling E(x) = 2x mod 1 (2ⁿ − 1 points of
    period n, conjugate to the one-sided 2-shift), tent map T, logistic
    Q_r(x) = rx(1 − x). Q₄ is conjugate to T via x = sin²(πy/2), so it has
    2ⁿ points of period n, density 1/(π√(x(1 − x))), exponent log 2.
    PERIOD DOUBLING: fixed point 1 − 1/r attracting for 1 < r < 3 (multi-
    plier 2 − r); 2-cycle attracting for 3 < r < 1 + √6 (multiplier −r² +
    2r + 4); r_∞ ≈ 3.569946; FEIGENBAUM δ = 4.669201..., α = 2.502907...
    (universal for unimodal maps with quadratic maximum); period-3 window
    opens at r = 1 + √8.
  - SHARKOVSKII: 3 ▹ 5 ▹ 7 ▹ ... ▹ 2·3 ▹ 2·5 ▹ ... ▹ 4·3 ▹ ... ▹ 8 ▹ 4 ▹ 2
    ▹ 1; a continuous map of an interval with a point of period p has
    points of every period q with p ▹ q, and this is sharp. "Period 3
    implies chaos" (Li-Yorke). SINGER: negative SCHWARZIAN Sf = f‴/f′ −
    (3/2)(f″/f′)² ⇒ every attracting cycle attracts a critical point or
    an endpoint (SQ_r = −6/(1 − 2x)² < 0: at most one attracting cycle).
  - CIRCLE HOMEOMORPHISMS (orientation preserving, lift F): ROTATION
    NUMBER ρ(f) = lim (Fⁿ(x) − x)/n mod 1, independent of x, conjugacy
    invariant, continuous in f. POINCARÉ: ρ = p/q (lowest terms) ⇔ a
    periodic orbit, and then all periodic orbits have period q; ρ ∉ ℚ ⇒
    f is semi-conjugate to R_ρ. DENJOY: f ∈ C² (or C¹ with Df of bounded
    variation) and ρ ∉ ℚ ⇒ f is conjugate to R_ρ; C¹ counterexamples
    (Denjoy examples with wandering intervals). ARNOLD FAMILY f(x) = x +
    Ω − (K/2π)sin 2πx: ARNOLD TONGUES {ρ = p/q} have interior for K > 0;
    the 0-tongue is |Ω| ≤ K/2π; for K = 1 the tongues have full measure.
  - RATIONAL MAPS f : Ĉ → Ĉ of degree d ≥ 2: d + 1 fixed points (with
    multiplicity), 2d − 2 critical points; a polynomial has dⁿ finite
    solutions of fⁿ(z) = z. MULTIPLIER λ = f′(z₀) (at ∞ use w = 1/z):
    SUPERATTRACTING λ = 0, ATTRACTING 0 < |λ| < 1, REPELLING |λ| > 1,
    PARABOLIC λ = e^{2πip/q}, IRRATIONALLY INDIFFERENT λ = e^{2πiθ}, θ ∉ ℚ
    (SIEGEL point if linearizable, CREMER point if not). HOLOMORPHIC
    INDEX FORMULA: ∑_{fixed} 1/(1 − λ) = 1 (all λ ≠ 1); for a polynomial
    the finite fixed points give ∑ 1/(1 − λ) = 0.
  - LOCAL THEORY: KOENIGS (0 < |λ| < 1: φ∘f = λφ), BÖTTCHER (f = zᵏ +
    ...: φ∘f = φᵏ; for z² + c at ∞, φ(z) = z + c/(2z) + ...), LEAU-FATOU
    FLOWER (f = z + az^{m+1} + ..., a ≠ 0: m attracting petals), SIEGEL
    (θ Diophantine ⇒ linearizable; BRJUNO ∑ log q_{n+1}/q_n < ∞ suffices,
    and is necessary for quadratic polynomials by Yoccoz), CREMER (for
    Liouville-like θ, e^{2πiθ}z + z² is not linearizable).
  - FATOU AND JULIA SETS: F(f) = where the iterates form a NORMAL family
    (Montel), J(f) = Ĉ ∖ F. J is non-empty, compact, perfect, completely
    invariant, J(fⁿ) = J(f), equals the closure of the repelling periodic
    points, and either J = Ĉ or J has empty interior; for z ∈ J
    (non-exceptional) the backward orbit is dense in J. For polynomials
    J = ∂K, K the FILLED Julia set (bounded orbits). Examples: J(z²) =
    S¹, J(z² − 2) = [−2, 2] (Chebyshev), J(1/z²) = S¹, J(z² + ¼) a Jordan
    curve (cauliflower), J(z² − 1) basilica, J(z² + i) a dendrite,
    J(Lattès) = Ĉ, J(Newton map of z² − 1) = iℝ ∪ {∞}.
  - FATOU COMPONENTS: SULLIVAN'S no wandering domains theorem; every
    periodic component is an attracting basin, a parabolic basin, a
    SIEGEL DISC or a HERMAN RING (no Herman rings for polynomials). Each
    attracting or parabolic cycle attracts a critical point; FATOU-
    SHISHIKURA: at most 2d − 2 non-repelling cycles (d − 1 finite ones
    for a polynomial of degree d).
  - QUADRATIC FAMILY f_c(z) = z² + c: ESCAPE: |z| > max(2, |c|) ⇒ fⁿ(z)
    → ∞. K_c connected ⇔ 0 ∈ K_c; otherwise K_c = J_c is a Cantor set.
    MANDELBROT SET M = {c : fⁿ_c(0) bounded} ⊆ {|c| ≤ 2}, M ∩ ℝ = [−2, ¼];
    M is compact, connected, full (Douady-Hubbard: Ĉ ∖ M ≅ Ĉ ∖ D̄ via the
    Böttcher map); ∂M has Hausdorff dimension 2 (Shishikura); MLC is open.
    MAIN CARDIOID c = λ/2 − λ²/4 (|λ| < 1): attracting fixed point; the
    period-2 disc |c + 1| < ¼ (2-cycle multiplier 4(c + 1)); the p/q-bulb
    is attached at λ = e^{2πip/q}. Centres of period n: roots of fⁿ_c(0)
    = 0; exact period n: 1, 1, 3, 6, 15, 27, 63 for n = 1, ..., 7; period 3:
    c³ + 2c² + c + 1 = 0 (c ≈ −1.7549, −0.1226 ± 0.7449i: airplane,
    rabbits). Logistic ↔ quadratic: c = (r/2)(1 − r/2).
  - NEWTON'S METHOD N_p = z − p/p′: roots are superattracting fixed
    points, ∞ is repelling with multiplier d/(d − 1); N′ = pp″/p′².
  - HAMILTONIAN SYSTEMS: q̇ = ∂H/∂p, ṗ = −∂H/∂q preserve ω = ∑dqᵢ ∧ dpᵢ
    (Liouville volume). LIOUVILLE-ARNOLD: n independent integrals in
    involution, compact connected level set ⇒ an invariant n-torus with
    ACTION-ANGLE coordinates (I, θ), I = (1/2π)∮p dq, θ̇ = ω(I) = ∂H₀/∂I.
    Tori are RESONANT if ⟨k, ω⟩ = 0 for some k ∈ ℤⁿ ∖ 0. NONDEGENERACY
    det ∂²H₀/∂I² ≠ 0 (KOLMOGOROV) or the ISOENERGETIC (Arnold) condition
    det[[H₀″, H₀′],[H₀′ᵀ, 0]] ≠ 0.
  - SMALL DIVISORS: the cohomological equation u(θ + α) − u(θ) = g(θ)
    gives ûₖ = ĝₖ/(e^{2πikα} − 1); needs DIOPHANTINE α: |qα − p| ≥ γ/q^τ.
    DIOPHANTINE VECTORS |⟨k, ω⟩| ≥ γ|k|^{−τ} (τ > n − 1) have full
    measure as γ → 0; on the circle the excluded set has measure ≤
    2γζ(τ) (τ > 1). Badly approximable numbers = bounded partial
    quotients (golden mean [0; 1, 1, 1, ...], the "most irrational").
  - KAM THEOREM (Kolmogorov 1954, Arnold, Moser): H = H₀(I) + εH₁(I, θ)
    real-analytic, H₀ nondegenerate ⇒ for ε small, all tori with
    Diophantine ω(I) (fixed γ, τ) persist as deformed invariant tori
    carrying quasi-periodic motion; the destroyed set has measure
    O(√ε). The union of KAM tori is a nowhere dense set of large
    measure. ARNOLD: analytic circle diffeomorphisms close to a
    Diophantine rotation with the same ρ are analytically conjugate to
    it. Consequences: n = 2 degrees of freedom ⇒ 2-tori separate the
    3-dimensional energy surface ⇒ perpetual stability of actions;
    n ≥ 3 ⇒ ARNOLD DIFFUSION possible; NEKHOROSHEV: |I(t) − I(0)| ≤ εᵃ
    for |t| ≤ exp(ε^{−b}) (steep, e.g. convex, H₀).
  - TWIST MAPS: area-preserving (θ, r) ↦ (θ + α(r), r) with α′ ≠ 0.
    MOSER TWIST THEOREM: a small smooth area-preserving perturbation keeps
    invariant curves with Diophantine rotation numbers. POINCARÉ-
    BIRKHOFF: an area-preserving homeomorphism of an annulus twisting the
    two boundaries in opposite directions has ≥ 2 fixed points; resonant
    circles ρ = p/q break into chains of ≥ 2 period-q orbits (elliptic
    and hyperbolic). AUBRY-MATHER: every rotation number in the twist
    interval is realized by an invariant circle or a CANTORUS. CHIRIKOV
    STANDARD MAP p′ = p + K sin θ, θ′ = θ + p′ (det = 1): GREENE RESIDUE
    R = (2 − tr DF^q)/4 (stable ⇔ 0 < R < 1); the golden invariant circle
    is the last to break, at K ≈ 0.971635; no rotational invariant
    circles for K > 4/3 (Mather), K > 63/64 (MacKay-Percival).

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. (a) State the Hartman-Grobman theorem and the stable manifold
    theorem, and explain why the conjugacy cannot be C¹ in general.
    (b) Find the global stable and unstable manifolds of the origin
    exactly for the flow ẋ = −x, ẏ = y + x², and for the map F(x, y) =
    (x/2, 2y − 7x²/4). (c) For the Hénon map H(x, y) = (1 − ax² + y, bx)
    with a = 1.4, b = 0.3 find both fixed points and classify them.
    Answer: (a) see below. (b) Flow: Wˢ = {y = −x²/3}, Wᵘ = {x = 0}. Map:
    Wˢ = {y = x²}, Wᵘ = {x = 0}. (c) P₊ ≈ (0.6314, 0.1894), eigenvalues
    ≈ −1.9237, 0.1559; P₋ ≈ (−1.1314, −0.3394), eigenvalues ≈ 3.2598,
    −0.0920; both are saddles (dim Eˢ = dim Eᵘ = 1).
    Teacher explains: (a) HARTMAN-GROBMAN: if p is a hyperbolic fixed
    point of a C¹ diffeomorphism f, there are neighbourhoods U ∋ p, V ∋ 0
    and a homeomorphism h : U → V with h∘f = Df(p)∘h (where defined); for
    flows, h maps orbits of X to orbits of ẋ = DX(p)x preserving time.
    STABLE MANIFOLD THEOREM: Wˢ_loc(p) = {x ∈ U : fⁿ(x) ∈ U ∀n ≥ 0} is a
    Cᵏ embedded disc tangent to Eˢ at p (f ∈ Cᵏ) and fⁿ → p on it
    exponentially fast. A C¹ conjugacy would preserve eigenvalues of all
    fixed points (Dh·Df = L·Dh), so e.g. x ↦ 2x and x ↦ 3x on ℝ, which are
    C⁰-conjugate by h(x) = sign(x)|x|^{log 3/log 2}, are not C¹-conjugate;
    h is not differentiable at 0. Smooth linearization needs NON-RESONANCE
    (Sternberg).
    (b) Flow: x = x₀e^{−t}, and ẏ − y = x₀²e^{−2t} has particular solution
    Ae^{−2t} with −2A − A = x₀², A = −x₀²/3. Hence
       y(t) = (y₀ + x₀²/3)eᵗ − (x₀²/3)e^{−2t}.
    As t → +∞ the orbit stays bounded iff y₀ = −x₀²/3: Wˢ = {y = −x²/3},
    tangent to Eˢ = x-axis. As t → −∞, x = x₀e^{−t} blows up unless x₀ =
    0, so Wᵘ = {x = 0} = Eᵘ (straight). Check invariance: d/dt(y + x²/3)
    = y + x² − (2/3)x² = y + x²/3, so {y + x²/3 = 0} is invariant.
    Map: try Wˢ = {y = cx²}: F(x, cx²) = (x/2, (2c − 7/4)x²) must equal
    (x/2, c(x/2)²), so 2c − 7/4 = c/4, c = 1. Moreover y − x² ↦ (2y −
    7x²/4) − x²/4 = 2(y − x²): off the parabola the defect doubles, so
    Wˢ = {y = x²} globally, and F(0, y) = (0, 2y) gives Wᵘ = {x = 0}.
    (c) Fixed points: y = bx and x = 1 − ax² + bx, i.e. ax² + (1 − b)x − 1
    = 0, x = (−0.7 ± √(0.49 + 5.6))/2.8 = (−0.7 ± 2.46779)/2.8, so x₊ =
    0.63135, x₋ = −1.13135 and y = 0.3x. DH = [[−2ax, 1], [b, 0]] has
    characteristic polynomial λ² + 2axλ − b = 0 (product of eigenvalues
    det DH = −b = −0.3 everywhere: H is dissipative). At x₊: 2ax =
    1.76779, λ = (−1.76779 ± √(3.12507 + 1.2))/2 = −1.9237, 0.1559. At
    x₋: 2ax = −3.16779, λ = (3.16779 ± √(10.0349 + 1.2))/2 = 3.2598,
    −0.0920. Both saddles; the Hénon attractor is the closure of Wᵘ(P₊).
    Common mistakes: using the linear stable space Eˢ as if it were the
    stable manifold (it is only tangent); forgetting that for maps the
    unit CIRCLE, not the imaginary axis, is the critical set; dropping
    the "local" in Hartman-Grobman.
    Now you try: find Wᵘ of the origin for F(x, y) = (2x, y/2 + x²).
    (Wᵘ = {y = 2x²/7}; Wˢ = {x = 0})

D2. Let A = [[2, 1], [1, 1]] act on T² = ℝ²/ℤ² (the CAT MAP). (a) Show
    that A is an Anosov diffeomorphism; find its eigenvalues, eigen-
    directions and topological entropy, and explain why the stable and
    unstable manifolds are dense lines. (b) Prove #Fix(Aⁿ) = |det(Aⁿ −
    I)| and compute it for n = 1, ..., 5; deduce the number of periodic
    orbits of least period 2, 3, 4. (c) List the period-2 points.
    Answer: (a) λ = (3 ± √5)/2 = φ^{±2}, directions (φ, 1) and (1, −φ),
    h = log φ² ≈ 0.9624. (b) 1, 5, 16, 45, 121; 2, 5, 10 orbits.
    (c) (0, 0) and the 2-cycles {(1/5, 2/5), (4/5, 3/5)}, {(2/5, 4/5),
    (3/5, 1/5)}.
    Teacher explains: (a) A ∈ SL(2, ℤ) preserves ℤ², so it descends to an
    automorphism of T² with inverse A⁻¹ = [[1, −1], [−1, 2]]. χ(λ) = λ² −
    3λ + 1, λ = (3 ± √5)/2 ≈ 2.618, 0.382. With φ = (1 + √5)/2: φ² = φ +
    1 = (3 + √5)/2, and A(φ, 1) = (2φ + 1, φ + 1) = (φ³, φ²) = φ²(φ, 1).
    The splitting TₓT² = Eᵘ ⊕ Eˢ is constant, Dfⁿ = Aⁿ expands Eᵘ by φ²ⁿ
    and contracts Eˢ by φ^{−2n}: the whole torus is hyperbolic. Wᵘ(x) is
    the projection of the line x + t(φ, 1), whose slope 1/φ is irrational,
    so it winds densely around T² (Kronecker); likewise Wˢ. The entropy
    of a toral automorphism is ∑_{|λ|>1} log|λ| = log φ² = 2 log φ ≈
    0.9624.
    (b) x ∈ T² is fixed by Aⁿ iff (Aⁿ − I)x̃ ∈ ℤ² for a lift x̃. Since 1 is
    not an eigenvalue, Aⁿ − I is invertible and Fix(Aⁿ) ≅ (Aⁿ − I)⁻¹ℤ²/ℤ²
    ≅ ℤ²/(Aⁿ − I)ℤ², a group of order |det(Aⁿ − I)|. With eigenvalues
    λⁿ, λ⁻ⁿ: |det(Aⁿ − I)| = |(λⁿ − 1)(λ⁻ⁿ − 1)| = λⁿ + λ⁻ⁿ − 2 = tr Aⁿ −
    2. Traces: tr Aⁿ = 3, 7, 18, 47, 123 (recursion tₙ = 3tₙ₋₁ − tₙ₋₂),
    so N₁,...,N₅ = 1, 5, 16, 45, 121. Least period 2: (5 − 1)/2 = 2 orbits;
    3: (16 − 1)/3 = 5 orbits; 4: (45 − 5)/4 = 10 orbits (subtract the
    points of period dividing n properly).
    (c) A² = [[5, 3], [3, 2]], A² − I = [[4, 3], [3, 1]], det = −5. The
    solutions of (A² − I)x ∈ ℤ² are the multiples k(1/5, 2/5): indeed
    (4/5 + 6/5, 3/5 + 2/5) = (2, 1). A(1/5, 2/5) = (4/5, 3/5) and A(4/5,
    3/5) = (11/5, 7/5) ≡ (1/5, 2/5); A(2/5, 4/5) = (8/5, 6/5) ≡ (3/5, 1/5).
    Common mistakes: counting tr Aⁿ instead of tr Aⁿ − 2; forgetting that
    orbits of least period n contain n points; claiming [[1, 1], [0, 1]]
    is Anosov (eigenvalue 1, entropy 0).
    Now you try: for B = [[3, 1], [2, 1]] find #Fix(Bⁿ), n = 1, 2, 3, and
    h(B). (2, 12, 50; log(2 + √3) ≈ 1.3170)

D3. (a) Describe the Smale horseshoe and explain why its invariant set
    is conjugate to the full 2-shift. (b) For a subshift of finite type
    σ_A prove #Fix(σⁿ_A) = tr Aⁿ and ζ(t) = 1/det(I − tA). (c) For the
    GOLDEN MEAN SHIFT (no two consecutive 1s) compute N₁, ..., N₆, the
    numbers of prime orbits, the entropy and ζ. (d) Same entropy question
    for the shift of 0-1 sequences with no block 111.
    Answer: (c) Nₙ = 1, 3, 4, 7, 11, 18 (Lucas numbers); prime orbits 1,
    1, 1, 1, 2, 2; h = log φ ≈ 0.4812; ζ(t) = 1/(1 − t − t²). (d) h = log
    β, β ≈ 1.8393 the real root of β³ = β² + β + 1, h ≈ 0.6094.
    Teacher explains: (a) f maps the square Q = [0, 1]² affinely on two
    horizontal strips H₀, H₁ to two vertical strips V₀, V₁ (contract
    horizontally by < ½, stretch vertically by > 2, fold into a U).
    Λ = ⋂_{n∈ℤ} fⁿ(Q). The itinerary x ↦ (sₙ), sₙ = i if fⁿ(x) ∈ Hᵢ, is a
    bijection Λ → {0, 1}^ℤ: a finite word s₋ₘ...sₘ determines a small
    rectangle whose sides shrink geometrically (hyperbolicity), so each
    two-sided sequence gives exactly one point; continuity is clear and
    h∘f = σ∘h. So f|Λ has 2ⁿ points of period n and entropy log 2, and Λ
    is a Cantor set.
    (b) A periodic point of period n in σ_A is a closed path of length n
    in the graph of A: x₀x₁...x_{n−1}x₀ with A_{xᵢxᵢ₊₁} = 1. The number of
    closed paths starting at i is (Aⁿ)ᵢᵢ, so Nₙ = tr Aⁿ = ∑λⱼⁿ. Then
    ∑Nₙtⁿ/n = ∑ⱼ∑ₙ(λⱼt)ⁿ/n = −∑ⱼlog(1 − λⱼt), so ζ(t) = ∏(1 − λⱼt)⁻¹ =
    1/det(I − tA). The growth rate of admissible words is the Perron
    eigenvalue, whence h = log ρ(A).
    (c) A = [[1, 1], [1, 0]], eigenvalues φ, −1/φ, Nₙ = φⁿ + (−1/φ)ⁿ = Lₙ:
    1, 3, 4, 7, 11, 18. Prime orbits Pₙ = (1/n)∑_{d|n}μ(n/d)N_d: P₁ = 1
    (0^∞), P₂ = (3 − 1)/2 = 1 ((01)^∞), P₃ = (4 − 1)/3 = 1 (001), P₄ =
    (7 − 3)/4 = 1 (0001), P₅ = (11 − 1)/5 = 2 (00001, 00101), P₆ = (18 −
    4 − 3 + 1)/6 = 2. det(I − tA) = det[[1 − t, −t], [−t, 1]] = 1 − t − t².
    (d) Use states = last two symbols {00, 01, 10, 11}, forbidding 11 → 11.
    The 4 × 4 matrix has χ(λ) = λ⁴ − λ³ − λ² − λ = λ(λ³ − λ² − λ − 1),
    Perron root the tribonacci constant β ≈ 1.83929, h = log β ≈ 0.6094;
    traces 1, 3, 7, 11, 21, ... (e.g. N₄ = 16 − 5 cyclic words containing
    111).
    Common mistakes: counting all words of length n instead of closed
    (cyclic) words; forgetting μ(n/d) signs; using the one-sided count
    2ⁿ − 1 (doubling map) for the two-sided shift, which has 2ⁿ.
    Now you try: prime orbits of length 1, ..., 6 of the full 2-shift.
    (2, 1, 2, 3, 6, 9)

D4. (a) Prove that the logistic map Q(x) = 4x(1 − x) on [0, 1] is
    conjugate to the tent map T(y) = 1 − |1 − 2y| by x = h(y) =
    sin²(πy/2), and deduce: the number of points of period n, the
    invariant density of Q and its Lyapunov exponent. (b) Find the
    period-2 orbit of Q exactly in two ways. (c) Show that the Gauss map
    G(x) = 1/x mod 1 with invariant density 1/((1 + x) ln 2) has Lyapunov
    exponent π²/(6 ln 2).
    Answer: (a) 2ⁿ, ρ(x) = 1/(π√(x(1 − x))), χ = ln 2. (b) {(5 − √5)/8,
    (5 + √5)/8}. (c) ≈ 2.3731.
    Teacher explains: (a) For y ≤ ½, T(y) = 2y and h(2y) = sin²(πy); for
    y ≥ ½, h(2 − 2y) = sin²(π − πy) = sin²(πy). On the other side Q(h(y))
    = 4 sin²(πy/2)cos²(πy/2) = sin²(πy). So Q∘h = h∘T with h an
    increasing homeomorphism of [0, 1]. Tⁿ is piecewise linear with 2ⁿ
    full branches of slope ±2ⁿ, each crossing the diagonal once: 2ⁿ fixed
    points of Tⁿ, hence of Qⁿ. Lebesgue measure is T-invariant; pushing
    it forward, dx = (π/2)sin(πy)dy = π√(x(1 − x))dy, so Q preserves
    dx/(π√(x(1 − x))). |T′| = 2, and h is a diffeomorphism on (0, 1), so
    (1/n)log|(Qⁿ)′(x)| → log 2 for a.e. x (the conjugacy factors are
    bounded along typical orbits). Check with the ergodic theorem:
    ∫₀¹ ln|4 − 8x| dx/(π√(x(1 − x))) = ln 2.
    (b) Directly: Q²(x) = x without the fixed points 0, ¾ gives 16x² −
    20x + 5 = 0, x = (5 ± √5)/8 ≈ 0.3455, 0.9045. Via T: the 2-cycle of T
    is {2/5, 4/5}, and h(2/5) = sin²(π/5) = (5 − √5)/8, h(4/5) =
    sin²(2π/5) = (5 + √5)/8. Multiplier (4 − 8x₁)(4 − 8x₂) = (−1 + √5)
    (−1 − √5) = −4, |·| = 2² as predicted.
    (c) G′(x) = −1/x², so χ = ∫₀¹ (−2 ln x) dx/((1 + x)ln 2). Expanding
    1/(1 + x) = ∑(−1)ᵏxᵏ and ∫₀¹ xᵏ ln x dx = −1/(k + 1)², ∫₀¹ ln x/(1 +
    x) dx = −∑(−1)ᵏ/(k + 1)² = −π²/12. So χ = (2/ln 2)(π²/12) =
    π²/(6 ln 2) ≈ 2.3731 (Lévy's constant is half of this).
    Common mistakes: forgetting the second branch when checking the
    conjugacy; taking χ = ln 4 from sup|Q′| = 4 (the exponent is an
    average, not a maximum); counting 2ⁿ fixed points for the doubling
    map E(x) = 2x mod 1 (there are 2ⁿ − 1: 0 and 1 coincide on S¹).
    Now you try: Lyapunov exponent and number of period-n points of the
    tripling map x ↦ 3x mod 1. (ln 3; 3ⁿ − 1)

D5. For the logistic family Q_r(x) = rx(1 − x): (a) show that the
    fixed point x* = 1 − 1/r is attracting for 1 < r < 3, find the
    2-cycle and prove its multiplier is −r² + 2r + 4; find where it is
    attracting and superattracting. (b) Given r₁ = 3, r₂ = 1 + √6, r₃ ≈
    3.544090 (onset of periods 2, 4, 8) estimate δ and r_∞. (c) State
    Sharkovskii's theorem and decide which periods are forced by period
    5, by period 6 and by period 12. (d) Why can Q_r have at most one
    attracting cycle?
    Answer: (a) 2-cycle x = ((r + 1) ± √((r + 1)(r − 3)))/(2r), attracting
    for 3 < r < 1 + √6 ≈ 3.4495, superattracting at r = 1 + √5. (b) δ ≈
    4.75, r_∞ ≈ 3.5699 (true 3.569946). (c) 5: every period except 3;
    6: every even period and 1; 12: 1, 2, 4, 8 and all 2ᵏ·m with k ≥ 3
    or (k = 2 and m odd ≥ 3). (d) Negative Schwarzian (Singer).
    Teacher explains: (a) Q_r′(x*) = r(1 − 2x*) = r(−1 + 2/r) = 2 − r;
    |2 − r| < 1 ⇔ 1 < r < 3, and at r = 3 the multiplier passes −1: a
    period-doubling bifurcation. Q²(x) − x is divisible by Q(x) − x;
    the quotient is r²x² − r(r + 1)x + (r + 1), with roots as stated
    (real iff r ≥ 3). For the cycle, x₁ + x₂ = (r + 1)/r, x₁x₂ = (r +
    1)/r², so the multiplier
       Q′(x₁)Q′(x₂) = r²(1 − 2(x₁ + x₂) + 4x₁x₂)
                    = r² − 2r(r + 1) + 4(r + 1) = −r² + 2r + 4.
    It equals 1 at r = 3 (r² − 2r − 3 = 0), −1 at r² − 2r − 5 = 0, r = 1 +
    √6, and 0 at r² − 2r − 4 = 0, r = 1 + √5 ≈ 3.2361 (then ½ lies on the
    cycle: r³ − 4r² + 8 = (r − 2)(r² − 2r − 4)).
    (b) δ₁ ≈ (r₂ − r₁)/(r₃ − r₂) = 0.449490/0.094600 ≈ 4.75, close to
    FEIGENBAUM'S δ = 4.6692. Geometric extrapolation with δ: r_∞ ≈ r₃ +
    (r₃ − r₂)/(δ − 1) = 3.544090 + 0.094600/3.669201 ≈ 3.56987.
    (c) SHARKOVSKII: for continuous f : I → I on an interval, period p ⇒
    period q whenever p ▹ q in 3 ▹ 5 ▹ 7 ▹ ... ▹ 2·3 ▹ 2·5 ▹ ... ▹ 2²·3 ▹
    ... ▹ 2³ ▹ 2² ▹ 2 ▹ 1, and for every p there is a map with period p
    but no period preceding p. Period 5: all odd ≥ 5 and everything
    after, so all periods except 3. Period 6 = 2·3: 2·5, 2·7, ..., all
    4·odd, 8·odd, ..., and all powers of 2: every even number and 1.
    Period 12 = 2²·3: 2²·m (m odd ≥ 3), all 2ᵏ·m (k ≥ 3, m odd ≥ 3), all
    powers of 2.
    (d) SQ_r = f‴/f′ − (3/2)(f″/f′)² = 0 − (3/2)(4r²)/(r²(1 − 2x)²) =
    −6/(1 − 2x)² < 0. By SINGER, the immediate basin of each attracting
    cycle contains the critical point ½ or an endpoint; 0 is attracting
    only for r < 1, and ½ can be in only one basin.
    Common mistakes: writing the Sharkovskii order backwards (3 is the
    STRONGEST); forgetting that the theorem is for intervals (a rotation
    of the circle by 1/3 has only period 3); confusing the onset of
    period 4 (r₂) with the superstable value 1 + √5.
    Now you try: at which r does Q_r have a 2-cycle of multiplier −½?
    (r² − 2r − 4.5 = 0: r = 1 + √5.5 ≈ 3.3452)

D6. (a) Define the rotation number of an orientation-preserving circle
    homeomorphism and prove that ρ(f) = p/q in lowest terms iff f has a
    periodic orbit (of period q). (b) Compute ρ for f(x) = x + ⅓ +
    sin(6πx)/(10π) and for the Arnold map F(x) = x + ½ − (K/2π)sin 2πx
    (0 ≤ K ≤ 1). (c) For the Arnold family F(x) = x + Ω − (K/2π)sin 2πx
    find the 0-tongue and its width at K = ½ and K = 1. (d) State
    Denjoy's theorem and give the first five convergents of √2 − 1.
    Answer: (b) ⅓ and ½. (c) |Ω| ≤ K/2π; widths 1/(2π) ≈ 0.159 and 1/π ≈
    0.318. (d) C² (or Df ∈ BV) and ρ ∉ ℚ ⇒ conjugate to R_ρ; 1/2, 2/5,
    5/12, 12/29, 29/70.
    Teacher explains: (a) For a lift F (F(x + 1) = F(x) + 1), ρ(F) = lim
    (Fⁿ(x) − x)/n exists and is independent of x: aₙ = Fⁿ(0) is
    subadditive up to 1 (Fᵐ⁺ⁿ(0) ≤ Fᵐ(0) + Fⁿ(0) + 1) and |Fⁿ(x) − x −
    Fⁿ(0)| < 1. If f^q(x) = x for q minimal then F^q(x̃) = x̃ + p, so ρ =
    p/q. Conversely if F^q(x) − x − p had no zero it would have constant
    sign, say ≥ ε > 0 by compactness, giving ρ ≥ (p + ε)/q. All periodic
    orbits then share the combinatorics of R_{p/q}, so have period q.
    (b) f: 0 ↦ ⅓ ↦ ⅔ ↦ 1 because sin(6πx) = 0 at these points, so F³(0) =
    0 + 1 and ρ = ⅓ (F′ = 1 + 0.6 cos 6πx > 0, a diffeomorphism). Arnold
    map with Ω = ½: let h(x) = −x; then hFh⁻¹(x) = −F(−x) = x − ½ −
    (K/2π)sin 2πx = F(x) − 1. An orientation-REVERSING conjugacy negates
    ρ, so −ρ ≡ ρ − 1, ρ = ½ (a period-2 orbit exists for every K).
    (c) ρ = 0 ⇔ F(x) = x has a solution ⇔ Ω = (K/2π)sin 2πx for some x ⇔
    |Ω| ≤ K/(2π). K = ½: width 2·(1/(4π)) = 1/(2π) ≈ 0.159; K = 1: 1/π.
    For K < 1 each rational tongue has non-empty interior while ρ(Ω)
    is a devil's staircase; for K = 1 the tongues fill a set of full
    measure in Ω.
    (d) DENJOY: if f is a C² diffeomorphism (C¹ with Df of bounded
    variation suffices) and ρ ∉ ℚ, then f is minimal and topologically
    conjugate to R_ρ. Denjoy's C¹ examples have a wandering interval and
    a Cantor minimal set. √2 − 1 = [0; 2, 2, 2, ...]: pₙ/qₙ with qₙ₊₁ =
    2qₙ + qₙ₋₁: 1/2, 2/5, 5/12, 12/29, 29/70. Smooth conjugacy (Herman,
    Yoccoz) holds for Diophantine ρ such as √2 − 1.
    Common mistakes: claiming ρ = Ω (only true for K = 0); assuming a
    homeomorphism with irrational ρ is always conjugate to a rotation
    (Denjoy counterexamples); forgetting that ρ depends on the lift only
    mod 1.
    Now you try: if ρ(f) = 2/5, find ρ(f³) and ρ(f⁻¹). (1/5; 3/5)

D7. (a) Prove the holomorphic index formula ∑ 1/(1 − λ) = 0 over the
    finite fixed points of a polynomial of degree d ≥ 2 (all λ ≠ 1), check
    it for z² + c and z³, and deduce that every such polynomial has a
    repelling fixed point or a multiple fixed point. (b) Find and
    classify the fixed points of λz + z², z + z², z + z³ and 1/z²,
    including the attracting directions of the parabolic ones. (c) Find
    the Koenigs coordinate of f(z) = z/2 + z² to second order and the
    Böttcher coordinate of z² + c at ∞ to order 1/z.
    Answer: (b) λz + z²: 0 (mult. λ), 1 − λ (mult. 2 − λ); z + z²: 0
    parabolic, one petal, attracting direction ℝ₋; z + z³: 0 parabolic,
    two petals, directions ±i; 1/z²: the cube roots of 1, all repelling
    with λ = −2. (c) φ(z) = z + 4z² + O(z³); φ(z) = z + c/(2z) + O(z⁻³).
    Teacher explains: (a) Put g(z) = 1/(z − f(z)). At a simple fixed point
    z₀, z − f(z) = (1 − λ)(z − z₀) + ..., so Res_{z₀} g = 1/(1 − λ). Since
    deg f ≥ 2, |g(z)| = O(|z|^{−2}) at ∞, so ∮_{|z|=R} g dz → 0 and the
    residues sum to 0. (On Ĉ one adds the index 1/(1 − 0) = 1 of the
    superattracting point ∞, giving the general formula ∑ = 1.) For z² +
    c the multipliers are λ± = 1 ± √(1 − 4c), λ₊ + λ₋ = 2, so 1/(1 − λ₊)
    + 1/(1 − λ₋) = (2 − λ₊ − λ₋)/((1 − λ₊)(1 − λ₋)) = 0. For z³: fixed
    points 0, ±1 with λ = 0, 3, 3: 1 − ½ − ½ = 0. If all |λ| ≤ 1 and λ ≠
    1 then Re 1/(1 − λ) ≥ ½ (the map λ ↦ 1/(1 − λ) sends the unit disc
    onto the half-plane Re w > ½), so the sum has real part ≥ d/2 > 0, a
    contradiction: some fixed point is repelling or has λ = 1.
    (b) λz + z² = z ⇔ z(z + λ − 1) = 0; f′(1 − λ) = λ + 2(1 − λ) = 2 − λ
    (and 1/(1 − λ) + 1/(λ − 1) = 0 ✓). LEAU-FATOU: f(z) = z + az^{m+1} +
    ... has m attracting directions v with av^m < 0. z + z²: m = 1, v <
    0; indeed w = −1/z turns f into w ↦ w + 1 + O(1/w), so zₙ ≈ −1/n.
    z + z³: m = 2, v² < 0, v = ±i (and repelling directions ±1). For
    1/z²: z³ = 1 and f′(z) = −2/z³ = −2; index 3·(1/3) = 1 on Ĉ ✓ (0 and
    ∞ form a superattracting 2-cycle, not fixed points).
    (c) KOENIGS: seek φ = z + az² + ... with φ∘f = ½φ. φ(f(z)) = z/2 + z²
    + a z²/4 + O(z³) and ½φ(z) = z/2 + (a/2)z² give 1 + a/4 = a/2, a = 4.
    BÖTTCHER: φ(f(z)) = φ(z)² with φ = z + b/z + ...: φ(z² + c) = z² + c +
    O(z⁻²) and φ(z)² = z² + 2b + O(z⁻²), so b = c/2. The Green function
    G_c(z) = log|φ_c(z)| = log|z| + O(1/|z|²) and φ_c extends to the
    basin of ∞ when c ∈ M.
    Common mistakes: forgetting ∞ in counts on Ĉ (a degree-d rational map
    has d + 1 fixed points, a polynomial d finite ones plus ∞); taking the
    attracting direction of z + z² to be ℝ₊ (there orbits leave 0); using
    the Koenigs recursion when λ = 0 (use Böttcher instead).
    Now you try: attracting directions of z − z⁴ and the multipliers of
    the fixed points of z² − ¾. (1, e^{±2πi/3}; 3 at 3/2 and −1 at −½)

D8. (a) Define the Fatou and Julia sets and prove J(z²) = S¹. List the
    main properties of J. (b) Prove J(z² − 2) = [−2, 2] and describe its
    dense set of repelling periodic points. (c) Prove the escape
    criterion for f_c(z) = z² + c: if |z| > max(2, |c|) then fⁿ(z) → ∞.
    For c = −3 show that 0 escapes and find the fixed points with their
    multipliers. What is J then?
    Answer: (b) J = [−2, 2]; periodic points 2cos(2πk/(2ⁿ ± 1)). (c) 0 ↦
    −3 ↦ 6 ↦ 33 ↦ ...; fixed points (1 ± √13)/2 ≈ 2.3028, −1.3028 with
    λ = 1 ± √13 ≈ 4.6056, −2.6056; J = K is a Cantor set.
    Teacher explains: (a) F(f) = {z : {fⁿ} is a normal family on some
    neighbourhood of z} (convergence locally uniformly in the spherical
    metric, ∞ allowed), J = Ĉ ∖ F. For f = z²: on |z| < 1, fⁿ → 0 locally
    uniformly; on |z| > 1, fⁿ → ∞; so both are in F. At |z₀| = 1 every
    neighbourhood contains points going to 0 and to ∞, while |fⁿ(z₀)| =
    1: no subsequence can converge to a continuous limit (it would jump),
    so z₀ ∈ J. Properties: J ≠ ∅ compact, completely invariant, perfect,
    J(fⁿ) = J(f), J = closure of the repelling periodic points, J = Ĉ or
    int J = ∅, backward orbits of non-exceptional points accumulate on
    all of J, and fⁿ(U) ⊇ J for large n for any open U meeting J
    (MONTEL: a family omitting 3 values is normal).
    (b) If z ∉ [−2, 2], write z = w + 1/w with |w| > 1 (the Joukowski map
    is a bijection from {|w| > 1} to ℂ ∖ [−2, 2]). Then f(z) = w² + 2 +
    w⁻² − 2 = w² + w⁻², so fⁿ(z) = w^{2ⁿ} + w^{−2ⁿ} → ∞. If z = 2cos θ ∈
    [−2, 2] then f(z) = 4cos²θ − 2 = 2cos 2θ stays in [−2, 2]. So K =
    [−2, 2] has no interior in ℂ and J = ∂K = [−2, 2]. f is semiconjugate
    to θ ↦ 2θ; period-n points 2cos θ with 2ⁿθ ≡ ±θ (mod 2π), θ =
    2πk/(2ⁿ ∓ 1). Fixed points 2 (λ = 4) and −1 (λ = −2): both repelling.
    (c) If |z| > max(2, |c|), write |z| = 2 + ε. Then |z² + c| ≥ |z|² −
    |c| > |z|² − |z| = |z|(|z| − 1) ≥ (1 + ε)|z|, and the new point still
    satisfies the hypothesis; so |fⁿ(z)| ≥ (1 + ε)ⁿ|z| → ∞. For c = −3:
    0 ↦ −3 ↦ 6 ↦ 33, and |6| > 3 already. Fixed points solve z² − z − 3 =
    0: z = (1 ± √13)/2, λ = 2z = 1 ± √13. When the critical point escapes,
    K_c is a Cantor set (the two inverse branches on a disc containing
    K are contractions, an iterated function system), so J_c = K_c is
    totally disconnected. For |c| > 2 in general, |f(c)| ≥ |c|(|c| − 1)
    > |c|, so 0 always escapes and J_c is a Cantor set.
    Common mistakes: claiming J is where the orbit is "chaotic" without
    the normal-family definition; forgetting ∞ in the spherical metric;
    using the escape radius 2 when |c| > 2 (then one needs |z| > |c|).
    Now you try: show J(4z³ − 3z) = [−1, 1] using z = cos θ.
    (T₃(cos θ) = cos 3θ; outside, z = (w + 1/w)/2 gives w ↦ w³)

D9. (a) Show that the parameters c for which z² + c has an attracting
    fixed point form the MAIN CARDIOID c = λ/2 − λ²/4, |λ| < 1, and those
    with an attracting 2-cycle form the disc |c + 1| < ¼. Find the root
    points c for λ = −1, i, e^{2πi/3}. (b) Prove M ∩ ℝ = [−2, ¼].
    (c) Find the centres of period 3 and count the centres of exact
    period n = 1, ..., 6. (d) Decide whether c = 1, −2, i, 0.2,
    −0.5 + 0.5i, −0.75 + 0.1i, −1.2 are in M.
    Answer: (a) −¾, ¼ + ½i, −⅛ + (3√3/8)i. (c) c(c³ + 2c² + c + 1) = 0:
    c ≈ −1.7549, −0.1226 ± 0.7449i; counts 1, 1, 3, 6, 15, 27. (d) 1 ∉ M;
    −2 ∈ M; i ∈ M; 0.2 ∈ M; −0.5 + 0.5i ∈ M; −0.75 + 0.1i ∉ M; −1.2 ∈ M.
    Teacher explains: (a) A fixed point z has multiplier λ = 2z and c = z
    − z² = λ/2 − λ²/4. Attracting ⇔ |λ| < 1. λ = −1: c = −½ − ¼ = −¾; λ =
    i: c = i/2 + ¼; λ = e^{2πi/3} = −½ + (√3/2)i: λ² = −½ − (√3/2)i, c =
    −¼ + (√3/4)i + ⅛ + (√3/8)i = −⅛ + (3√3/8)i ≈ −0.125 + 0.6495i (the root
    of the ⅓-bulb, where the rabbits live). Period 2: (f²(z) − z)/(f(z) −
    z) = z² + z + c + 1, product of the two points z₁z₂ = c + 1, so the
    multiplier is 4z₁z₂ = 4(c + 1), attracting ⇔ |c + 1| < ¼. The two
    components touch at c = −¾, where λ = −1 on one side and the 2-cycle
    multiplier is 1 on the other.
    (b) c > ¼: z² + c − z = (z − ½)² + c − ¼ > 0, so fⁿ(0) increases to
    ∞. c < −2: f(c) = c² + c > −c = |c| > 2, then escape. For c ∈ [−2, ¼]
    put β = (1 + √(1 − 4c))/2 (the larger fixed point); f maps [−β, β]
    into [c, β], and c ≥ −β (equivalent to c(c + 2) ≤ 0 for c ≤ −½, and
    obvious for c > −½), so 0 has a bounded orbit.
    (c) f³_c(0) = (c² + c)² + c = c(c³ + 2c² + c + 1). The cubic has one
    real root −1.75488 (AIRPLANE) and −0.12256 ± 0.74486i (RABBIT and
    anti-rabbit). fⁿ_c(0) has degree 2^{n−1} with simple roots (Gleason);
    subtracting centres of smaller period dividing n: n = 4: 8 − 1 − 1 =
    6; n = 5: 16 − 1 = 15; n = 6: 32 − 1 − 1 − 3 = 27.
    (d) c = 1: 0, 1, 2, 5: out. c = −2: 0, −2, 2, 2, ...: in (tip).
    c = i: 0, i, −1 + i, −i, −1 + i, ...: in (preperiodic, a Misiurewicz
    point). 0.2: in, λ = 1 − √0.2 ≈ 0.553. −0.5 + 0.5i: λ = 1 − √(3 − 2i)
    has |λ| ≈ 0.985 < 1, in the cardioid. −0.75 + 0.1i: in the "seahorse
    valley" gap, not in the cardioid (|λ| ≈ 1.007) nor in the 2-disc
    (|4(c + 1)| ≈ 1.077), and the orbit of 0 leaves |z| ≤ 2 at n = 33.
    −1.2: |c + 1| = 0.2 < ¼, attracting 2-cycle with multiplier −0.8.
    Common mistakes: testing |c| ≤ 2 as if it were sufficient; forgetting
    that real c ∈ [−2, ¼] are all in M even outside hyperbolic components;
    concluding "not in M" after a few hundred bounded iterates near ∂M
    (only escape is a certificate).
    Now you try: with c = (r/2)(1 − r/2), find the c-values of r = 3,
    1 + √6, 1 + √8 and 4. (−¾, −5/4, −7/4 (birth of period 3), −2)

D10. (a) State Sullivan's classification of Fatou components and prove
    that every attracting cycle attracts a critical point; deduce the
    maximal number of attracting cycles of z² + c and of a cubic.
    (b) Newton's method for p = z² − 1: show N(z) = (z² + 1)/(2z) is
    conjugate to w² and find J(N). (c) For p = z³ − 1 find N, its
    critical points and the multiplier at ∞. (d) Where is the critical
    point of e^{2πiθ}z + z², and what happens at 0 for θ = (√5 − 1)/2?
    Answer: (a) at most 1 and 2. (b) φ(z) = (z − 1)/(z + 1), φ∘N = φ²;
    J = iℝ ∪ {∞}. (c) N = (2z³ + 1)/(3z²), critical points the cube roots
    of 1 and 0; λ(∞) = 3/2. (d) −e^{2πiθ}/2; a Siegel disc around 0.
    Teacher explains: (a) SULLIVAN: every Fatou component is eventually
    periodic, and a periodic one is an attracting basin, a parabolic
    basin, a Siegel disc or a Herman ring. Let z₀ be attracting, 0 < |λ|
    < 1, with immediate basin B, and ψ the inverse of the Koenigs map:
    ψ(w) = g(ψ(λw)), g the branch of f⁻¹ fixing z₀. This formula
    continues ψ to larger discs, with values in B, until a critical point
    is met. With no critical point in B, ψ would extend to a non-constant
    entire map into B ⊆ Ĉ ∖ J, omitting infinitely many values: Picard
    contradiction. So z² + c (one finite critical point 0) has at most
    one attracting cycle, a cubic at most 2.
    (b) N(z) − 1 = (z − 1)²/(2z) and N(z) + 1 = (z + 1)²/(2z), so φ(N(z))
    = φ(z)². The Möbius map φ sends 1 ↦ 0, −1 ↦ ∞ and iℝ ∪ {∞} onto S¹
    (|z − 1| = |z + 1|). Hence J(N) = φ⁻¹(J(w²)) = iℝ ∪ {∞}: Newton
    converges to the root in the same half-plane and fails exactly on
    the perpendicular bisector.
    (c) N = z − (z³ − 1)/(3z²) = (2z³ + 1)/(3z²) has degree 3, so 4
    critical points. N′ = pp″/p′² = 2(z³ − 1)/(3z³): the three roots
    (superattracting) and the double pole z = 0. Near ∞, N ≈ 2z/3, so in
    w = 1/z the multiplier is 3/2 (repelling); 0 ↦ ∞ ↦ ∞, so 0 ∈ J. Every
    non-repelling cycle and every rotation domain needs a free critical
    orbit, so the Fatou set consists of the three basins and their
    preimages, and J is their common (fractal) boundary.
    (d) f′(z) = e^{2πiθ} + 2z = 0 at z = −e^{2πiθ}/2. The golden mean has
    bounded partial quotients, so it is Diophantine and by SIEGEL'S
    theorem f is conjugate near 0 to z ↦ e^{2πiθ}z: a Siegel disc Δ,
    whose boundary lies in the closure of the critical orbit. For
    Liouville-like θ, 0 is a Cremer point; for quadratics the dividing
    line is the Brjuno condition (Yoccoz).
    Common mistakes: forgetting that the pole 0 of N is critical; saying
    Newton always converges; confusing Siegel discs (simply connected)
    with Herman rings (doubly connected, impossible for polynomials by
    the maximum principle).
    Now you try: how many non-repelling cycles can a degree-3 rational
    map have, and how many attracting cycles can z³ + c have? (4; 1:
    its only finite critical point is 0)

D11. (a) Find action-angle variables for H = (p² + ω²q²)/2; for the
    pendulum H = p²/2 − cos q find the small-oscillation frequency, the
    area enclosed by the separatrix and its action. (b) Decide Kolmogorov
    and isoenergetic nondegeneracy for H₀ = (I₁² + I₂²)/2, I₁ + I₂²/2 and
    I₁ + I₂. (c) Solve u(θ + α) − u(θ) = g(θ) on ℝ/ℤ by Fourier series,
    explicitly for g = cos 2πθ, and explain the small divisors. (d) Show
    that D(γ, τ) = {α : |qα − p| ≥ γq^{−τ} ∀p, q ≥ 1} has measure ≥ 1 −
    2γζ(τ) in ℝ/ℤ (τ > 1) and evaluate for γ = 0.01, τ = 2.
    Answer: (a) I = E/ω, H = ωI; frequency 1, area 16, action 8/π ≈
    2.546. (b) first: both; second: only isoenergetic; third: neither.
    (c) ûₖ = ĝₖ/(e^{2πikα} − 1) (k ≠ 0), ĝ₀ = 0 needed; u = sin(2π(θ −
    α/2))/(2 sin πα). (d) ≥ 1 − γπ²/3 ≈ 0.967.
    Teacher explains: (a) H = E is an ellipse with semi-axes √(2E)/ω and
    √(2E), area 2πE/ω, so I = (1/2π)∮p dq = E/ω, H = ωI, and ∂H/∂I = ω is
    independent of I (ISOCHRONOUS: degenerate). Pendulum: H ≈ p²/2 + q²/2
    − 1 near 0, frequency 1. The separatrix E = 1 is p = ±2cos(q/2), |q|
    ≤ π: area = 2∫_{−π}^{π}2cos(q/2)dq = 16, I_sep = 16/(2π) = 8/π; the
    period → ∞ there.
    (b) Kolmogorov: det H₀″; isoenergetic: det[[H₀″, ω], [ωᵀ, 0]], ω =
    ∇H₀. (I₁² + I₂²)/2: det H₀″ = 1, bordered det −(I₁² + I₂²) ≠ 0.
    I₁ + I₂²/2: H₀″ = diag(0, 1), det 0; [[0, 0, 1], [0, 1, I₂], [1, I₂,
    0]] has det −1. I₁ + I₂: both vanish (constant frequencies (1, 1)).
    (c) ûₖ(e^{2πikα} − 1) = ĝₖ: k = 0 forces ∫g = 0; for α ∉ ℚ the other
    ûₖ are determined. For g = cos 2πθ: u(θ + α) − u(θ) = [sin(2πθ + πα)
    − sin(2πθ − πα)]/(2 sin πα) = cos 2πθ. The divisor |e^{2πikα} − 1| =
    2|sin πkα| ≥ 4‖kα‖ is tiny when kα is near an integer. If ‖kα‖ ≥
    γ|k|^{−τ} and |ĝₖ| ≤ Ce^{−2πs|k|} (g analytic on |Im θ| < s), then |ûₖ|
    ≤ (C/4γ)|k|^τe^{−2πs|k|}: u is analytic only on a thinner strip |Im
    θ| < s − δ, with norm ~ γ⁻¹δ^{−τ−1}. KAM's quadratically convergent
    Newton scheme beats this loss.
    (d) For fixed q the bad set is q arcs of length 2γq^{−τ−1} around the
    p/q: measure ≤ 2γq^{−τ}; summing, ≤ 2γζ(τ) = 0.02·π²/6 ≈ 0.0329 for
    τ = 2. As γ → 0: a.e. α is Diophantine for each τ > 1.
    Common mistakes: forgetting ĝ₀ = 0; thinking Diophantine numbers are
    rare (full measure, though the complement is dense); calling the
    harmonic oscillator nondegenerate.
    Now you try: solve u(θ + α) − u(θ) = sin 4πθ. (u = −cos(4πθ −
    2πα)/(2 sin 2πα))

D12. (a) State the KAM theorem and Moser's twist theorem; why do tori
    confine the motion for n = 2 degrees of freedom but not for n ≥ 3?
    (b) For the standard map F(θ, p) = (θ + p + K sin θ, p + K sin θ)
    (mod 2π) show det DF = 1, find the fixed points, their residues R =
    (2 − tr)/4 and stability, and do the same for the period-2 orbit
    {(0, π), (π, π)}. (c) What does Poincaré-Birkhoff say about the
    resonant circle p = π of the unperturbed map? (d) What is known about
    the last invariant circle?
    Answer: (b) (0, 0): R = −K/4, hyperbolic; (π, 0): R = K/4, elliptic
    for 0 < K < 4; period 2: tr = 2 − K², R = K²/4, elliptic for 0 < K <
    2. (c) ≥ 2 period-2 orbits survive. (d) the golden circle, broken at
    K ≈ 0.9716; none for K > 63/64.
    Teacher explains: (a) KAM: H = H₀(I) + εH₁(I, θ) real-analytic,
    det H₀″ ≠ 0. For γ > 0, τ > n − 1 and small |ε|, every torus whose
    frequency ω(I₀) = ∇H₀(I₀) satisfies |⟨k, ω⟩| ≥ γ|k|^{−τ} survives,
    slightly deformed, with the flow conjugate to θ̇ = ω. Since I ↦ ω(I)
    is a local diffeomorphism, most tori qualify: the complement has
    measure O(√ε) (γ ~ √ε). MOSER: a small, sufficiently smooth, area-
    preserving perturbation of a twist map (θ, r) ↦ (θ + α(r), r), α′ ≠
    0, keeps the invariant curves with Diophantine rotation numbers.
    Energy surfaces have dimension 2n − 1 and tori dimension n; a torus
    separates only if n = 2n − 2, i.e. n = 2, so for 2 degrees of freedom
    each orbit is trapped between two KAM tori and the actions stay close
    to their initial values forever. For n ≥ 3 ARNOLD DIFFUSION may
    occur (at most exponentially slowly for steep H₀: Nekhoroshev).
    (b) DF = [[1 + K cos θ, 1], [K cos θ, 1]], det = 1 (F is the kicked
    rotor's time-1 map). Fixed points: sin θ = 0 and p ≡ 0. At (0, 0) tr
    = 2 + K, R = −K/4 < 0: hyperbolic. At (π, 0) tr = 2 − K, R = K/4:
    elliptic for 0 < K < 4. Period 2: (0, π) ↦ (π, π) ↦ (2π, π) as sin
    vanishes; DF(π)DF(0) = [[1 − K, 1], [−K, 1]][[1 + K, 1], [K, 1]] =
    [[1 + K − K², 2 − K], [−K², 1 − K]], tr = 2 − K², R = K²/4.
    (c) At K = 0 the circle p = π consists of period-2 points (rotation
    number ½). POINCARÉ-BIRKHOFF applied to F² on an annulus between
    circles turning slower and faster: for small K > 0 at least two
    period-2 orbits survive; the one in (b) is elliptic, its partner
    hyperbolic, and the circle becomes a chain of islands.
    (d) GREENE: the circle of rotation number ω exists iff the residues
    of the periodic orbits for the convergents of ω stay bounded. The
    golden-mean circle is the last to break, at K_c ≈ 0.971635
    (numerical); MATHER: no rotational invariant circles for K > 4/3;
    MACKAY-PERCIVAL: none for K > 63/64. They become CANTORI.
    Common mistakes: using DF at one point of the cycle only; believing
    KAM tori fill an open set (they are nowhere dense); applying KAM to
    the degenerate I₁ + I₂.
    Now you try: residue and stability of (π, 0) at K = 3 and K = 5.
    (¾, elliptic; 5/4, hyperbolic with reflection)

      j) source (0, 2)       k) sink (λ = ½ double)
      l) sink (2, 0)         m) centre, non-hyperbolic
      n) saddle (λ = 2, −1)  o) sink (spiral, λ = −1 ± i)

      q) saddle: λ = (−11 ± √1201)/2 ≈ 11.83, −22.83 and −8/3; (2, 1)
