       in d = 5
    e) Talagrand for ν = N(1, 1): W₂² and 2H(ν|γ)
    f) the same for N(0, 4)
    g) log-Sobolev for N(1, 4): H and ½I
    h) Gaussian Poincaré for f(x) = x² and f(x) = x
    i) Brunn-Minkowski for [0, 1]² and [0, 2] × [0, 8]
    j) Brunn-Minkowski for discs of radii 1 and 2
    k) isoperimetric bound for a 1 × 4 rectangle; for the unit cube
    l) CD(K, N) for Rⁿ, unit Sⁿ, Gaussian space, hyperbolic Hⁿ
    m) Bonnet-Myers bound for CD(3, 4) and CD(1, 3)
    n) Talagrand constant for a CD(2, ∞) space
    o) matching x ∈ {1, 2}, y ∈ {3, 4}, surplus xy; surplus −xy
    p) KR/WGAN critic and value for δ₀ versus δ₂
    q) sliced W₂ between δ₀ and δ_v in R² (average over directions)
    Answers:
      a) U[1, 5/2]                   b) δ₃
      c) ½δ₂ + ½δ₆                   d) n^{−1/2}; √(log n/n); n^{−1/5}; 32
      e) 1 = 1 (equality)            f) 1 ≤ 3 − 2log 2 ≈ 1.614
      g) 2 − log 2 ≈ 1.307 ≤ 13/8 = 1.625
      h) Var = 2 ≤ E(2X)² = 4; 1 = 1 (equality)
      i) √27 ≈ 5.196 ≥ 1 + 4 = 5     j) 3√π = √π + 2√π (equality)
      k) 10 ≥ 2√(4π) ≈ 7.090; 6 ≥ 3(4π/3)^{1/3} ≈ 4.836
      l) CD(0, n); CD(n − 1, n); CD(1, ∞); CD(−(n − 1), n)
      m) π√(3/3) = π; π√2
      n) W₂² ≤ (2/K)H = H
      o) 1–3, 2–4 (assortative, 11/2); 1–4, 2–3 (anti-assortative, −5)
      p) f(x) = x (1-Lipschitz), value 2
      q) (E_θ(v·θ)²)^{1/2} = |v|/√2

M12. True (T) or false (F)? Give a counterexample for F.
     a) For compactly supported μ, ν the Monge problem has a solution.
     b) For continuous c on compact X × Y an optimal plan exists.
     c) W₁ ≤ W₂ for probability measures.
     d) For c = |x − y| on R the optimal map is unique.
     e) If μ ≪ Lebesgue and μ, ν ∈ P₂(Rⁿ), the optimal plan for |x − y|²
        is unique.
     f) If φ is convex and μ ∈ P₂ with (∇φ)#μ ∈ P₂, then ∇φ is optimal
        between μ and (∇φ)#μ.
     g) The Brenier map between smooth positive densities on smooth
        bounded domains is continuous.
     h) On R the monotone coupling is optimal for |x − y|ᵖ for all p > 0.
     i) W₂²(N(0, Σ₁), N(0, Σ₂)) = ‖Σ₁^{1/2} − Σ₂^{1/2}‖²_F always.
     j) The mixture (1 − t)μ₀ + tμ₁ is a W₂-geodesic.
     k) (P₂(R), W₂) is isometric to a convex subset of a Hilbert space.
     l) ∫ρ log ρ is displacement convex on Rⁿ.
     m) U(ρ) = −2∫ρ^{1/2} (= ∫ρᵐ/(m − 1) with m = ½) is displacement
        convex on R³.
     n) The heat equation is the W₂-gradient flow of ½∫|∇ρ|².
     o) The Sinkhorn plan P_ε has the same support as the optimal plan.
     p) If a = b and Cᵢᵢ = 0, then ⟨C, P_ε⟩ = 0.
     q) Kantorovich potentials are unique.
     r) Equality holds in Talagrand's inequality for translates of γ.
     s) The log-Sobolev inequality implies Talagrand's inequality.
     t) A manifold with Ric ≥ 0 satisfies CD(0, ∞).
     u) CD(K, N) is stable under measured Gromov-Hausdorff limits.
     v) E W₁(μₙ, μ) ≍ n^{−1/2} for μ uniform on [0, 1]ᵈ, every d.
     Answers:
       a) F: δ₀ → ½δ₋₁ + ½δ₁ has no admissible map
       b) T        c) T (Jensen / Hölder)
       d) F: U[0, 2] → U[1, 3], shift and book map (D3)
       e) T (Brenier)              f) T (its graph is cyclically monotone)
       g) F: disc → two separated half-discs (target not convex)
       h) F: p = ½, {0, 1} → {1, 2} (M1 g)
       i) F: diag(1, 4) versus [[2, 1], [1, 2]]: 9 − 2√(10 + 4√3) ≈ 0.771
          but the formula gives 6 − 3√3 ≈ 0.804 (non-commuting)
       j) F: δ₀ to δ₂ (W₂(μₛ, μₜ) = 2√|t − s|)
       k) T (quantile functions)   l) T (McCann)
       m) F: needs m ≥ 1 − 1/3 = ⅔
       n) F: it is the W₂-flow of ∫ρ log ρ (and the L²-flow of ½∫|∇ρ|²)
       o) F: P_ε > 0 entrywise (M10 n)
       p) F: 1/(1 + e) ≈ 0.269 in M10 b), ε = 1
       q) F: (φ + k, ψ − k) is again optimal
       r) T (both sides equal |m|²)   s) T (Otto-Villani)
       t) T (von Renesse-Sturm)       u) T (Lott-Villani, Sturm)
       v) F: n^{−1/d} for d ≥ 3

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: start every problem by drawing the two measures as piles
  of sand and holes on a line and asking "who goes where?"; in 1D the
  answer is always "sort and pair" for convex costs, so let the child
  compute many W₁ = ∫|F − G| and quantile integrals before touching
  higher dimensions. Keep the 2 × 2 swap test C₁₁ + C₂₂ vs C₁₂ + C₂₁ on
  the board — it is cyclical monotonicity in miniature, and it explains
  duality (potentials), assortative matching and why concave costs cross.
  In Rⁿ, reduce to three model cases: translations (W₂ = |shift|),
  dilations (Gaussians, the Bures formula) and radial maps (match the
  mass of balls), and always check the answer is the GRADIENT OF A
  CONVEX function (symmetric PSD Jacobian). For flows, first write the
  first variation δF/δρ, then ∂ₜρ = ∇·(ρ∇δF/δρ), and test on Gaussians.
  For Sinkhorn, do two iterations by hand on a 2 × 2 example. Harder:
  prove Kantorovich duality via Fenchel-Rockafellar or via c-cyclical
  monotonicity (Rüschendorf); prove Brenier's theorem and McCann's
  version without moments; derive the Bures-Wasserstein formula and the
  Riemannian structure on Gaussian covariances; prove displacement
  convexity of ∫ρ log ρ using det^{1/n} concavity; prove convergence of
  the JKO scheme for Fokker-Planck; show linear convergence of Sinkhorn
  in the Hilbert metric; prove Talagrand from LSI (Otto-Villani) and
  isoperimetry from the Knothe map; and read how Lott-Sturm-Villani
  obtain Bishop-Gromov and Brunn-Minkowski from CD(K, N).


================================================================================
TOPIC 13.15 — TOPOLOGICAL DATA ANALYSIS: PERSISTENT HOMOLOGY, COMPLEXES
FROM DATA, BARCODES, STABILITY, INTERLEAVINGS, ZIGZAG AND MULTIPARAMETER
PERSISTENCE, MAPPER, EULER CHARACTERISTIC CURVES, STATISTICS OF DIAGRAMS
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - CONVENTIONS used throughout: X a finite metric space (a POINT CLOUD).
    VIETORIS-RIPS VR_t(X) = {σ ⊆ X : diam σ ≤ t} (DIAMETER convention); it
    is a FLAG (clique) complex, determined by its 1-skeleton, and intrinsic
    to the metric. ČECH Č_r(X) = nerve of the closed balls B̄(x, r), x ∈ X
    ⊂ ℝᵈ: σ ∈ Č_r iff ⋂_{x∈σ} B̄(x, r) ≠ ∅ iff the MINIMUM ENCLOSING BALL
    (MEB) of σ has radius ≤ r (RADIUS convention). MEB radius of a
    triangle: circumradius if acute, half the longest side if right or
    obtuse. On ℝ¹ (Helly): Č_r = VR_{2r}. Bars are half-open [b, d).
  - NERVE THEOREM: a finite cover by closed convex sets (more generally a
    GOOD cover: all non-empty finite intersections contractible) has
    nerve ≃ union. Hence Č_r(X) ≃ ⋃_x B̄(x, r) (the "thickening").
    SANDWICH: Č_r ⊆ VR_{2r} ⊆ Č_{2r} in any metric space containing X, and
    in ℝᵈ VR_{2r} ⊆ Č_{r√(2d/(d+1))} (JUNG's theorem; factor 2/√3 in ℝ²).
    Rips can have homology the union of balls does not (hexagon: S²).
  - ALPHA COMPLEX (X ⊂ ℝᵈ in general position): A_r = {σ : ⋂_{x∈σ}(B̄(x,
    r) ∩ Vor(x)) ≠ ∅}, a subcomplex of the DELAUNAY triangulation, A_r ⊆
    Č_r and A_r ≃ Č_r ≃ ⋃B̄(x, r) (same barcode as Čech), size O(n^{⌈d/2⌉})
    instead of 2ⁿ. An edge is GABRIEL (enters at half its length) iff its
    diametral ball contains no other point; otherwise it enters with an
    obtuse coface. WITNESS COMPLEX (de Silva-Carlsson): landmarks L ⊂ W
    witnesses; σ ⊆ L is weakly witnessed by w if every l ∈ σ is at least
    as close to w as every l′ ∉ σ; W(L, W) = simplices all of whose faces
    are witnessed. Cheap (#L ≪ #W), no guarantee of correct homotopy.
  - FILTRATION: nested complexes K₀ ⊆ K₁ ⊆ ... ⊆ K_m (or K_t, t ∈ ℝ),
    equivalently a monotone function f on simplices (f(τ) ≤ f(σ) for τ ⊆
    σ). LOWER-STAR filtration of vertex values: f(σ) = max of its vertex
    values; SUBLEVEL sets f⁻¹(−∞, t]. Applying Hₖ(−; F) gives a sequence
    of vector spaces and linear maps: a PERSISTENCE MODULE.
  - HOMOLOGY OVER A FIELD F: βₖ = dim Hₖ = nₖ − rank ∂ₖ − rank ∂ₖ₊₁;
    χ = ∑(−1)ᵏnₖ = ∑(−1)ᵏβₖ (independent of F). Betti numbers depend on
    char F only through torsion: RP² has β = (1, 1, 1) over F₂, (1, 0, 0)
    over ℚ; Klein bottle (1, 2, 1) vs (1, 1, 0). PERSISTENT BETTI NUMBERS
    βₖ^{i,j} = rank(Hₖ(Kᵢ) → Hₖ(Kⱼ)) = dim Zₖ(Kᵢ)/(Bₖ(Kⱼ) ∩ Zₖ(Kᵢ)).
  - MATRIX REDUCTION (Edelsbrunner-Letscher-Zomorodian; Cohen-Steiner-
    Edelsbrunner-Morozov): order simplices σ₁, ..., σₙ compatibly with the
    filtration (faces first); D = boundary matrix over F; low(j) = row of
    the lowest non-zero entry of column j. Reduce left to right: while
    some earlier column has the same low, add a multiple of it. Then R =
    DV (V upper unitriangular), lows are distinct, and each pair (i =
    low(j), j) is a class BORN at σᵢ (positive simplex) and KILLED by σⱼ
    (negative): bar [f(σᵢ), f(σⱼ)); zero columns never used as a low give
    ESSENTIAL bars [f(σᵢ), ∞). The pairing does not depend on choices.
    Worst case O(n³); CLEARING/TWIST (zero column i if i = low(j) already)
    and COHOMOLOGY (same barcodes) make Ripser-type software fast.
  - STRUCTURE THEOREM: a pointwise finite-dimensional persistence module
    over ℤ or ℝ (Webb, Crawley-Boevey) is ≅ ⊕ interval modules I[b, d),
    uniquely up to order: the BARCODE. For a finite filtration: Hₖ is a
    graded module over the PID F[t] and ≅ ⊕ t^{bᵢ}F[t] ⊕ ⊕ t^{bⱼ}F[t]/
    (t^{dⱼ}) ↔ bars [bᵢ, ∞), [bⱼ, dⱼ). PERSISTENCE DIAGRAM Dgm = multiset
    of points (b, d) above the diagonal, plus the diagonal with infinite
    multiplicity. Multiplicity of [i, j) = β^{i,j−1} − β^{i,j} −
    β^{i−1,j−1} + β^{i−1,j} (inclusion-exclusion of the rank invariant).
  - ELDER RULE for H₀: when two components merge, the YOUNGER (larger
    birth) dies. H₀ of VR = SINGLE-LINKAGE clustering; the finite death
    times are the edge lengths of a minimum spanning tree.
  - BOTTLENECK DISTANCE d_B(D, D′) = inf over bijections η : D → D′ (each
    diagram augmented by the diagonal) of sup ‖p − η(p)‖_∞; matching p =
    (b, d) to the diagonal costs (d − b)/2. q-WASSERSTEIN W_q = inf
    (∑‖p − η(p)‖_∞^q)^{1/q}; W_∞ = d_B. Essential points must be matched
    to essential points (else distance ∞).
  - STABILITY THEOREM (Cohen-Steiner-Edelsbrunner-Harer): f, g tame on a
    triangulable space (or monotone on a finite complex) ⇒ d_B(Dgmₖ f,
    Dgmₖ g) ≤ ‖f − g‖_∞. GEOMETRIC: d_B(Dgm Č(X), Dgm Č(Y)) ≤ d_H(X, Y)
    (ambient Hausdorff); for Rips (diameter) ≤ 2 d_GH(X, Y). Wasserstein
    stability needs more: W_p ≤ ‖f − g‖_p (ℓᵖ over simplices, Skraba-
    Turner) for monotone functions on a finite complex.
  - INTERLEAVINGS: M, N are ε-INTERLEAVED if there are maps φ_t : M_t →
    N_{t+ε}, ψ_t : N_t → M_{t+ε} commuting with the structure maps with
    ψφ = M(t ≤ t + 2ε), φψ = N(t ≤ t + 2ε). d_I = inf such ε. ‖f − g‖_∞
    ≤ ε ⇒ sublevel modules ε-interleaved. ISOMETRY THEOREM (Lesnick;
    Bauer-Lesnick; algebraic stability of Chazal et al.): d_I = d_B. For
    intervals: d_I(I[a, b), I[c, d)) = min(max(|a − c|, |b − d|),
    max((b − a)/2, (d − c)/2)).
  - ZIGZAG PERSISTENCE (Carlsson-de Silva): modules V₁ ↔ V₂ ↔ ... ↔ Vₙ with
    arrows in either direction still decompose into intervals (GABRIEL's
    theorem for type Aₙ quivers). Uses: X₁ ⊆ X₁ ∪ X₂ ⊇ X₂ ⊆ ...
    (time-varying data, subsampling). MULTIPARAMETER persistence (e.g.
    scale × density): modules over ℝ² are of WILD type; no complete
    discrete invariant (Carlsson-Zomorodian). The RANK INVARIANT rank(M_u
    → M_v), u ≤ v, is computable but incomplete; tools: fibred barcodes
    along lines, Hilbert function, signed barcodes.
  - MAPPER (Singh-Mémoli-Carlsson): filter f : X → ℝ (height, density,
    eccentricity, PCA coordinate), cover of f(X) by overlapping intervals
    Uᵢ, cluster each f⁻¹(Uᵢ) (single linkage), output the NERVE of the
    clusters: a graph (1-skeleton) summarizing shape; a discrete version
    of the REEB GRAPH. Depends on resolution (number of intervals) and
    gain (overlap); too coarse covers lose loops.
  - EULER CHARACTERISTIC CURVE χ(t) = ∑(−1)ᵏ#{k-simplices with f ≤ t} =
    ∑(−1)ᵏβₖ(t): linear-time, additive, stable-ish, but forgets pairings
    (different barcodes can have the same curve). The Euler characteristic
    transform over all directions determines constructible shapes in ℝᵈ.
  - STATISTICS: diagrams form a non-linear space (Fréchet means exist but
    need not be unique). Vectorizations: PERSISTENCE LANDSCAPE (Bubenik)
    λₖ(t) = k-th largest of max(0, min(t − b, d − t)), 1-Lipschitz, in a
    Banach space, so means and CLT work; persistence images; total
    persistence ∑(d − b)ᵖ; kernels. BOOTSTRAP confidence sets (Fasy et
    al.): points of distance > c from the diagonal band (length > 2c) are
    significant. Applications: shape/protein/material classification,
    sliding-window (Takens) embeddings detecting periodicity, neuroscience
    (circular place-cell data), image and time-series features.

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. Let X ⊂ ℝᵈ be finite. (a) Prove Č_r(X) ⊆ VR_{2r}(X) ⊆ Č_{2r}(X) and
    explain why Č_r(X) ≃ ⋃B̄(x, r). (b) For the vertices of an equilateral
    triangle of side 1 compute the H₀ and H₁ barcodes of the Rips and Čech
    filtrations. (c) Same for the unit square (0,0), (1,0), (1,1), (0,1).
    Answer: (b) Rips: H₀ {[0,1)², [0,∞)}, H₁ = ∅. Čech: H₀ {[0,½)²,
    [0,∞)}, H₁ {[½, 1/√3)}. (c) Rips: H₀ {[0,1)³, [0,∞)}, H₁ {[1, √2)};
    Čech: H₀ {[0,½)³, [0,∞)}, H₁ {[½, √2/2)}.
    Teacher explains: (a) If σ ∈ Č_r, pick c ∈ ⋂B̄(x, r); for x, y ∈ σ,
    |x − y| ≤ |x − c| + |c − y| ≤ 2r, so diam σ ≤ 2r: σ ∈ VR_{2r}. If σ ∈
    VR_{2r}, fix a vertex x₀ ∈ σ: every x ∈ σ has |x − x₀| ≤ 2r, so x₀ ∈
    ⋂B̄(x, 2r) and σ ∈ Č_{2r}. The balls are convex, so every non-empty
    intersection is convex, hence contractible: the nerve theorem gives
    Č_r ≃ union of balls. (Rips has no such theorem: it can contain
    spurious higher homology.)
    (b) All pairwise distances are 1. Rips: at t = 1 the three edges AND
    the triangle (diameter 1) appear together, so H₁ never appears; two
    components die at 1 (elder rule; ties broken arbitrarily). Čech: the
    edges enter at r = ½ (half the side), creating a hollow triangle, β₁
    = 1; the triangle enters at its circumradius R = abc/(4·Area) =
    1/(4·√3/4) = 1/√3 ≈ 0.577, killing the loop: H₁ bar [½, 1/√3). The
    union of three discs of radius r ∈ (½, 1/√3) indeed has a small hole
    around the centroid.
    (c) Distances: sides 1, diagonals √2. Rips: at t = 1 the 4-cycle, β₁ =
    4 − 4 + 1 = 1; every triangle has a diagonal, so none enters before
    √2; at t = √2 the whole 3-simplex appears (contractible): [1, √2).
    Čech: sides at ½, diagonals at √2/2; each triangle of three corners
    is RIGHT-angled, MEB radius = half the hypotenuse = √2/2, and the full
    tetrahedron also has MEB radius √2/2 (the circumscribed circle).
    Check the sandwich: Rips at 2r contains Čech at r: [1, √2) = 2·[½, √2/2).
    Common mistakes: taking the Čech filtration value of a triangle to be
    the circumradius when the triangle is obtuse (it is half the longest
    side); mixing the diameter and radius conventions (a factor 2);
    believing Rips and Čech always have the same bars up to scaling.
    Now you try: the rectangle (0,0), (2,0), (2,1), (0,1). (Rips H₀ {[0,1)²,
    [0,2), [0,∞)}, H₁ {[2, √5)}; Čech H₁ {[1, √5/2)})

D2. (a) Define the alpha complex and explain why it has the same barcode
    as the Čech filtration while being much smaller. (b) For the rhombus
    X = {(−2, 0), (2, 0), (0, 1), (0, −1)} find the Delaunay triangulation,
    the alpha filtration values and the barcode; compare with Rips. (c)
    Landmarks L = {0, 3, 10} ⊂ ℝ, witnesses W = {1, 2, 6, 7, 9}: find the
    weak witness complex.
    Answer: (b) Delaunay: 4 sides + short diagonal, 2 triangles. Values:
    short diagonal 1, sides √5/2, triangles 5/4. H₀ {[0,1), [0,√5/2)²,
    [0,∞)}, H₁ {[√5/2, 5/4)²}. Rips: H₀ {[0,2), [0,√5)², [0,∞)}, H₁ = ∅.
    (c) The path 0 — 3 — 10 (no edge {0, 10}, no triangle).
    Teacher explains: (a) Restrict each ball to the VORONOI cell of its
    centre: B̄(x, r) ∩ Vor(x). These convex pieces have the SAME union as
    the balls, so by the nerve theorem their nerve A_r ≃ ⋃B̄(x, r) ≃ Č_r;
    the inclusions A_r ⊆ Č_r commute with the filtration maps, so the
    persistence modules are isomorphic. Since Voronoi cells meet only
    along Delaunay simplices, A_r ⊆ Del(X), with O(n^{⌈d/2⌉}) simplices.
    (b) Circumcircle of T = (−2,0), (0,1), (0,−1): centre (x, 0) with (x +
    2)² = x² + 1, x = −3/4, R = 5/4; the fourth point (2, 0) is at
    distance 11/4 > 5/4, outside: T and its mirror image are Delaunay, the
    long diagonal (length 4) is not. T is acute (at (−2,0): cos = (2,1)·
    (2,−1)/5 = 3/5 > 0; at (0,1): cos = 2/(2√5) > 0), so its value is the
    circumradius 5/4. Edges: short diagonal, length 2, diametral disc
    centred at 0 of radius 1 contains no other point (Gabriel): value 1.
    Side (−2,0)(0,1): centre (−1, ½), radius √5/2 ≈ 1.118, distance to
    (0,−1) is √(1 + 9/4) ≈ 1.80: Gabriel, value √5/2. Filtration: at r =
    1 the points (0, ±1) merge; at √5/2 all four sides appear: 4 vertices,
    5 edges, one component, β₁ = 5 − 4 + 1 = 2; at 5/4 both triangles
    fill both holes. Rips: at t = √5 the sides AND both triangles (diam √5)
    enter together: no H₁ at all. The union of discs really has two holes
    for r ∈ (1.118, 1.25): Rips misses them.
    (c) A witness w weakly witnesses the edge {l, l′} iff these are its two
    nearest landmarks: w = 1, 2 → {0, 3}; w = 6 (distances 3, 4), 7, 9 →
    {3, 10}. No witness has {0, 10} as its two nearest, so that edge and
    hence the triangle are absent: W(L, W) = path, contractible, like the
    segment [0, 10] that the data sample.
    Common mistakes: taking the full Delaunay triangulation as the alpha
    complex at every scale; giving a non-Gabriel edge the value half its
    length; forgetting that a witness complex needs every face witnessed.
    Now you try: alpha values for (0,0), (1,0), (0,1). (edges ½, ½, √2/2;
    triangle √2/2, entering together with the hypotenuse: no H₁)

D3. (a) Describe the standard reduction algorithm and prove that the
    pairs (low(j), j) give the barcode. (b) Over F₂ reduce the filtration
    a(0), b(0), c(1), d(1), ab(1), bc(2), cd(2), ac(3), ad(3), abc(4),
    acd(5) (times in brackets; simplices listed in the order used) and
    read off the barcodes.
    Answer: (b) H₀: [0,∞) (a), [0,1) (b), [1,2) (c), [1,2) (d);
    H₁: [3,4) (born ac, killed abc), [3,5) (born ad, killed acd).
    Teacher explains: (a) Columns of D are the boundaries ∂σⱼ. Adding an
    earlier column to a later one is a right multiplication by an upper
    unitriangular matrix, so R = DV keeps the key property that for every
    j the first j columns of R span ∂(first j simplices), and more finely
    rank of the lower-left submatrices R[i.., ..j] equals that of D; the
    persistent Betti numbers are expressed through exactly these ranks,
    so they can be read from R. Once lows are distinct, the non-zero
    columns are independent with distinct pivots: column j (low i) says
    that ∂(chain of V's column j) has youngest simplex σᵢ, i.e. the class
    created when σᵢ entered becomes a boundary exactly at time j. A zero
    column j means σⱼ closes a cycle (column j of V is a cycle): positive
    simplex; if it is never a low, that cycle is never killed: essential.
    (b) Columns over F₂ (lows in capitals): ab = {a, B}: pair (b, ab), bar
    [0,1). bc = {b, C}: pair (c, bc), bar [1,2). cd = {c, D}: pair (d, cd),
    bar [1,2). ac = {a, C}: low c is used by bc, so add bc: {a, c} + {b,
    c} = {a, B}; low b is used by ab, add ab: {a, b} + {a, b} = 0. Zero
    column: ac is positive (the cycle ab + bc + ac). ad = {a, D}
    → + cd → {a, C} → + bc → {a, B} → + ab → 0: positive (cycle ab + bc +
    cd + ad). abc = {ab, bc, AC}: low ac is free: pair (ac, abc), bar [3,
    4). acd = {ac, cd, AD}: low ad free: pair (ad, acd), bar [3, 5). Vertex
    a is never a low: H₀ essential bar [0, ∞).
    Common mistakes: listing a coface before its face (not a filtration);
    reading births from rows of D instead of R; reporting zero-length
    bars such as [3,3) as features (they are usually discarded).
    Now you try: same complex but all vertices at 0, ab, bc, cd, ad at 1,
    ac at 2, abc and acd at 3 (in this order). (H₀ [0,1)³, [0,∞); H₁
    [2,3) and [1,3): acd's column reduces to ab + bc + cd + ad, low ad)

D4. (a) Why does persistence use FIELD coefficients? (b) The 6-vertex
    triangulation of RP² has 6 vertices, 15 edges and 10 triangles, each
    edge in exactly two triangles. Compute β₀, β₁, β₂ over F₂ and over ℚ.
    (c) Consider the filtration K₀ = RP¹ (a circle inside) ⊆ K₁ = RP².
    Find the barcodes over F₂ and ℚ.
    Answer: (b) χ = 1; over F₂ (1, 1, 1), over ℚ (1, 0, 0).
    (c) F₂: H₀ [0,∞), H₁ [0,∞), H₂ [1,∞). ℚ: H₀ [0,∞), H₁ [0,1).
    Teacher explains: (a) The homology of a filtration is a graded module
    over R[t]; to classify it by barcodes we need R[t] to be a PID whose
    graded modules are sums of cyclic ones with ONE parameter per summand
    (birth, death): this holds for R = F a field. Over ℤ, ℤ[t] is not a
    PID and extra torsion data appears. Also only over a field is "rank"
    of a map the whole story.
    (b) χ = 6 − 15 + 10 = 1 regardless of coefficients. Connected: β₀ = 1.
    Over F₂: the sum of all ten triangles has boundary ∑ (each edge twice)
    = 0, so it is a 2-cycle; there are no 3-simplices, so β₂ = 1, and β₁ =
    β₀ + β₂ − χ = 1. Over ℚ: a 2-cycle would need coefficients ±c on
    adjacent triangles with orientations cancelling along each edge; that
    is a coherent orientation, impossible on a non-orientable surface, so
    β₂ = 0 and β₁ = 1 + 0 − 1 = 0 (indeed H₁(RP²; ℤ) = ℤ/2 dies over ℚ).
    (c) H₁(RP¹) → H₁(RP²) is ℤ → ℤ/2, reduction mod 2. Over F₂ this is F₂
    → F₂, an isomorphism: the circle's class persists forever, and the
    new 2-cycle is born at 1. Over ℚ, H₁(RP²; ℚ) = 0: the loop is born at
    0 and dies at 1 (it bounds twice the disc, and ½ exists in ℚ); no H₂.
    Common mistakes: thinking β's are coefficient-free (only χ is);
    running reduction over F₂ and calling it "the" barcode for data with
    torsion; forgetting that a death over ℚ may be "twice a boundary".
    Now you try: the Klein bottle over F₂ and ℚ. ((1, 2, 1) and (1, 1, 0);
    χ = 0 either way)

D5. (a) State the structure theorem and the correspondence between a
    finite filtration and a graded F[t]-module. (b) Prove that the number
    of bars [i, j) equals μ^{i,j} = β^{i,j−1} − β^{i,j} − β^{i−1,j−1} +
    β^{i−1,j}. (c) A filtration K₀ ⊆ ... ⊆ K₄ has β₁(Kᵢ) = 0, 1, 3, 2, 1 and
    persistent ranks r(1,2) = 1, r(1,3) = r(1,4) = 0, r(2,3) = 2, r(2,4) =
    1, r(3,4) = 1. Find the H₁ barcode.
    Answer: (c) [1,3), [2,4), [2,∞) (essential: alive in K₄).
    Teacher explains: (a) Set M = ⊕ᵢ Hₖ(Kᵢ) with t acting by the inclusion
    maps Hₖ(Kᵢ) → Hₖ(Kᵢ₊₁): a finitely generated graded F[t]-module. The
    graded structure theorem for the PID F[t] gives M ≅ ⊕ t^{bᵢ}F[t] ⊕ ⊕
    t^{bⱼ}F[t]/(t^{dⱼ}); a free summand generated in degree b is a class
    born at b living forever, a torsion summand t^{b}F[t]/(t^{d}) is
    non-zero in degrees b, ..., d − 1: the bar [b, d). Uniqueness of the
    decomposition = uniqueness of the barcode.
    (b) By the structure theorem β^{i,j} = rank(Mᵢ → Mⱼ) = #bars [b, d)
    with b ≤ i and d > j (the bar contains both i and j). Then β^{i,j−1} −
    β^{i,j} counts bars with b ≤ i and d = j exactly; subtracting the same
    count with i − 1 (b ≤ i − 1, d = j) leaves b = i, d = j.
    (c) Dimension check first. μ(1,3) = r(1,2) − r(1,3) − r(0,2) + r(0,3)
    = 1 − 0 − 0 + 0 = 1 (r(0,·) = 0 since β₁(K₀) = 0). μ(1,2) = r(1,1) −
    r(1,2) − 0 + 0 = 1 − 1 = 0. μ(2,3) = r(2,2) − r(2,3) − r(1,2) + r(1,3)
    = 3 − 2 − 1 + 0 = 0. μ(2,4) = r(2,3) − r(2,4) − r(1,3) + r(1,4) = 2 −
    1 − 0 + 0 = 1. Bars alive at the end, born at i: r(i,4) − r(i−1,4):
    at 2: 1 − 0 = 1; at 3: r(3,4) − r(2,4) = 0. So [1,3), [2,4), [2,∞);
    check dims: at 2 all three alive (3), at 3 two (2), at 4 one (1).
    Common mistakes: counting a bar [b, d) as alive at d; mixing up
    rank(Mᵢ → Mⱼ) with dim Mⱼ; applying the inclusion-exclusion without
    the boundary terms r(i − 1, ·) = 0 when i is the first index.
    Now you try: F[t]-module F[t] ⊕ tF[t]/(t³) ⊕ t²F[t]/(t³): barcode and
    dims in degrees 0..3. ([0,∞), [1,3), [2,3); dims 1, 2, 3, 1)

D6. (a) State the stability theorem for sublevel-set persistence and its
    geometric corollaries. (b) On the path graph with vertices v₁, ..., v₆
    let f = (0, 3, 1, 4, 2, 5) and g = (0.5, 2.5, 1.5, 3.5, 2.5, 4.5)
    (lower-star filtrations). Compute Dgm₀ f, Dgm₀ g, d_B and ‖f − g‖_∞.
    (c) X = {0, 1, 3, 7}, Y = {0, 1, 3, 8} ⊂ ℝ: compare d_B of the H₀
    Rips diagrams with the stability bound.
    Answer: (b) Dgm₀ f = {(1,3), (2,4), (0,∞)}, Dgm₀ g = {(1.5, 2.5), (2.5,
    3.5), (0.5,∞)}; d_B = ½ = ‖f − g‖_∞ (the bound is sharp). (c) d_B =
    1 ≤ 2 d_H(X, Y) = 2.
    Teacher explains: (a) If f, g : K → ℝ are monotone on a finite complex
    (or tame continuous on a triangulable space) then for every k,
    d_B(Dgmₖ f, Dgmₖ g) ≤ ‖f − g‖_∞. Proof idea: ‖f − g‖_∞ ≤ ε gives
    inclusions f⁻¹(−∞, t] ⊆ g⁻¹(−∞, t + ε] ⊆ f⁻¹(−∞, t + 2ε]: an ε-
    interleaving; the isometry theorem turns it into an ε-matching.
    Corollaries: Č with d_H(X, Y) ≤ ε in ℝᵈ: ⋃_X B̄(x, r) ⊆ ⋃_Y B̄(y, r + ε)
    so d_B ≤ d_H; Rips (diameter) d_B ≤ 2d_GH ≤ 2d_H.
    (b) f: minima 0 (v₁), 1 (v₃), 2 (v₅); edge values max of ends: 3, 3,
    4, 4, 5. At 3 edges v₁v₂, v₂v₃ join v₃'s component (min 1) to v₁'s (min
    0): the younger dies, (1, 3). At 4 the component of v₅ (min 2) joins:
    (2, 4). g: minima 0.5, 1.5, 2.5; edges 2.5, 2.5, 3.5, 3.5, 4.5: pairs
    (1.5, 2.5), (2.5, 3.5), essential 0.5. Matching point to point costs
    max(½, ½) = ½ each, essential |0 − 0.5| = ½. Could it be less? The
    essential bars must be matched to each other, forcing ≥ ½. And ‖f −
    g‖_∞ = ½ (every entry moved by ½), so equality.
    (c) Dgm₀ VR(X) = {(0,1), (0,2), (0,4), (0,∞)} (gaps 1, 2, 4), Dgm₀
    VR(Y) = {(0,1), (0,2), (0,5), (0,∞)}: match identically except (0,4) ↔
    (0,5), cost 1. d_H(X, Y) = 1 (7 ↔ 8), bound 2.
    Common mistakes: letting the older component die; matching an
    essential point to the diagonal; forgetting that stability bounds are
    in the sup norm, so one large outlier can move a diagram a lot.
    Now you try: f as above, h = (0, 3, 2.9, 4, 2, 5). (Dgm₀ h = {(2.9,
    3), (2,4), (0,∞)}; d_B(f, h) = 1 ≤ ‖f − h‖_∞ = 1.9)

D7. (a) Define the bottleneck and q-Wasserstein distances between
    persistence diagrams and explain why the diagonal must be included.
    (b) Compute d_B, W₁, W₂ (ground metric ‖·‖_∞) for D = {(0,4), (1,3)},
    D′ = {(0,5)}. (c) Same for E = {(0,2), (4,6)}, E′ = {(0,6)}. (d) Show
    d_B(D, ∅) = max (d − b)/2 and W_q(D, ∅)^q = ∑((d − b)/2)^q.
    Answer: (b) d_B = 1, W₁ = 2, W₂ = √2. (c) d_B = 3, W₁ = 5, W₂ = √11
    ≈ 3.317. (d) see below.
    Teacher explains: (a) A diagram is a multiset of points above Δ =
    {b = d}; we add every point of Δ with infinite multiplicity. A partial
    matching between D and D′ is then a bijection of the augmented
    multisets; a point sent to Δ is sent to its nearest diagonal point
    ((b+d)/2, (b+d)/2), at ‖·‖_∞-distance (d − b)/2. d_B = inf over
    bijections of the largest cost; W_q = inf of the ℓ^q norm of the
    costs. Without Δ diagrams with different numbers of points would be
    incomparable, and a tiny perturbation creating a short bar (noise)
    would make the distance jump: including Δ is what makes STABILITY
    possible. Triangle inequality: compose optimal bijections.
    (b) Options: (0,4) ↔ (0,5) cost max(0, 1) = 1, (1,3) → Δ cost 1: max 1,
    sum 2, ℓ² √2. Alternative (1,3) ↔ (0,5) cost 2 is worse. All to Δ
    costs 2.5 for (0,5). So d_B = 1, W₁ = 2, W₂ = √(1 + 1) = √2.
    (c) Options: (0,6) ↔ (0,2): cost 4, (4,6) → Δ: 1 — max 4, sum 5,
    squares 17. (0,6) ↔ (4,6): symmetric, same. Everything to Δ: costs 3,
    1, 1 — max 3, sum 5, squares 11. So d_B = 3, W₁ = 5 (a tie), W₂ = √11.
    Moral: one long bar is far from two short bars at its ends.
    (d) Only matchings to Δ are available, so the costs are exactly the
    half-lengths: d_B(D, ∅) = max(d − b)/2, W_q^q = ∑((d − b)/2)^q.
    Common mistakes: using the Euclidean distance to the diagonal
    ((d − b)/√2) while the ground metric is ‖·‖_∞; allowing an essential
    point to be matched to Δ; forgetting that W₁ ≥ d_B always.
    Now you try: D = {(0,5), (1,2)}, D′ = {(0,4), (3,4)}. (d_B = 1, W₁ = 2,
    W₂ = √(3/2))

D8. (a) Define ε-interleavings of persistence modules over ℝ and show
    that ‖f − g‖_∞ ≤ ε gives an ε-interleaving of the sublevel homology
    modules. (b) Prove that d_I(I[a, b), 0) = (b − a)/2 and that d_I(I[a,
    b), I[c, d)) ≤ min(max(|a − c|, |b − d|), max((b − a)/2, (d − c)/2)).
    (c) Compute d_I for the pairs I[0,4), I[1,5); I[0,10), I[1,3); I[0,2),
    I[5,7); I[0,∞), I[0,5).
    Answer: (c) 1; 5; 1; ∞.
    Teacher explains: (a) M_ε denotes M shifted: (M_ε)_t = M_{t+ε}. An ε-
    interleaving is a pair of morphisms φ : M → N_ε, ψ : N → M_ε with
    ψ_ε ∘ φ = (shift by 2ε map of M) and φ_ε ∘ ψ = (same for N). If f ≤ g
    + ε and g ≤ f + ε then Fₜ = {f ≤ t} ⊆ {g ≤ t + ε} = G_{t+ε} ⊆ F_{t+2ε};
    these inclusions commute with all other inclusions, and applying Hₖ
    gives φ, ψ; the composites are the maps induced by F_t ⊆ F_{t+2ε}.
    (b) M = I[a, b) is ε-interleaved with 0 iff the 2ε-shift map M_t →
    M_{t+2ε} (which factors through 0) is zero for all t, i.e. no t with
    t, t + 2ε both in [a, b): iff 2ε ≥ b − a. For two intervals: either
    both are interleaved with 0 (ε ≥ both half-lengths; then the zero
    maps work), or we use the "identity" maps F → F wherever both
    intervals are non-zero: this is an ε-interleaving as soon as |a − c|
    ≤ ε and |b − d| ≤ ε (endpoints moved by at most ε).
    (c) I[0,4), I[1,5): max(1, 1) = 1 vs max(2, 2) = 2: d_I = 1. I[0,10),
    I[1,3): max(1, 7) = 7 vs max(5, 1) = 5: d_I = 5 — the short bar is
    killed rather than stretched. I[0,2), I[5,7): max(5, 5) = 5 vs max(1,
    1) = 1: d_I = 1. I[0,∞), I[0,5): an infinite bar is not interleaved
    with 0, and |∞ − 5| = ∞: d_I = ∞. By the ISOMETRY THEOREM these equal
    the bottleneck distances of the one-point diagrams, as expected.
    Common mistakes: asking only φψ = shift by ε (it must be 2ε);
    forgetting the option of killing both bars; thinking d_I only sees
    the endpoints and not the lengths.
    Now you try: d_I(I[0,3) ⊕ I[1,2), I[0,3)) and d_I(I[2,6), I[3,4)).
    (½; 2)

D9. (a) What is a zigzag module and why does it have a barcode? Compute
    the barcodes of F →^{e₁} F² ←^{e₁} F and F →^{e₁} F² ←^{e₂} F. (b) Use
    zigzag H₀ with Rips at scale 1.5 to compare X₁ = {0, 1} with X₂ = {5, 6}
    and with X₂′ = {1, 2} via X₁ ⊆ X₁ ∪ X₂ ⊇ X₂. (c) On the grid {0,1}²
    let M = I{(1,0),(0,1),(1,1)} ⊕ I{(1,1)} and N = I{(1,0),(1,1)} ⊕
    I{(0,1),(1,1)}. Show M and N have the same rank invariant but are not
    isomorphic. (d) Why is there no barcode for 2-parameter persistence?
    Answer: (a) [1,3], [2,2]; [1,2], [2,3] (closed index intervals).
    (b) {5,6}: [1,2], [2,3] — no common cluster; {1,2}: [1,3] — one
    cluster persists. (c) See below. (d) Continuous moduli (wild type).
    Teacher explains: (a) A zigzag is V₁ ↔ V₂ ↔ ... ↔ Vₙ with each arrow in
    either direction: a representation of a quiver of type Aₙ. GABRIEL:
    the indecomposables are exactly the interval modules (F on a run of
    consecutive indices, identities between them), and Krull-Schmidt
    gives uniqueness. First example: both copies of F land on the same
    line span(e₁): one interval [1,3]; the complement span(e₂) at index
    2 is a bar [2,2]. Second: the images are different lines: [1,2] and
    [2,3].
    (b) VR_{1.5}({0,1}) is an edge: H₀ = F. X₁ ∪ X₂ = {0,1,5,6}: two
    components, H₀ = F²; the component of X₁ maps to e₁, that of X₂ to e₂:
    the second example, bars [1,2], [2,3]. For X₂′: {0,1,2} is connected,
    both maps are isomorphisms F → F: a single bar [1,3]. Zigzags thus
    track features through data that is not nested (time series of
    point clouds, bootstrap subsamples).
    (c) Dimensions: 0 at (0,0), 1 at (1,0) and (0,1), 2 at (1,1), in both.
    Rank of (1,0) → (1,1) and of (0,1) → (1,1): 1 in both; (0,0) → any:
    0. So equal rank invariants. In M the images of M(1,0) and M(0,1) in
    M(1,1) ≅ F² are the SAME line (both come from the first summand); in
    N they are two DIFFERENT lines. An isomorphism would carry images to
    images, so M ≇ N.
    (d) Take lines ℓ₁, ..., ℓ₄ ⊂ F² at the incomparable grid points (0,3),
    (1,2), (2,1), (3,0) and let M_u = ∑_{pᵢ ≤ u} ℓᵢ ⊆ F². Isomorphisms
    must match the four lines up to GL₂, which preserves their CROSS-
    RATIO: a continuous invariant, while the rank invariant is the same
    for all choices of four distinct lines. Carlsson-Zomorodian: no
    complete discrete invariant exists; one uses the rank invariant,
    fibred barcodes and Hilbert functions instead.
    Common mistakes: thinking zigzags need all maps injective; believing
    the rank invariant is complete in two parameters (it is in one).
    Now you try: F ←^{1} F →^{0} F →^{1} F. ([1,2], [3,4])

D10. (a) Describe the Mapper algorithm and its relation to the Reeb graph.
    (b) Take the 12 points P_k = (cos 30k°, sin 30k°), filter f = y,
    cover U₁ = [−1, −0.2], U₂ = [−0.6, 0.6], U₃ = [0.2, 1], and single-
    linkage clusters at threshold 0.6. Find the Mapper graph. (c) Redo it
    with the two-interval cover [−1, 0.3], [−0.3, 1] (same data points).
    Answer: (b) 4 nodes, 4 edges: a cycle (β₁ = 1). (c) 2 nodes, 1 edge:
    the loop is lost.
    Teacher explains: (a) Given f : X → ℝ and a cover {Uᵢ} of f(X), split
    each f⁻¹(Uᵢ) into clusters; the Mapper graph has a node per cluster
    and an edge (more generally a simplex) whenever clusters share data
    points. For a continuous f on a space and connected components as
    clusters, this is the nerve of the pulled-back cover refined into
    components; as the cover gets finer it approximates the REEB GRAPH
    (points of X identified when they lie in the same component of a
    level set).
    (b) Adjacent points are 2 sin 15° ≈ 0.518 apart (< 0.6), points two
    steps apart are 1 apart (> 0.6), so clusters = runs of consecutive
    angles. U₁ (sin ≤ −0.2): 210°, 240°, 270°, 300°, 330°: one cluster B.
    U₂ (|sin| ≤ 0.6): 330°, 0°, 30° (right, R) and 150°, 180°, 210° (left,
    L): two clusters (the gap between 30° and 150° is too large). U₃ (sin
    ≥ 0.2): 30°, ..., 150°: one cluster T. Shared points: B ∩ R ∋ 330°, B
    ∩ L ∋ 210°, T ∩ R ∋ 30°, T ∩ L ∋ 150°. Graph B–R–T–L–B: a 4-cycle, 4
    − 4 + 1 = 1 loop, matching the circle.
    (c) f⁻¹[−1, 0.3] = {sin ≤ 0.3} = 180°, 210°, ..., 330°, 0° (30° and
    150° have sin = 0.5): one run, one cluster; similarly the top {sin ≥
    −0.3} is one cluster 0°, 30°, ..., 180°. They share 0° and
    180°, but the nerve records only ONE edge between two clusters: 2
    nodes, 1 edge, a tree. The overlap region has two components that
    Mapper cannot see at this resolution.
    Common mistakes: counting an edge per shared point; clustering the
    whole data set instead of each preimage; reading Mapper loops as
    proven homology (they depend on the cover and clustering scale).
    Now you try: same data, filter f = y, one interval [−1, 1]. (a single
    node: all points in one cluster)

D11. (a) Prove that χ(K_t) = ∑(−1)ᵏβₖ(t) and deduce the Euler
    characteristic curve from a barcode. (b) Compute the ECC of the Rips
    filtration of the regular hexagon of side 1 by counting simplices and
    check it against the barcode H₀ {[0,1)⁵, [0,∞)}, H₁ {[1,√3)}, H₂
    {[√3, 2)}. (c) ECC of the Čech filtration of the unit square. (d)
    Give two filtrations with different barcodes and the same ECC.
    Answer: (b) χ = 6 on [0,1), 0 on [1,√3), 2 on [√3, 2), 1 on [2,∞).
    (c) 4 on [0,½), 0 on [½, √2/2), 1 on [√2/2, ∞). (d) a point versus a
    point with a circle and a 2-sphere wedged on during [1, 2).
    Teacher explains: (a) Rank-nullity: nₖ = dim Zₖ + dim Bₖ₋₁ and βₖ =
    dim Zₖ − dim Bₖ; so ∑(−1)ᵏnₖ = ∑(−1)ᵏ(βₖ + dim Bₖ + dim Bₖ₋₁) and the
    B terms telescope. βₖ(t) = number of k-bars containing t, so χ(t) =
    ∑ over bars (−1)^{dim}·𝟙_{[b,d)}(t). It needs only counting of
    simplices: O(n) time, no reduction.
    (b) t < 1: 6 vertices, χ = 6. At 1: the 6 sides, 6 − 6 = 0. At √3 the
    6 short diagonals: 12 edges; the triangles are the 8 triples without
    an antipodal pair (2 choices from each of the 3 antipodal pairs: 2³ =
    8): the octahedron, 6 − 12 + 8 = 2. At 2: everything, a 5-simplex,
    χ = 1. Barcode: [0,1): β₀ = 6 → 6; [1,√3): β₀ − β₁ = 1 − 1 = 0; [√3,
    2): β₀ + β₂ = 2; [2,∞): 1. ✓
    (c) 4 points; at ½ the 4 sides: 0 (= 1 − 1); at √2/2 the diagonals,
    all 4 triangles and the tetrahedron: 4 − 6 + 4 − 1 = 1.
    (d) K_t = a point for all t, versus: a point v for t < 1; at t = 1 add
    a hollow triangle and a hollow tetrahedron, both through v; at t = 2
    fill the triangle and the tetrahedron. Barcodes: H₀ [0,∞) against H₀
    [0,∞), H₁ [1,2), H₂ [1,2); both curves are ≡ 1 (on [1,2): 1 − 1 + 1).
    The ECC forgets how classes pair and cancels odd against even degree.
    Common mistakes: counting the octahedron triangles as C(6,3) = 20;
    forgetting the sign of odd-dimensional simplices; expecting the ECC
    to detect a loop that coexists with a void.
    Now you try: ECC of Rips on {0, 1, 3, 7} ⊂ ℝ. (4, 3, 2, 1 on [0,1),
    [1,2), [2,4), [4,∞))

D12. (a) Define the persistence landscape and explain why it is useful for
    statistics. (b) For D = {(0,4), (1,3), (2,6)} compute λ₁, λ₂ at t = 1,
    2, 2.5, 3, 4 and ∫λ₁, ∫λ₂, ∫λ₃. (c) Show that the average of the
    landscapes of {(0,2)} and {(0,4)} is not the landscape of any diagram.
    (d) Sliding windows: for f(t) = cos t let SW(t) = (f(t), f(t + τ)),
    sampled at t = 2πk/12. Describe the H₁ Rips bar for τ = π/2 and τ = π.
    Answer: (b) λ₁ = 1, 2, 1.5, 1, 2; λ₂ = 0, 1, 0.5, 1, 0; ∫λ₁ = 7, ∫λ₂
    = 7/4, ∫λ₃ = 1/4. (c) it has slope −½. (d) τ = π/2: regular 12-gon of
    radius 1, H₁ [2 sin 15°, √3) ≈ [0.518, 1.732); τ = π: no H₁.
    Teacher explains: (a) Each point (b, d) gives the tent Λ(t) = max(0,
    min(t − b, d − t)), peak (d − b)/2 at the midpoint; λₖ(t) = k-th
    largest tent value. The map D ↦ (λₖ) is injective, 1-Lipschitz from
    d_B to sup norm, and lands in the vector space Lᵖ(ℕ × ℝ): means,
    variances, a law of large numbers and CLT and permutation tests all
    make sense there, unlike in diagram space where Fréchet means may be
    non-unique.
    (b) Tents: A of (0,4) peak 2 at 2; B of (1,3) peak 1 at 2; C of (2,6)
    peak 2 at 4. t = 1: A = 1, B = 0, C = 0. t = 2: 2, 1, 0. t = 2.5: 1.5,
    0.5, 0.5. t = 3: 1, 0, 1. t = 4: 0, 0, 2. Sort. Areas: λ₁ = A on [0,
    3], C on [3, 6]: ∫ = 2 + 1.5 + 1.5 + 2 = 7. λ₂ = B on [1,2], max(B, C)
    on [2,3] (area 2·∫₂^{2.5}(3 − t)dt = 0.75), A on [3,4] (area ½): 1.75.
    λ₃ = min(B, C) on [2,3]: 0.25. Check: ∑∫λₖ = ∑(d − b)²/4 = 4 + 1 + 4
    = 9 = 7 + 1.75 + 0.25. ✓
    (c) Average: t on [0,1], (2 − t + t)/2 = 1 on [1,2], (4 − t)/2 on [2,4]:
    slope −½, but every landscape is piecewise linear with slopes 0, ±1.
    (d) τ = π/2: SW(t) = (cos t, −sin t), the unit circle; 12 equally
    spaced points: the loop is born at the side 2 sin 15° ≈ 0.518 and
    dies when the equilateral triangles (chord √3, four steps) appear:
    persistence ≈ 1.21, a strong periodicity signal (Perea-Harer). τ =
    π: (cos t, −cos t) lies on a segment: no H₁. Significance: with a
    bootstrap bound c for d_B, only bars longer than 2c are kept.
    Common mistakes: averaging diagrams point by point; using λ₁ only
    (it ignores all but the most persistent feature locally); forgetting
    that landscapes of H₀ with infinite bars must be truncated first.
    Now you try: λ₁ and ∫λ₁ for {(0,4), (2,6)}. (tent up to 2 at t = 2,
    down to 1 at 3, up to 2 at 4, down to 0 at 6; ∫ = 7)

PART B — MATHEMATICAL ONLY
--------------------------
M1. Complexes from data (VR_t: diameter ≤ t; Č_r: balls of radius r).
    Give the numbers of simplices (f-vector) and β₀, β₁ where asked.
    a) X = {0, 1, 3, 7} ⊂ ℝ: VR₂(X)        b) same X: VR₄(X)
    c) same X: Č₁(X)                     d) unit square corners: VR₁
    e) unit square: VR_{√2}              f) unit square: Č_{0.6}

    i) (0,0), (2,0), (1, ½): the smallest r with the triangle in Č_r

    l) same hexagon: Č₁ (number of simplices)

      f) (4, 4): β₁ = 1 (diagonals need √2/2 ≈ 0.707)
      g) Č_{0.55}: (3, 3), β₁ = 1 (needs 1/√3 ≈ 0.577); VR₁: (3, 3, 1)
      h) no (13/6 ≈ 2.167 needed; edges need 2, √13/2); yes
      i) r = 1 (obtuse: half the longest side)
      j) (6, 12, 8): the octahedron, χ = 2, β = (1, 0, 1)
      k) (6, 6): a hexagon, β₁ = 1   l) all 2⁶ − 1 = 63 (centre in all)
      m) 2⁵ − 1 = 31
      n) (4, 5, 2): the long diagonal (4) is missing; contractible
      o) VR yes; Čech no (equilateral triangle at r ∈ [½, 1/√3))
      p) VR₁: full 3-simplex (4, 6, 4, 1); Č_{0.6}: (4, 6, 4) = hollow
