      s) 0.7 and 1.5 (longer than 2c = 0.6)
      t) yes (a circle); no (a segment)

M12. True (T) or false (F)? Give a counterexample for F.
     a) VR_t(X) is determined by its 1-skeleton.
     b) For finite X ⊂ ℝᵈ, Č_r(X) ≃ ⋃_{x∈X} B̄(x, r).
     c) VR_t(X) ≃ ⋃ B̄(x, t/2) for every finite X ⊂ ℝ².
     d) Rips and Čech barcodes agree after rescaling by a factor 2.
     e) Barcodes do not depend on the coefficient field.
     f) The pairing from matrix reduction does not depend on which
        admissible column additions are made.
     g) Every pointwise finite-dimensional ℝ-indexed persistence module
        is a direct sum of interval modules.
     h) Two ℝ²-indexed modules with the same rank invariant are
        isomorphic.
     i) d_B(D, D′) ≤ W₁(D, D′).
     j) d_B(Dgm f, Dgm g) ≤ ‖f − g‖_∞ for monotone f, g on a finite
        complex.
     k) W₁(Dgm f, Dgm g) ≤ ‖f − g‖_∞ for such f, g.
     l) An essential point may be matched to the diagonal.
     m) The finite H₀ death times of Rips are the MST edge lengths.
     n) If X ⊆ Y then β₁(VR_t(X)) ≤ β₁(VR_t(Y)) for every t.
     o) The alpha complex lies in the Delaunay triangulation.
     p) The witness complex always has the homotopy type of the data
        at some scale.
     q) The Euler characteristic curve determines the barcode.
     r) The Mapper graph does not depend on the cover.
     s) Zigzag modules of type Aₙ decompose into intervals.
     t) The mean of two persistence landscapes is a landscape.
     u) d_I(I[a,b), I[c,d)) = max(|a − c|, |b − d|).
     v) Adding a point to X never decreases β₀(VR_t(X)).
     Answers:
       a) T (flag complex)          b) T (nerve theorem)
       c) F: regular hexagon, t = √3: VR ≃ S², the union ≃ S¹
       d) F: equilateral triangle (Čech H₁ [½, 1/√3), Rips none)
       e) F: RP¹ ⊆ RP² (D4)          f) T (pairing uniqueness)
       g) T (Crawley-Boevey)         h) F: D9 (c)
       i) T                          j) T (stability theorem)
       k) F: f ≡ 0 on a path of 7 vertices, g = (0, ε, 0, ε, 0, ε, 0):
          three bars (0, ε), W₁ = 3ε/2 > ε
       l) F: it must go to an essential point (else cost ∞)
       m) T
       n) F: unit square (β₁ = 1 at t = 1) inside square plus centre
          (β₁ = 0 at t = 1)
       o) T
       p) F: L = W = {0, 10} ⊂ ℝ gives an edge at every scale, although
          the data are two far-apart points
       q) F: D11 (d)                 r) F: D10 (b), (c)
       s) T (Gabriel)                t) F: M11 i) has slope −½
       u) F: I[0,10), I[1,3) have d_I = 5 < 7
       v) F: {0, 2} at t = 1 has β₀ = 2; adding 1 gives β₀ = 1

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: draw everything. For each point cloud draw the growing
  discs (Čech) next to the growing graph with filled cliques (Rips) and
  literally watch components merge and holes open and close; write each
  simplex with its filtration value before touching a matrix. Drill the
  three "entry rules": a Rips simplex enters at its longest edge, a Čech
  triangle at its circumradius if acute and at half its longest side if
  not, and a lower-star simplex at its highest vertex. For H₀ always use
  the elder rule and the minimum spanning tree; for H₁ count β₁ = E − V +
  (components) − (filled triangles that kill loops). Compute bottleneck
  distances by listing, for each point, "match it or send it to the
  diagonal at cost half its length", and check the answer against the
  stability bound. Harder: prove the stability theorem through the
  interleaving and the isometry theorem (induced matchings of Bauer-
  Lesnick); prove Gabriel's theorem for Aₙ zigzags; show that the rank
  invariant is complete for one parameter but not for two, and find a
  continuous family of ℝ²-modules (cross-ratio); prove that VR of the
  circle passes through S¹, S³, S⁵, ... (Adamaszek-Adams); implement
  the reduction algorithm with clearing and compare homology with
  cohomology on the regular 12-gon; and design a permutation test using
  mean persistence landscapes to compare two samples of point clouds.


================================================================================
TOPIC 13.16 — HIGHER CATEGORY THEORY AND HOMOTOPY TYPE THEORY: BICATEGORIES,
SIMPLICIAL SETS, QUASI-CATEGORIES, MODEL CATEGORIES, STABLE ∞-CATEGORIES,
MARTIN-LÖF TYPE THEORY, UNIVALENCE, HIGHER INDUCTIVE TYPES
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - STRICT 2-CATEGORY: objects, 1-morphisms f : x → y, 2-morphisms
    α : f ⇒ g; VERTICAL composition β∘α and HORIZONTAL composition β*α,
    both strictly associative and unital, linked by the INTERCHANGE LAW
    (β′∘α′)*(β∘α) = (β′*β)∘(α′*α). Model example: Cat (categories,
    functors, natural transformations; (β*α)_x = β_{Gx}∘F′(α_x)).
    ECKMANN-HILTON: the 2-endomorphisms of an identity 1-cell form a
    COMMUTATIVE monoid.
  - BICATEGORY: composition associative and unital only up to coherent
    invertible 2-cells (associator a, unitors l, r) satisfying the
    PENTAGON (the 5 bracketings of 4 composable arrows; bracketings of
    n + 1 factors are counted by Catalan numbers 1, 1, 2, 5, 14, 42) and
    TRIANGLE identities. Examples: a monoidal category = a one-object
    bicategory; SPANS A ← S → B (composition by pullback; for finite
    sets a span up to iso is an ℕ-matrix and composition is matrix
    product); RINGS, BIMODULES and bimodule maps (composition ⊗_S);
    Morita equivalence = equivalence in this bicategory (Mₙ(k) ~ k).
    COHERENCE (Mac Lane, Paré): every bicategory is biequivalent to a
    strict 2-category. Pseudofunctors, pseudonatural transformations,
    equivalences and adjunctions inside a 2-category.
  - SIMPLICIAL SETS: functors X : Δᵒᵖ → Set, Δ = finite ordinals [n] =
    {0 < 1 < ... < n} and monotone maps. FACES dᵢ : Xₙ → Xₙ₋₁ (delete i),
    DEGENERACIES sᵢ : Xₙ → Xₙ₊₁ (repeat i); SIMPLICIAL IDENTITIES, e.g.
    dᵢdⱼ = dⱼ₋₁dᵢ for i < j. STANDARD n-SIMPLEX Δ[n] = Hom(−, [n]): it has
    C(n + k + 1, k + 1) k-simplices, C(n + 1, k + 1) of them
    non-degenerate. Boundary ∂Δ[n] (omit the top cell), HORN Λᵏ[n] (omit
    also the k-th face; INNER if 0 < k < n). Eilenberg-Zilber: every
    simplex is uniquely sᵢ...(non-degenerate). GEOMETRIC REALIZATION
    |−| ⊣ Sing; the product Δ[p] × Δ[q] has C(p + q, p) top simplices.
  - KAN COMPLEX: every horn Λᵏ[n] → X (0 ≤ k ≤ n) extends to Δ[n].
    Examples: Sing X for every space X; every simplicial group (Moore);
    N(C) iff C is a groupoid. Non-examples: Δ[n] (n ≥ 1), ∂Δ[2],
    Δ[1]/∂Δ[1]. For Kan X, simplicial homotopy groups πₙ(X, v) agree
    with πₙ(|X|, v). DOLD-KAN: simplicial abelian groups ≃ Ch≥0(ℤ),
    πₙ = Hₙ of the normalized (Moore) complex; K(A, m) = Γ(A[m]) has
    A^{C(n, m)} as its set of n-simplices.
  - NERVE N(C)ₙ = strings c₀ → c₁ → ... → cₙ of composable arrows; N is
    fully faithful with left adjoint τ₁ (the homotopy category).
    CHARACTERIZATION: X is a nerve iff every INNER horn has a UNIQUE
    filler. N(G) for a group G = BG (|G|ⁿ n-simplices, (|G| − 1)ⁿ
    non-degenerate; |N(G)| = K(G, 1), e.g. |N(ℤ/2)| = ℝP^∞). Nerve of a
    poset = order complex; C with an initial or terminal object ⇒ |N(C)|
    contractible; an adjunction induces a homotopy equivalence of nerves.
  - QUASI-CATEGORY (Boardman-Vogt, Joyal, Lurie): every INNER horn has a
    (not necessarily unique) filler. Fillers of Λ¹[2] are "choices of
    composite", unique up to a contractible space of choices. HOMOTOPY
    CATEGORY hC: objects X₀, morphisms homotopy classes of edges.
    JOYAL: a quasi-category is a Kan complex iff hC is a groupoid.
  - (∞,1)-CATEGORIES: categories with mapping SPACES and all morphisms
    above dimension 1 invertible. Equivalent models (Bergner, Joyal-
    Tierney, Lurie): quasi-categories, simplicial (Kan-enriched)
    categories (via the homotopy coherent nerve), complete Segal spaces,
    Segal categories, relative categories. hC(x, y) = π₀ Map(x, y).
    HOMOTOPY HYPOTHESIS: ∞-groupoids = Kan complexes = homotopy types.
    The ∞-category S of spaces; the core of Fin is ⊔ₙ BΣₙ (groupoid
    cardinality ∑ 1/n! = e).
  - MODEL CATEGORY: classes W (weak equivalences, 2-out-of-3), Cof, Fib,
    closed under retracts, lifting (Cof ⧄ Fib∩W, Cof∩W ⧄ Fib) and
    functorial factorizations. Ho(M) = M[W⁻¹] = (cofibrant-fibrant
    objects)/homotopy. Examples: Top (Quillen/Serre); sSet KAN-QUILLEN
    (cof = mono, fibrant = Kan); sSet JOYAL (cof = mono, fibrant =
    quasi-categories); Ch≥0(R) PROJECTIVE (W = quasi-isos, cofibrant =
    degreewise projective), Ho = D≥0(R).
  - QUILLEN ADJUNCTION F ⊣ G: F preserves cofibrations and trivial
    cofibrations (⇔ G preserves fibrations and trivial fibrations); it
    gives derived adjunction LF ⊣ RG. QUILLEN EQUIVALENCE when this is an
    equivalence of homotopy categories: |−| ⊣ Sing (sSet ⇄ Top), Dold-
    Kan, Joyal ⇄ complete Segal spaces. Derived functors: Torₙ = Hₙ of
    ⊗ᴸ, Extⁿ = H⁻ⁿ of RHom; KEN BROWN: left Quillen functors preserve
    weak equivalences between cofibrant objects.
  - HOMOTOPY LIMITS/COLIMITS: the derived functors of lim/colim (replace
    the diagram cofibrantly/fibrantly first); the (∞-categorical) limits
    and colimits. Strict colim(* ← S¹ → *) = *, but hocolim = ΣS¹ = S².
    hocolim(* ← X → *) = ΣX, hocolim(X ← X × Y → Y) = X * Y (join),
    holim(* → X ← *) = ΩX, homotopy FIBER of f; the hocolim over BG of
    a G-space is X_{hG} = EG ×_G X, with *_{hG} = BG. Mapping telescopes
    (hocolim of ℤ →2 ℤ →2 ... is ℤ[1/2]); Milnor lim¹ sequences.
    χ(hocolim(B ← A → C)) = χ(B) + χ(C) − χ(A).
  - STABLE ∞-CATEGORY: pointed (zero object), has fibers and cofibers,
    and a square is a pullback iff it is a pushout; equivalently finite
    limits/colimits and Σ is an equivalence (Ω = Σ⁻¹). Its homotopy
    category is TRIANGULATED (cofiber sequences = exact triangles,
    octahedral axiom automatic). Examples: spectra Sp (πₙS: ℤ, ℤ/2, ℤ/2,
    ℤ/24, 0, 0, ℤ/2, ℤ/240), D(R), Stab of pointed ∞-categories. In
    D(ℤ): cofib(ℤ →n ℤ) = ℤ/n, [A, B[k]] = Extᵏ(A, B).
  - MARTIN-LÖF TYPE THEORY: judgments Γ ⊢ a : A, Γ ⊢ a ≡ b : A; type
    formers Π (functions), Σ (pairs), +, 0, 1, Bool, ℕ, universes U with
    formation, introduction, elimination (induction), computation (β)
    and uniqueness (η) rules. PROPOSITIONS-AS-TYPES (Curry-Howard):
    ¬A := A → 0; constructive logic (¬¬(A + ¬A) provable, LEM not). The
    type-theoretic "axiom of choice" (Π x Σ y R) → (Σ f Π x R(x, f x)) is
    PROVABLE.
  - IDENTITY TYPES a =_A b with refl_a and PATH INDUCTION (J): to prove
    C(x, y, p) for all paths it suffices to do C(x, x, refl). Derived:
    inverse p⁻¹, concatenation p·q, ap_f, transport^P(p, −) : P(x) →
    P(y), dependent paths. Groupoid laws hold up to higher paths (types
    are ∞-GROUPOIDS); ECKMANN-HILTON: Ω²(A, a) is commutative. UIP is
    NOT provable (Hofmann-Streicher groupoid model).
  - H-LEVELS: isContr A := Σ(a : A) Π(x : A) a = x (level −2); PROP (−1):
    all x, y equal; SET (0): all x = y are props; n-TYPE: all path types
    are (n − 1)-types (Voevodsky h-level n + 2). Cumulative; closed
    under Σ, Π, ×, retracts; isContr A, isProp A, isEquiv f are props.
    HEDBERG: decidable equality ⇒ set (ℕ, Bool, ℤ are sets). EQUIVALENCE
    A ≃ B: f with contractible fibers ⇔ bi-invertible ⇔ half-adjoint.
  - UNIVALENCE (Voevodsky): idtoeqv : (A =_U B) → (A ≃ B) is an
    equivalence, with inverse ua; transport along ua(e) is e. Implies
    FUNCTION EXTENSIONALITY and propositional extensionality; (Bool =
    Bool) ≃ Bool, so U is NOT a set; (Fin n = Fin n) has n! elements;
    the universe of n-types is an (n + 1)-type. Consistent via the
    Kan-complex model (Voevodsky); computational in cubical type theory.
    Incompatible with UIP for U.
  - HIGHER INDUCTIVE TYPES: CIRCLE S¹ (base, loop : base = base; to map
    out give b : B and ℓ : b = b); INTERVAL; SUSPENSION ΣA (N, S, merid :
    A → N = S), ΣBool ≃ S¹, Sⁿ⁺¹ := ΣSⁿ, Σ1 contractible, Σ(Fin 3) ≃ S¹ ∨
    S¹; pushouts, torus T² (π₁ = ℤ²), truncations as HITs.
  - π₁(S¹) = ℤ IN HoTT (Licata-Shulman): the UNIVERSAL COVER code : S¹ →
    U, code(base) = ℤ, ap_code(loop) = ua(succ). ENCODE(p) =
    transport^code(p, 0), DECODE(n) = loopⁿ; they are inverse, so
    Ω(S¹, base) ≃ ℤ, S¹ is a 1-type, loop ≠ refl, πₖ(S¹) = 0 for k ≥ 2.
  - TRUNCATIONS ‖A‖ₙ: the universal n-type under A; ‖A‖₋₁ = propositional
    truncation ("there exists", mere inhabitation); πₙ(A, a) :=
    ‖Ωⁿ(A, a)‖₀. A is n-CONNECTED if ‖A‖ₙ is contractible; Sⁿ is
    (n − 1)-connected. Synthetic results: πₙ(Sⁿ) = ℤ (Freudenthal), π₂(S²)
    = π₃(S²) = ℤ (Hopf fibration), π₄(S³) = ℤ/2 (Brunerie), van Kampen,
    Blakers-Massey.
  - ENCODE-DECODE METHOD: to compute a =_A x, guess a family code : A → U
    with c₀ : code(a), set encode(p) = transport^code(p, c₀), define
    decode by induction and check both composites. Examples: (inl a =
    inl a′) ≃ (a = a′), (inl a = inr b) ≃ 0; (m =_ℕ n) ≃ code(m, n) with
    code(0, 0) = 1, code(0, n + 1) = 0, code(m + 1, n + 1) = code(m, n)
    (so 0 ≠ 1 and succ is injective); paths in the circle, in S¹ ∨ S¹
    (free group F₂), in truncations.

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. (a) Prove ECKMANN-HILTON: in a strict 2-category, any two 2-cells
    α, β : 1ₓ ⇒ 1ₓ satisfy α∘β = α*β = β∘α. Deduce that End(I) is
    commutative in any strict monoidal category. (b) In the bicategory
    of spans of finite sets, a span A ← S → B is recorded by the matrix
    M_{ab} = #(fibre of S over (a, b)). Let S : A → B have matrix
    [[1, 2], [0, 1]] and T : B → C have matrix [[1, 0], [3, 1]]. Find
    the matrix and the size of the apex of T∘S, and explain why
    composition is associative only up to a 2-cell. (c) How many
    bracketings do 5 composable 1-cells have? What polytope organizes
    the associators between them?
    Answer: (a) proof below. (b) matrix MN = [[7, 2], [3, 1]], apex of
    size 13; associator ((s, t), u) ↦ (s, (t, u)). (c) Catalan C₄ = 14;
    the associahedron K₅ (14 vertices, 21 edges, 9 faces: 6 pentagons
    and 3 squares).
    Teacher explains: (a) Whiskering by an identity 2-cell does nothing:
    α*1₁ₓ = α = 1₁ₓ*α. The interchange law (β′∘α′)*(β∘α) =
    (β′*β)∘(α′*α) does all the work:
       α*β = (α∘1)*(1∘β) = (α*1)∘(1*β) = α∘β,
       α*β = (1∘α)*(β∘1) = (1*β)∘(α*1) = β∘α.
    So both compositions coincide and are commutative. A strict
    monoidal category is a one-object 2-category (objects ↦ 1-cells, ⊗ ↦
    horizontal composition, I ↦ 1_*), and End(I) = 2-cells 1_* ⇒ 1_*,
    hence commutative (in the non-strict case use the unitors).
    (b) The composite is the pullback S ×_B T → A × C. Over (a, c) its
    fibre is ⊔_b S_{ab} × T_{bc}, of size ∑_b M_{ab}N_{bc} = (MN)_{ac}:
       MN = [[1·1 + 2·3, 1·0 + 2·1], [0·1 + 1·3, 0·0 + 1·1]]
          = [[7, 2], [3, 1]],  apex size 7 + 2 + 3 + 1 = 13.
    For a third span U, (U∘T)∘S has apex S ×_B (T ×_C U), elements
    (s, (t, u)), while U∘(T∘S) has elements ((s, t), u): different sets,
    canonically isomorphic. That isomorphism is the ASSOCIATOR; the
    identity span B ← B → B (matrix I) is a unit only up to the
    isomorphisms S ×_B B ≅ S (unitors). Matrices forget this and see a
    strictly associative product.
    (c) Bracketings of n + 1 factors are counted by the Catalan number
    Cₙ = C(2n, n)/(n + 1): 1, 1, 2, 5, 14 for n = 0, ..., 4. Four arrows
    give the 5 vertices of Mac Lane's PENTAGON; five give the 14
    vertices of K₅, whose 2-faces are pentagons (one inner bracketing
    fixed: 6 such) and squares (two independent reassociations: 3);
    edges 21, and 14 − 21 + 9 = 2 (a sphere). COHERENCE says every
    diagram built from associators commutes; the pentagon suffices.
    Common mistakes: thinking Eckmann-Hilton applies to 2-cells between
    arbitrary 1-cells (it needs identity 1-cells); multiplying the
    matrices in the order NM; believing a bicategory is "just" a
    2-category (true only up to biequivalence).
    Now you try: the span on a 2-element set with matrix M = [[1, 1],
    [1, 0]] composed with itself three times: matrix and apex size.
    (M³ = [[3, 2], [2, 1]], 8 elements)

D2. (a) Show that Δ[n] has C(n + k + 1, k + 1) k-simplices, of which
    C(n + 1, k + 1) are non-degenerate; list the numbers for Δ[2],
    k = 0, 1, 2, 3. (b) Count the non-degenerate simplices of ∂Δ[3] and
    of the horn Λ¹[3] and compute their Euler characteristics.
    (c) Count the non-degenerate simplices of Δ[1] × Δ[1] and Δ[1] ×
    Δ[2]; check the identity d₁d₃ = d₂d₁ on the 3-simplex (0, 1, 2, 3).
    Answer: (a) Δ[2]: 3, 6, 10, 15 simplices; 3, 3, 1, 0 non-degenerate.
    (b) ∂Δ[3]: 4, 6, 4, χ = 2; Λ¹[3]: 4, 6, 3, χ = 1. (c) 4, 5, 2 and
    6, 12, 10, 3; both sides give (0, 2).
    Teacher explains: (a) A k-simplex of Δ[n] = Hom_Δ([k], [n]) is a
    sequence 0 ≤ a₀ ≤ a₁ ≤ ... ≤ a_k ≤ n, i.e. a multiset of size k + 1
    from n + 1 values: C((n + 1) + (k + 1) − 1, k + 1) = C(n + k + 1,
    k + 1). It is degenerate iff some aᵢ = aᵢ₊₁ (it factors through a
    codegeneracy), so the non-degenerate ones are the strictly
    increasing sequences, i.e. subsets: C(n + 1, k + 1). For n = 2:
    C(3,1), C(4,2), C(5,3), C(6,4) = 3, 6, 10, 15; C(3, k + 1) = 3, 3, 1, 0.
    (b) ∂Δ[3] = all proper faces: 4 vertices, 6 edges, 4 triangles, and
    χ = 4 − 6 + 4 = 2, as |∂Δ[3]| ≅ S². The horn Λ¹[3] also omits the
    face d₁ = (0, 2, 3), so 4, 6, 3 and χ = 1; indeed every horn is
    contractible (it deformation retracts onto the cone point k).
    (c) Δ[p] × Δ[q] = N([p] × [q]), so non-degenerate k-simplices are
    strictly increasing chains of length k + 1 in the product poset.
    [1] × [1]: vertices 4; edges = comparable pairs: 4 sides and the
    diagonal (0,0) < (1,1), total 5; triangles = maximal chains = 2.
    χ = 4 − 5 + 2 = 1. [1] × [2]: 6 vertices, 12 edges, 10 triangles, 3
    tetrahedra (maximal chains = lattice paths = C(3, 1)), χ = 6 − 12 +
    10 − 3 = 1. Faces: d₃(0, 1, 2, 3) = (0, 1, 2), then d₁ gives (0, 2);
    d₁(0, 1, 2, 3) = (0, 2, 3), then d₂ gives (0, 2). This is dᵢdⱼ =
    dⱼ₋₁dᵢ with i = 1, j = 3.
    Common mistakes: counting only non-degenerate simplices when asked
    for all of Δ[n]ₖ; forgetting the diagonal edge of the square;
    thinking a horn is a sphere (it is a disc).
    Now you try: non-degenerate simplices of Δ[2] × Δ[2], and the number
    of k-simplices of S¹ = Δ[1]/∂Δ[1]. (9, 27, 37, 24, 6 with χ = 1;
    k + 1)

D3. (a) Prove that the nerve N(C) is a Kan complex iff C is a groupoid.
    (b) Show that Δ[1] and S¹ = Δ[1]/∂Δ[1] are not Kan complexes but
    Sing X is Kan for every space X. (c) By Dold-Kan, K(A, m) is the
    simplicial abelian group Γ(A[m]) with n-simplices A^{C(n, m)}.
    Count the n-simplices of K(ℤ/2, 1) and K(ℤ/2, 2) for n = 0, ..., 4
    and state their homotopy groups.
    Answer: (a) proof below. (b) explicit unfillable horns below.
    (c) K(ℤ/2, 1) = N(ℤ/2): 1, 2, 4, 8, 16; K(ℤ/2, 2): 1, 1, 2, 8, 64;
    π₁ = ℤ/2 resp. π₂ = ℤ/2, all other πₙ = 0.
    Teacher explains: (a) Suppose N(C) is Kan. Given f : c₀ → c₁, the
    outer horn Λ⁰[2] with edge 01 = f and edge 02 = id_{c₀} has a
    filler, i.e. h : c₁ → c₀ with h∘f = id: f has a left inverse. The
    horn Λ²[2] (edges 02 = id_{c₁}, 12 = f) gives a right inverse, so f
    is invertible. Conversely let C be a groupoid. Λ⁰[2]: given f : 0 →
    1, g : 0 → 2 take h = g∘f⁻¹; Λ²[2] similarly. In dimension 3 every
    edge ij lies in two faces dₘ (m ∉ {i, j}), so the horn contains all
    edges, and the missing face commutes after cancelling an invertible
    arrow (e.g. k = 0: e₁₃∘f₁ = f₃f₂f₁ gives e₁₃ = f₃f₂). For n ≥ 4 the
    horn contains the whole 2-skeleton, and a simplex of a nerve is
    determined by (and exists for) compatible 2-skeleta.
    (b) Map Λ⁰[2] → Δ[1] by vertices 0 ↦ 0, 1 ↦ 1, 2 ↦ 0: the edges
    01 ↦ (0, 1) and 02 ↦ (0, 0) exist, but a filler would be the
    non-monotone (0, 1, 0). In S¹ let σ be the loop; the horn Λ¹[2] with
    both edges σ needs a 2-simplex with d₂ = d₀ = σ, but S¹ has only
    the degenerate 2-simplices s₀σ (faces σ, σ, deg) and s₁σ (faces deg,
    σ, σ) and s₀s₀(v). For Sing X: a horn Λᵏ[n] → Sing X is a map
    |Λᵏ[n]| → X, and |Λᵏ[n]| is a retract of |Δ[n]| (project radially
    from a point beyond the missing face), so compose with the retraction.
    (c) Γ(C)ₙ = ⊕ over surjections [n] ↠ [k] of Cₖ; for C = A[m] only
    k = m contributes and monotone surjections [n] ↠ [m] correspond to
    choosing m of the n "jumps": C(n, m). So K(ℤ/2, 1)ₙ = (ℤ/2)ⁿ, sizes
    1, 2, 4, 8, 16 (= |N(ℤ/2)ₙ|), and K(ℤ/2, 2)ₙ = (ℤ/2)^{C(n,2)}, sizes
    2⁰, 2⁰, 2¹, 2³, 2⁶ = 1, 1, 2, 8, 64. Simplicial groups are Kan and
    πₙ = Hₙ(normalized complex) = Hₙ(A[m]), which is A in degree m.
    Common mistakes: using an inner horn to show Δ[1] is not Kan (Δ[1]
    is a quasi-category: inner horns do fill); forgetting that K(A, m)
    has a single vertex and trivial (m − 1)-skeleton.
    Now you try: |K(ℤ/3, 2)ₙ| for n = 3, 4, and |K(ℤ, 3)₃|.
    (27 and 729; ℤ, countably infinite)

D4. (a) Show that every inner horn in N(C) has exactly one filler, and
    conversely that a simplicial set with unique fillers for Λ¹[2],
    Λ¹[3], Λ²[3] (and higher inner horns) is the nerve of a category.
    Why does Sing X have non-unique fillers? (b) Let C have objects 0, 1
    and two non-identity arrows a, b : 0 → 1. Find #N(C)ₙ, the
    non-degenerate simplices and the homotopy type of |N(C)|. (c) Show
    that |N(ℤ/2)| has one non-degenerate simplex in each dimension and
    identify it; what is τ₁ of Sing X?
    Answer: (a) below. (b) #N(C)ₙ = 2n + 2; non-degenerate: 2 vertices,
    2 edges; |N(C)| ≃ S¹. (c) ℝP^∞ = K(ℤ/2, 1); τ₁ Sing X = Π₁(X), the
    fundamental groupoid.
    Teacher explains: (a) Λ¹[2] → N(C) is a pair f : c₀ → c₁,
    g : c₁ → c₂; a 2-simplex of N(C) is a composable pair whose d₁ face
    is the composite, so the unique filler has long edge g∘f. For
    Λ¹[3] and Λ²[3] the edges are determined by the 2-faces present,
    and the filler exists and is unique precisely because composition
    is associative: h(gf) = (hg)f. Conversely given X with unique inner
    fillers, set objects X₀, morphisms X₁, identities s₀, and define g∘f
    := d₁ of the unique filler of (f, g); uniqueness for Λ¹[3], Λ²[3]
    gives associativity, degenerate simplices give unit laws, and X ≅
    N(C) since simplices are determined by their spines (the 2-
    COSKELETAL property). In Sing X a filler of (f, g) is any path
    homotopic to f·g together with a homotopy: many choices, but a
    contractible space of them. That is the quasi-categorical idea.
    (b) An n-simplex is a string of n composable arrows. Since a and b
    cannot be composed with a non-identity arrow, a string is either all
    identities at 0 or at 1 (2 strings) or contains exactly one of a, b
    in one of n positions (2n strings): 2n + 2 (n = 1: id₀, id₁, a, b).
    Non-degenerate simplices contain no identities: vertices 0, 1 and
    edges a, b; all higher simplices are degenerate. So |N(C)| is two
    vertices joined by two edges: a circle, with π₁|N(C)| = ℤ, although
    C has no loops at all.
    (c) N(ℤ/2)ₙ = (ℤ/2)ⁿ; non-degenerate means no entry equals the
    identity, leaving only (g, g, ..., g): one cell per dimension. The
    CW complex |N(ℤ/2)| = K(ℤ/2, 1) with one cell in each dimension is
    ℝP^∞ (H₁ = ℤ/2, H₂ = 0, H₃ = ℤ/2, ...). τ₁(Sing X) has objects the
    points and morphisms paths up to homotopy rel endpoints: Π₁(X).
    Common mistakes: "every quasi-category is a nerve" (fillers need not
    be unique); counting identity arrows as non-degenerate edges;
    confusing N(C) Kan (C groupoid) with N(C) quasi-category (always).
    Now you try: #N(ℤ/3)ₙ and its non-degenerate count; the non-
    degenerate simplices of the barycentric subdivision N(faces of Δ[2]).
    (3ⁿ and 2ⁿ; 7, 12, 6)

D5. (a) Explain in what sense a quasi-category C is an (∞,1)-category:
    what are the mapping spaces, what does "composition is defined up
    to a contractible space of choices" mean, and what is hC? State
    Joyal's theorem and the homotopy hypothesis. (b) In the ∞-category
    S of spaces, determine π₀ and the components of Map(S¹, S¹),
    Map(Bℤ/3, Bℤ/3) and Map(BΣ₃, BΣ₃). (c) The groupoid cardinality of
    an ∞-groupoid with finitely many components, each a BGᵢ, is
    ∑ 1/|Gᵢ|. Compute it for the core of finite sets, for groups of
    order 4 and for 2-dimensional vector spaces over F₂.
    Answer: (a) below. (b) Map(S¹, S¹) ≃ ℤ × S¹; Map(Bℤ/3, Bℤ/3) ≃
    3 copies of Bℤ/3; Map(BΣ₃, BΣ₃) ≃ BΣ₃ ⊔ Bℤ/2 ⊔ * (3 components).
    (c) e = 2.71828...; 1/2 + 1/6 = 2/3; 1/6.
    Teacher explains: (a) Objects are vertices, morphisms edges. For
    x, y the RIGHT MAPPING SPACE has k-simplices the (k + 1)-simplices
    with last vertex y whose face on the first k + 1 vertices is
    totally degenerate at x; it is a
    Kan complex, well defined up to homotopy equivalence, with
    π₀ Map(x, y) = hC(x, y). A pair f : x → y, g : y → z gives an inner
    horn Λ¹[2]; the space of fillers (the fibre of Fun(Δ[2], C) →
    Fun(Λ¹[2], C), a trivial Kan fibration) is CONTRACTIBLE, so "g∘f"
    exists and any two choices are joined by a homotopy, unique up to
    higher homotopy, and so on. hC has objects C₀ and morphisms edges
    modulo f ~ f′ iff some 2-simplex has d₂ = f, d₁ = f′, d₀ = id_y.
    JOYAL: C is a Kan complex iff hC is a groupoid; so Kan complexes =
    ∞-GROUPOIDS, and the HOMOTOPY HYPOTHESIS (Sing, |−| a Quillen
    equivalence) identifies them with homotopy types of CW complexes.
    (b) S¹ is a topological group, so ev : Map(S¹, S¹) → S¹ is a trivial
    bundle with fibre Map_*(S¹, S¹) = ΩS¹ ≃ ℤ: Map ≃ ℤ × S¹, components
    indexed by the degree. For groups, Map(BG, BH) ≃ ⊔ B C_H(φ) over
    Hom(G, H)/(conjugation by H). G = H = ℤ/3: three homomorphisms
    (0, id, −id), H abelian so no identifications, centralizers ℤ/3:
    three copies of Bℤ/3. G = H = Σ₃: homomorphisms up to conjugacy are
    the trivial one (centralizer Σ₃), those with image of order 2 (all
    3 transpositions conjugate: one class, centralizer of a
    transposition ℤ/2), and automorphisms (all inner: one class,
    centralizer Z(Σ₃) = 1). No image has order 3: Σ₃ has no normal
    subgroup of order 2. Total: 3 components.
    (c) core(Fin) = ⊔ₙ BΣₙ: ∑ 1/n! = e. Groups of order 4: ℤ/4 with
    |Aut| = 2 and ℤ/2 × ℤ/2 with |Aut| = |GL₂(F₂)| = 6: 1/2 + 1/6 = 2/3.
    F₂² has automorphism group GL₂(F₂) of order (4 − 1)(4 − 2) = 6: 1/6.
    Common mistakes: taking hC(x, y) to be Map(x, y) itself; treating
    homomorphisms rather than conjugacy classes as components; forgetting
    that each component of Map(BG, BH) is itself a K(π, 1).
    Now you try: groupoid cardinality of 2-element sets and of
    1-dimensional F₃-vector spaces; π₀ Map(Bℤ/2, Bℤ/4). (1/2; 1/2;
    2 components)

D6. (a) State the axioms of a model category, describe Ho(M), and
    define Quillen adjunctions and Quillen equivalences. Why is
    |−| ⊣ Sing a Quillen equivalence, while id : sSet_Joyal → sSet_Quillen
    is a left Quillen functor that is not one? (b) In Ch≥0(ℤ) with the
    projective model structure, use the cofibrant replacement P = (ℤ →4
    ℤ) → ℤ/4 to compute ℤ/4 ⊗ᴸ ℤ/6 and RHom(ℤ/4, ℤ/6). (c) Show that the
    strict tensor product does not preserve quasi-isomorphisms.
    Answer: (a) below. (b) H₀ = ℤ/2, H₁ = ℤ/2 (Tor₀, Tor₁); H⁰ = ℤ/2
    (Hom), H¹ = ℤ/2 (Ext¹). (c) P → ℤ/4 is a quasi-iso, but P ⊗ ℤ/2
    has H₁ = ℤ/2 while ℤ/4 ⊗ ℤ/2 has none.
    Teacher explains: (a) M has limits and colimits and classes W, Cof,
    Fib such that: W has 2-out-of-3; all three are closed under
    retracts; Cof has the left lifting property against Fib ∩ W and
    Cof ∩ W against Fib; every map factors as Cof ∩ W then Fib and as
    Cof then Fib ∩ W. Ho(M) = M[W⁻¹] exists and Ho(M)(X, Y) = [QX, RY],
    homotopy classes from a cofibrant to a fibrant replacement. A
    Quillen adjunction F ⊣ G has F preserving Cof and Cof ∩ W; then
    LF = F∘Q ⊣ RG = G∘R on Ho. It is a Quillen EQUIVALENCE if for
    cofibrant X and fibrant Y, FX → Y is a weak equivalence iff
    X → GY is. For |−| ⊣ Sing: |−| sends monos to relative CW
    inclusions and horn inclusions to deformation retracts; the unit
    X → Sing|X| and counit |Sing Y| → Y are weak equivalences (Quillen,
    Milnor). Joyal vs Kan-Quillen: same cofibrations (monos), and every
    Joyal equivalence is a weak homotopy equivalence, so id is left
    Quillen; but Δ[1] → Δ[0] is a Kan-Quillen equivalence and not a
    Joyal one (h Δ[1] = [1] is not equivalent to a point).
    (b) P is degreewise free, hence cofibrant. P ⊗ ℤ/6 = (ℤ/6 →4 ℤ/6).
    Kernel: 4x ≡ 0 (mod 6) ⇔ x ∈ {0, 3}, so H₁ = ℤ/2. Image of ×4 is
    {0, 2, 4}, so H₀ = ℤ/6 / 2ℤ/6 = ℤ/2. Hom(P, ℤ/6) = (ℤ/6 →4 ℤ/6) in
    cohomological degrees 0, 1: H⁰ = ker = ℤ/2 = Hom(ℤ/4, ℤ/6), H¹ =
    coker = ℤ/2 = Ext¹(ℤ/4, ℤ/6). In general Tor₁ and Ext¹ of ℤ/m, ℤ/n
    are ℤ/gcd(m, n).
    (c) P → ℤ/4 is a quasi-iso (H₀P = ℤ/4, H₁P = 0). Tensoring with
    ℤ/2 gives ℤ/2 →0 ℤ/2, homology ℤ/2 in degrees 0 and 1, while
    ℤ/4 ⊗ ℤ/2 = ℤ/2 in degree 0 only. So ⊗ must be DERIVED: ℤ/2 is
    not cofibrant, and Ken Brown's lemma only guarantees invariance
    between cofibrant objects.
    Common mistakes: forgetting that fibrant replacement is needed in
    the target of Hom; thinking "Quillen equivalence" means the model
    categories are equivalent (only the homotopy theories are); assuming
    every object of Ch≥0(ℤ) is cofibrant (only the projective ones).
    Now you try: ℤ/6 ⊗ᴸ ℤ/9, and Ext¹(ℤ/8, ℤ). (H₀ = H₁ = ℤ/3; ℤ/8)

D7. (a) Compare the strict and homotopy pushouts of * ← S¹ → *, and
    the strict and homotopy pullbacks of * → S¹ ← *. Why are homotopy
    (co)limits the "correct" ones? (b) Compute the homology and π₁ of
    the homotopy cofibre Cₙ of the degree-n map S¹ → S¹, and χ(Cₙ).
    (c) Use the homotopy fibre sequence S¹ → S³ → S² of the Hopf map to
    compute π₂(S²) and π₃(S²).
    Answer: (a) pushouts: * versus S²; pullbacks: * versus ΩS¹ ≃ ℤ.
    (b) H₁ = ℤ/|n| (n ≠ 0), H₂ = 0, π₁ = ℤ/|n|, χ = 1 (C₂ = ℝP²).
    (c) π₂(S²) = π₃(S²) = ℤ.
    Teacher explains: (a) The strict pushout collapses S¹ to a point
    twice: *. But * ← S¹ → * is weakly equivalent (objectwise) to
    D² ← S¹ → D², whose pushout is S², and a functor that sends weakly
    equivalent diagrams to non-equivalent spaces does not descend to
    homotopy theory. The homotopy pushout is the left derived functor:
    make one leg a cofibration (S¹ ↪ D²) and glue: hocolim(* ← X → *) =
    ΣX, so here ΣS¹ = S². Dually, replace * → S¹ by the path fibration
    PS¹ → S¹ (or the cover ℝ → S¹); the pullback is ΩS¹, with π₀ = ℤ.
    (b) Replace S¹ → S¹ (degree n) by S¹ ↪ mapping cylinder; the
    homotopy cofibre is Cₙ = D² ∪_n S¹. Cellular chains: 0 → ℤ →n ℤ →0
    ℤ → 0 in degrees 2, 1, 0: H₂ = ker(n) = 0, H₁ = ℤ/n, H₀ = ℤ. Van
    Kampen: π₁ = ⟨a | aⁿ⟩ = ℤ/n. Euler characteristic of a homotopy
    pushout B ← A → C is χ(B) + χ(C) − χ(A); here Cₙ = hocolim(* ←
    S¹ → S¹): 1 + 0 − 0 = 1 (cells: 1 − 1 + 1 = 1). For n = 2: ℝP².
    (c) The long exact sequence of homotopy groups of a fibre sequence
    F → E → B: ... → πₖF → πₖE → πₖB → πₖ₋₁F → ... With F = S¹, E = S³,
    B = S²: πₖ(S¹) = 0 for k ≥ 2, and π₂(S³) = π₁(S³) = 0. So
    0 = π₂(S³) → π₂(S²) → π₁(S¹) = ℤ → π₁(S³) = 0 gives π₂(S²) ≅ ℤ, and
    0 = π₃(S¹) → π₃(S³) = ℤ → π₃(S²) → π₂(S¹) = 0 gives π₃(S²) ≅ ℤ,
    generated by the Hopf map. (Same argument works in HoTT.)
    Common mistakes: computing a pushout without first making a leg a
    cofibration; identifying the homotopy fibre with the strict fibre
    of a non-fibration (the strict fibre of * → S¹ over the base is a
    point); using χ(Cₙ) = 0.
    Now you try: hocolim(S¹ ← S¹ × S¹ → S¹) (the join) and its χ; the
    homotopy fibre of * → K(A, n). (S³, χ = 0 = 0 + 0 − 0; K(A, n − 1))

D8. (a) Define a stable ∞-category and show that Σ and Ω are inverse
    equivalences and that for every object W the functor [W, −] turns a
    cofibre sequence X → Y → Z into a long exact sequence infinite in
    both directions. (b) In D(ℤ) compute cofib(ℤ →n ℤ), cofib(ℤ/4 ↠
    ℤ/2), [ℤ/n, ℤ[1]] and [ℤ/4, ℤ/6[1]]; illustrate the octahedral axiom
    with ℤ →2 ℤ →3 ℤ. (c) In spectra, with π₀S = ℤ, π₁S = π₂S = ℤ/2,
    π₃S = ℤ/24, π₄S = 0, compute πₖ(S/2) for k ≤ 2 and πₖ(S/3) for k ≤ 4.
    Answer: (b) ℤ/n; ℤ/2[1]; ℤ/n; ℤ/2; cofibre sequence ℤ/2 → ℤ/6 →
    ℤ/3. (c) S/2: ℤ/2, ℤ/2, a group of order 4 (in fact ℤ/4);
    S/3: ℤ/3, 0, 0, ℤ/3, ℤ/3.
    Teacher explains: (a) C is STABLE if it has a zero object, every map
    has a fibre and a cofibre, and a square is a pullback iff it is a
    pushout. The square with corners X, 0, 0, ΣX defines ΣX as a
    pushout; being also a pullback it says X = Ω(ΣX); similarly ΣΩY = Y.
    Every cofibre sequence X → Y → Z is also a fibre sequence, and
    rotating gives Y → Z → ΣX → ΣY → ... and to the left ΩZ → X → Y.
    Mapping from W into a fibre sequence of spaces gives an exact
    sequence of π₀'s, and since ΩⁿΣⁿ = id it extends to all integers.
    hC is additive and TRIANGULATED; the octahedral axiom comes from
    pasting pushout squares.
    (b) The cone of ℤ →n ℤ (n ≠ 0) has H₀ = coker = ℤ/n and H₁ = ker =
    0: ℤ/n. For a surjection with kernel K the cone has H₁ = K, H₀ = 0:
    cofib(ℤ/4 ↠ ℤ/2) = ℤ/2[1]. [A, B[k]] = Extᵏ(A, B): Ext¹(ℤ/n, ℤ) =
    ℤ/n (from 0 → ℤ →n ℤ → ℤ/n → 0: Hom(ℤ, ℤ) →n Hom(ℤ, ℤ) has
    coker ℤ/n) and Ext¹(ℤ/4, ℤ/6) = ℤ/gcd(4, 6) = ℤ/2. Octahedral: for
    f = 2, g = 3 there is a cofibre sequence cofib f → cofib(gf) →
    cofib g, i.e. ℤ/2 → ℤ/6 → ℤ/3 (1 ↦ 3, then reduction mod 3).
    (c) From S →n S → S/n → ΣS: 0 → coker(n on πₖS) → πₖ(S/n) →
    ker(n on πₖ₋₁S) → 0. For n = 2: k = 0: coker ℤ/2, no kernel; k = 1:
    coker(2 on ℤ/2) = ℤ/2, ker(2 on ℤ) = 0 → ℤ/2; k = 2: coker(2 on
    ℤ/2) = ℤ/2 and ker(2 on ℤ/2) = ℤ/2 → order 4 (the extension is
    non-split, reflecting 2·id_{S/2} ≠ 0). For n = 3: k = 0: ℤ/3; k = 1:
    3 is invertible on ℤ/2 and injective on ℤ → 0; k = 2: 0; k = 3:
    coker(3 on ℤ/24) = ℤ/3, ker(3 on ℤ/2) = 0 → ℤ/3; k = 4: coker(3 on
    0) = 0, ker(3 on ℤ/24) = ℤ/3 → ℤ/3.
    Common mistakes: reasoning stably with spaces, where cofibre
    sequences are not fibre sequences; forgetting the kernel term (giving
    π₁(S/2) = 0 wrongly or π₂(S/2) = ℤ/2); thinking the triangulated
    homotopy category has functorial cones (it does not; the ∞-category
    does).
    Now you try: cofib(ℤ/2 ↪ ℤ/4) and [ℤ/6, ℤ/4[1]] in D(ℤ); π₃(S/2)'s
    order. (ℤ/2; ℤ/2; 4)

D9. (a) Using path induction define p⁻¹ and p·q, prove refl·p = p and
    ap_f(p·q) = ap_f(p)·ap_f(q), and compute transport along p : x = y
    in the families x ↦ (a = x), x ↦ (x = a), x ↦ (x = x). (b) By the
    encode-decode method prove (m =_ℕ n) ≃ code(m, n), where code(0, 0)
    = 1, code(0, n + 1) = code(m + 1, 0) = 0, code(m + 1, n + 1) =
    code(m, n); deduce ¬(0 = 1), injectivity of succ and that ℕ is a
    set. (c) Give closed terms of ¬¬(A + ¬A) and of (Π(x : A) Σ(y : B)
    R(x, y)) → Σ(f : A → B) Π(x : A) R(x, f x).
    Answer: (a) q ↦ q·p, q ↦ p⁻¹·q, q ↦ p⁻¹·q·p. (b) below.
    (c) λk. k(inr(λa. k(inl a))); λh. (λx. pr₁(h x), λx. pr₂(h x)).
    Teacher explains: (a) PATH INDUCTION: to define something for all
    x, y and p : x = y it suffices to give it for refl_x. So refl⁻¹ :≡
    refl, and p·q is defined by induction on q with p·refl :≡ p. Then
    p·refl = p holds by definition, while refl·p = p needs induction on
    p (for p ≡ refl both sides are refl). For ap: by induction on q,
    ap_f(p·refl) = ap_f(p) = ap_f(p)·refl = ap_f(p)·ap_f(refl). For the
    transports, induct on p: when p ≡ refl each transport is the
    identity and q·refl = refl⁻¹·q = refl⁻¹·q·refl = q, so the formulas
    hold; path induction extends them to all p.
    (b) Define r : Π(n : ℕ) code(n, n) by r(0) = ⋆, r(n + 1) = r(n).
    ENCODE(p) := transport^{code(m, −)}(p, r(m)) : code(m, n) for p :
    m = n. DECODE : code(m, n) → (m = n) by double induction: decode(0,
    0, ⋆) = refl; the mixed cases are maps out of 0; decode(m + 1, n +
    1, c) = ap_succ(decode(m, n, c)). decode(encode p) = p: by path
    induction take p = refl_m; encode(refl) = r(m) and decode(r(m)) =
    refl by induction on m. encode(decode c) = c: every code(m, n) is a
    proposition (1, 0, or a smaller code), so any two elements are
    equal. Hence (m = n) ≃ code(m, n). Consequences: (0 = 1) ≃ code(0,
    1) ≡ 0, so ¬(0 = 1); (m + 1 = n + 1) ≃ code(m, n) ≃ (m = n), so succ
    is injective; all path types of ℕ are propositions: ℕ is a SET.
    (c) Curry-Howard: a proof is a program. For ¬¬(A + ¬A) = ((A + ¬A)
    → 0) → 0 take k : (A + ¬A) → 0; then λa. k(inl a) : ¬A, and
    k(inr(λa. k(inl a))) : 0. For "choice", h(x) : Σ(y : B) R(x, y) is
    a pair, whose first projections form f and second projections
    prove R(x, f x). No choice principle is used: Σ is the STRONG
    (proof-relevant) existential; with ‖Σ‖₋₁ the statement is the
    genuine axiom of choice, which is not provable.
    Common mistakes: trying to prove p = refl for all p : x = x by
    path induction (J needs a free endpoint); confusing p⁻¹·q with q·p⁻¹
    in transports; claiming A + ¬A is provable (it is independent).
    Now you try: transport along p : x = y in x ↦ (f x = g x) for f, g
    : A → B. (q ↦ ap_f(p)⁻¹·q·ap_g(p))

D10. (a) Prove: isContr A ⇒ isProp A ⇒ isSet A, and HEDBERG's theorem
    (decidable equality ⇒ set). (b) Give the least n for which each type
    is an n-type: 1, 0, Bool, ℕ, Σ(x : A) (a = x), S¹, hProp, hSet,
    Bool → ℕ, Ω(S¹, base). (c) Compute ‖0‖₋₁, ‖Bool‖₋₁, ‖S¹‖₀, ‖S¹‖₁,
    ‖S²‖₁, π₁(S¹), π₃(S²), and explain why Σ(n : ℕ) (n + n = 4) is a
    proposition while Σ(n : ℕ) isEven(n) is not.
    Answer: (a) below. (b) −2, −1, 0, 0, −2, 1, 0, 1, 0, 0. (c) 0, 1, 1,
    S¹, 1, ℤ, ℤ; the first has exactly one element (n = 2), the second
    has infinitely many.
    Teacher explains: (a) If (a, h) : isContr A then x = y via
    h(x)⁻¹·h(y). Now let A be a proposition, fix a and let g(x) : a = x
    be g(x) := f(a, x) with f : isProp A. For p : x = y, dependent ap
    gives transport(p, g x) = g y, i.e. g(x)·p = g(y), so p = g(x)⁻¹·
    g(y), which does not depend on p: any two p, q : x = y are equal.
    HEDBERG: a decision d(x, y) : (x = y) + ¬(x = y) gives ρ : (x = y)
    → (x = y): ρ(p) := r if d(x, y) = inl r, and the case inr n is
    impossible given p. So ρ is CONSTANT: ρ(p) = ρ(q). By path induction
    on p (y free), p = ρ(refl_x)⁻¹·ρ(p); hence p = ρ(refl)⁻¹·ρ(p) =
    ρ(refl)⁻¹·ρ(q) = q. So ℕ, ℤ, Bool, Fin n are sets.
    (b) 1 is contractible (−2); 0 is a proposition but not contractible
    (−1); Bool is a set with true ≠ false (0); ℕ is a set (0); the
    based path space Σ(x : A)(a = x) is contractible (−2) by path
    induction, centre (a, refl); S¹ is a 1-type and not a set because
    Ω(S¹) ≃ ℤ (1); hProp is a SET (paths between propositions are
    logical equivalences, themselves propositions) but not a
    proposition (0); hSet is a 1-type (its paths are bijections, which
    form sets) and not a set (1); Bool → ℕ is a set since Π preserves
    levels (0); Ω(S¹) ≃ ℤ (0).
    (c) ‖0‖₋₁ = 0, ‖Bool‖₋₁ = 1 (inhabited propositions are
    contractible), ‖S¹‖₀ = 1 since S¹ is connected, ‖S¹‖₁ = S¹ since S¹
    is already a 1-type, ‖S²‖₁ = 1 as S² is 1-connected. π₁(S¹) =
    ‖Ω S¹‖₀ = ℤ, π₃(S²) = ℤ (Hopf fibration). n + n = 4 has the single
    solution 2 and ℕ is a set, so the Σ-type is contractible, a fortiori
    a proposition; isEven has solutions 0, 2, 4, ... so we must truncate
    to get the proposition "∃n even".
    Common mistakes: "every set is a proposition" (reverse inclusion);
    thinking truncation ‖−‖₀ keeps the loops of S¹ (it kills them);
    using Σ where a mere existence ‖Σ‖₋₁ is intended.
    Now you try: the levels of Bool × S¹, Σ(X : U) ‖X = Bool‖₋₁, and
    ‖S¹ + S¹‖₀. (1; 1, it is BΣ₂; Bool)

D11. (a) State the univalence axiom and prove that (Bool =_U Bool) ≃
    Bool; deduce that U is not a set and that univalence contradicts
    UIP. (b) With e = not : Bool ≃ Bool, compute transport along ua(e)
    in P(X) = X × X at (true, false) and in P(X) = X → X at the
    constant function c_true and at id. (c) How many elements has
    (Fin n = Fin n)? Describe the type BΣₙ := Σ(X : U) ‖X = Fin n‖₋₁ and
    compute its π₀ and π₁.
    Answer: (a) below. (b) (false, true); c_false; id. (c) n!; the type
    of n-element sets, connected, π₁ = Σₙ, a 1-type.
    Teacher explains: (a) For A, B : U define idtoeqv : (A = B) → (A ≃
    B) by path induction (refl ↦ id). UNIVALENCE: idtoeqv is an
    equivalence; its inverse is ua, and transport^{X ↦ X}(ua e, a) =
    e(a). Hence (Bool = Bool) ≃ (Bool ≃ Bool). An equivalence of Bool
    is determined by f(true) (f(false) must be the other value, since f
    is injective and surjective, and equality on Bool is decidable), so
    (Bool ≃ Bool) ≃ Bool: {id, not}. Thus ua(not) ≠ refl, because
    idtoeqv(refl) = id ≠ not (they differ at true). So Bool = Bool has
    two distinct elements: U is not a set, and UIP (all paths in all
    types equal) is refuted.
    (b) Transport along ua(e) in a family built from X acts by e
    "everywhere X occurs": for X × X, (a, b) ↦ (e a, e b), so (true,
    false) ↦ (false, true). For X → X, f ↦ e∘f∘e⁻¹ (transport in a
    function type conjugates). c_true ↦ not∘c_true∘not = c_false; id ↦
    not∘id∘not = id. (By path induction on the path ua(e), these
    formulas hold since they hold for refl.)
    (c) (Fin n = Fin n) ≃ (Fin n ≃ Fin n) = the permutations of an
    n-element set: n! elements. BΣₙ collects the types merely equal to
    Fin n. Any two points (X, _), (Y, _) are merely equal, so ‖BΣₙ‖₀ is
    contractible: π₀ = 1. Its loop space at (Fin n, _) is (since the
    second component is a proposition) (Fin n = Fin n) ≃ Σₙ as a set
    with composition, so π₁ = Σₙ and higher homotopy vanishes: BΣₙ =
    K(Σₙ, 1), the groupoid of D5 (c) in type-theoretic form.
    Common mistakes: believing univalence identifies isomorphic
    types "definitionally" (it gives paths, and transport computes
    only propositionally in book HoTT); forgetting to conjugate in the
    function type; counting (Fin 3 = Fin 3) as 3 instead of 6.
    Now you try: transport along ua(e), e the cycle (0 1 2) of Fin 3,
    in P(X) = X × X at (0, 0); #(Fin 4 = Fin 4). ((1, 1); 24)

D12. (a) Prove Ω(S¹, base) ≃ ℤ by encode-decode, using code : S¹ → U
    with code(base) = ℤ and ap_code(loop) = ua(succ). (b) Compute
    encode(loop²·loop⁻⁵), transport^code(loop⁻¹·loop³, 5), and ap_f of
    loop³ where f : S¹ → S¹ is defined by f(base) = base, ap_f(loop) =
    loop². Deduce loop ≠ refl and πₖ(S¹) = 0 for k ≥ 2. (c) Show ΣBool
    ≃ S¹; identify Σ1, Σ0 and Σ(Fin 3).
    Answer: (a) below. (b) −3; 7; loop⁶. (c) ΣBool ≃ S¹; Σ1 ≃ 1,
    Σ0 ≃ Bool, Σ(Fin 3) ≃ S¹ ∨ S¹.
    Teacher explains: (a) ENCODE : Π(x : S¹) (base = x) → code(x),
    encode(p) := transport^code(p, 0). Transport in code along p equals
    idtoeqv(ap_code p); along loop it is succ, along loop⁻¹ pred.
    DECODE : Π(x : S¹) code(x) → (base = x) by circle induction:
    decode(base) := λn. loopⁿ, and we must check that transporting this
    function along loop (in x ↦ code(x) → (base = x)) returns it:
    transport(loop, g)(n) = g(n − 1)·loop = loop^{n−1}·loop = loopⁿ.
    decode(encode p) = p: by based path induction it suffices that
    decode(encode refl) = decode(0) = loop⁰ = refl. encode(decode n) = n:
    induct on n ∈ ℤ: encode(loopⁿ) = transport^code(loopⁿ, 0) = succⁿ(0)
    = n (pred for n < 0). So (base = base) ≃ ℤ; in fact (base = x) ≃
    code(x) for all x, so the path types of S¹ are sets: S¹ is a 1-type.
    (b) encode(loop²·loop⁻⁵) = 2 − 5 = −3 (transport along a composite
    is the composite of transports). transport^code(loop⁻¹·loop³, 5) =
    5 − 1 + 3 = 7. ap_f(loop³) = ap_f(loop)³ = loop⁶ (ap preserves
    composition): f has degree 2. Since encode(loop) = 1 ≠ 0 =
    encode(refl), loop ≠ refl, so S¹ is not a set. Ω²(S¹) = Ω(ℤ, 0) is
    contractible because ℤ is a set; hence πₖ(S¹) = 0 for k ≥ 2.
    (c) ΣBool has N, S, merid(false), merid(true) : N = S. Map to S¹ by
    N, S ↦ base, merid(false) ↦ loop, merid(true) ↦ refl; back by base ↦
    N, loop ↦ merid(false)·merid(true)⁻¹. Both composites are homotopic
    to the identity by (suspension/circle) induction. Σ1 has one
    meridian from N to S: contractible (an interval). Σ0 has no
    meridians: just N and S, i.e. Bool (= S⁰). Σ(Fin 3) has three
    meridians; after contracting one of them the other two become
    independent loops: S¹ ∨ S¹, with π₁ = F₂ (free group on 2).
    Common mistakes: defining code(loop) as a path in ℤ (it must be a
    path in U: an equivalence via ua); forgetting to check decode
    respects loop; reading ΣBool as having one meridian.
    Now you try: winding number of loop⁴·loop⁻¹·loop⁻⁶; degree of g∘f if
    g has degree −3 and f degree 2. (−3; −6)

M9. Martin-Löf type theory, Curry-Howard, identity types.
    Count the elements (closed terms up to equality):
    a) Bool → Bool            b) Fin 3 → Fin 2       c) Σ(n : Fin 4) Fin n
    d) Π(n : Fin 3) Fin(n + 1)                       e) Fin 2 × Fin 3 → Fin 2
    f) (Fin 2 → Fin 2) → Fin 2                       g) Fin 3 + Fin 4
    h) Π(b : Bool) (if b then Fin 3 else Fin 5)
    i) 0 → A, and A → 0 for inhabited A
    Provable in MLTT (without axioms)? (P, Q propositions-as-types)
    j) ¬¬(P + ¬P)       k) P + ¬P           l) ¬¬¬P → ¬P
    m) ¬¬P → P          n) ((P → Q) → P) → P
    o) ¬(P + Q) → ¬P × ¬Q                  p) ¬(P × Q) → ¬P + ¬Q
    q) (P → Q) → (¬Q → ¬P)                 r) (¬Q → ¬P) → (P → Q)
    Path algebra (p : x = y, q : y = z):
    s) (p·q)⁻¹        t) ap_{g∘f}(p)      u) ap of a constant map λx. b
    v) transport^{x ↦ (x = x)}(p, r) for r : x = x
    w) transport^{x ↦ B(x) → C(x)}(p, h)
    Answers:
      a) 4        b) 8        c) 0 + 1 + 2 + 3 = 6     d) 1·2·3 = 6
      e) 2⁶ = 64  f) 2⁴ = 16  g) 7        h) 3·5 = 15
      i) exactly 1 (the empty function); none
      j) yes      k) no       l) yes      m) no       n) no (Peirce)
      o) yes      p) no       q) yes      r) no
      s) q⁻¹·p⁻¹  t) ap_g(ap_f(p))        u) refl_b
      v) p⁻¹·r·p  w) transport^C(p, −) ∘ h ∘ transport^B(p⁻¹, −)

M10. h-levels, univalence and truncations.
    Least n such that the type is an n-type:
    a) Fin 0     b) Fin 1     c) Fin 5     d) ℤ       e) S¹
    f) Ω(S¹)     g) S¹ × S¹   h) hProp     i) hSet    j) U_{≤1}
    k) Bool ≃ Bool          l) isContr A (any A)
    m) Σ(x : A) (x = a)     n) BΣ₃ = Σ(X : U) ‖X = Fin 3‖₋₁
    Compute:
    o) #(Fin 3 = Fin 3), #(Fin 2 = Fin 3), #(Fin 5 = Fin 5)
    p) ‖Fin 7‖₋₁, ‖Fin 0‖₋₁, ‖S⁰‖₀, ‖S¹ ∨ S¹‖₀, ‖S¹‖₁
    q) π₁(S²), π₂(S²), π₃(S²), π₄(S³), π₅(S⁵)
    r) ‖S³‖₂ and ‖S³‖₃ (contractible or not?)
    s) π₀ and π₁ of BΣ₃
    t) transport along ua(not) in X ↦ (X → Bool) at id and at c_true
    u) transport along ua(e), e(n) = n + 1 on ℤ, in X ↦ X × X at (2, 5)
    Answers:
      a) −1   b) −2   c) 0    d) 0    e) 1
      f) 0    g) 1    h) 0    i) 1    j) 2
      k) 0 (a 2-element set)          l) −1
      m) −2                           n) 1
      o) 6; 0 (empty); 120
      p) 1; 0; Bool; 1; S¹
      q) 0; ℤ; ℤ; ℤ/2; ℤ
      r) contractible (S³ is 2-connected); not (π₃ = ℤ survives)
      s) π₀ = 1 (connected); π₁ = Σ₃
      t) f ↦ f∘not⁻¹: id ↦ not; c_true ↦ c_true
      u) (3, 6)

M11. Higher inductive types and encode-decode.
    a) Winding numbers: loop³, loop·loop⁻¹, loop⁻²·loop⁵·loop⁻⁴,
       (loop²)⁻¹·loop⁷
    b) transport^code(loop⁴, −2), transport^code(loop⁻³·loop, 10)
    c) f : S¹ → S¹ with ap_f(loop) = loop⁻¹: ap_f(loop⁵); degree of
       g∘f when deg f = 3, deg g = −2
    d) Ω(T², base) for the torus T²
    e) In Ω(S¹ ∨ S¹) with loops a, b: is a·b = b·a?
    f) Σ(Fin 4) and its π₁
    g) ΣS¹, ΣS², Σ(S¹ ∨ S¹)
    h) (inl 3 = inl 3), (inl 2 = inl 5), (inl 0 = inr true), (inr true
       = inr true) in ℕ + Bool
    i) code(3, 3) and code(4, 2) for ℕ
    j) (true =_Bool false), via P(true) = 1, P(false) = 0
    k) π₁ of the HIT with base, loop and a 2-cell loopⁿ = refl; n = 2
    l) π₁ and H₁ of the Klein bottle ⟨a, b | a·b·a·b⁻¹ = 1⟩
    m) the interval HIT (0, 1, seg : 0 = 1)
    n) ‖S¹‖₋₁, ‖S¹‖₀
    o) Ω(ΣBool)
    p) the homotopy fibre of the degree-2 map S¹ → S¹ as a type
    Answers:
      a) 3; 0; −1; 5                  b) 2; 8
      c) loop⁻⁵; −6                   d) ℤ × ℤ
      e) no (free group F₂)           f) S¹ ∨ S¹ ∨ S¹; F₃
      g) S², S³, S² ∨ S²
      h) 1 (contractible); 0; 0; 1    i) 1; code(2, 0) = 0
      j) 0 (empty): transport would map ⋆ : 1 into 0
      k) ℤ/n; ℤ/2 (this is ℝP²)
      l) ⟨a, b | abab⁻¹⟩, infinite non-abelian; H₁ = ℤ ⊕ ℤ/2
      m) contractible                 n) 1; 1
      o) ℤ                            p) Bool (2 points)

M12. True (T) or false (F)? Give a counterexample for F.
     a) Every quasi-category is a Kan complex.
     b) Every Kan complex is a quasi-category.
     c) N(C) is a quasi-category for every small category C.
     d) N(C) is a Kan complex for every small category C.
     e) Composition of 1-cells in a bicategory is strictly associative.
     f) Every bicategory is biequivalent to a strict 2-category.
     g) Strict pushouts of spaces are invariant under objectwise
        weak equivalence of diagrams.
     h) A left Quillen functor preserves all weak equivalences.
     i) |−| ⊣ Sing is a Quillen equivalence between sSet (Kan-Quillen)
        and Top.
     j) id : sSet_Joyal → sSet_Kan-Quillen is a Quillen equivalence.
     k) In a stable ∞-category every fibre sequence is a cofibre
        sequence.
     l) The homotopy category of a stable ∞-category is abelian.
     m) The ∞-category of pointed spaces is stable.
     n) UIP is provable in Martin-Löf type theory.
     o) Every proposition is a set.
     p) Every set is a proposition.
     q) Univalence implies function extensionality.
     r) Assuming univalence, the universe U is a set.
     s) In HoTT, loop = refl in S¹.
     t) From ‖A‖₋₁ one can always construct an element of A, for all A.
     u) A type with decidable equality is a set.
     v) The Σ-form of the "axiom of choice" is provable in MLTT.
     w) hocolim(X ← X × Y → Y) ≃ X × Y.
     x) Ω(S¹) ≃ ℤ implies πₖ(S¹) = 0 for all k ≥ 2.
     Answers:
       a) F: Δ[1] (the horn Λ⁰[2] → Δ[1] of D3)
       b) T         c) T
       d) F: N([1]) = Δ[1] (C must be a groupoid)
       e) F: spans, (S ×_B T) ×_C U ≠ S ×_B (T ×_C U) as sets
       f) T (coherence)
       g) F: * ← S¹ → * versus D² ← S¹ → D² (* versus S²)
       h) F: − ⊗ ℤ/2 on Ch≥0(ℤ) and the quasi-iso (ℤ →4 ℤ) → ℤ/4
       i) T         j) F: Δ[1] → Δ[0] is a Kan-Quillen but not a Joyal
                       equivalence
       k) T
       l) F: D(ℤ); the triangle ℤ →2 ℤ → ℤ/2 → ℤ[1] does not split,
          while abelian triangulated categories are semisimple
       m) F: Σ is not an equivalence there: ΩΣS⁰ = ΩS¹ ≃ ℤ ≄ S⁰
       n) F: the groupoid model; with univalence Bool = Bool has two
          distinct elements
       o) T         p) F: Bool
       q) T         r) F: (Bool = Bool) ≃ Bool
       s) F: encode(loop) = 1 ≠ 0 = encode(refl)
       t) F: global choice Π_A (‖A‖₋₁ → A) contradicts univalence
          (apply it to Σ(X : U) ‖X = Bool‖₋₁ and transport along
          ua(not))
       u) T (Hedberg)
       v) T (it is the strong Σ; the ‖·‖₋₁ form is not provable)
       w) F: it is the join X * Y; S⁰ * S⁰ = S¹
       x) T (Ω²(S¹) = Ω(ℤ) is contractible)

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: draw everything. A 2-cell is a "surface" between arrows,
  and Eckmann-Hilton is the picture of two discs sliding around each
  other; a span is a matrix; a simplex is a sequence a₀ ≤ ... ≤ aₖ, so
  every counting question becomes binomial coefficients; a horn is "a
  simplex with the lid and one wall missing", and the only question is
  whether the missing wall is inner (composition) or outer (inverses).
  Keep a two-column table "1-category / ∞-category": composite ↔
  contractible space of fillers, hom-set ↔ mapping space, limit ↔
  homotopy limit, abelian category ↔ stable ∞-category, and always test
  a construction on * ← S¹ → * before trusting it. In type theory,
  insist that p : x = y is a path, not a proof of a boolean fact:
  compute transports by path induction (reduce to refl), draw the
  circle's universal cover as a helix over S¹ when doing encode-decode,
  and count elements of finite types to make h-levels and univalence
  concrete ((Fin n = Fin n) has n! elements). Harder: prove that N(C)
  is exactly the simplicial sets with unique inner fillers; prove
  Joyal's theorem that a quasi-category with groupoidal hC is Kan;
  construct the Kan-Quillen model structure via Kan's Ex^∞; prove Ken
  Brown's lemma and the Quillen equivalence criterion; show that the
  homotopy category of a stable ∞-category is triangulated, including
  the octahedral axiom; in HoTT, derive function extensionality from
  univalence, prove Hedberg's theorem and the Freudenthal suspension
  theorem, construct the Hopf fibration S¹ → S³ → S² as a family over
  S² using univalence and the H-space structure of S¹, and deduce
  π₂(S²) = ℤ; finally, read Brunerie's proof that π₄(S³) = ℤ/2 and
  explain why the resulting "Brunerie number" is ±2.
